#10401. Open source project renders your Windows 11 desktop using the NPU rather than the GPU

Developer is aiming for 30 Hz performance on Intel Panther Lake NPUs but the current version of this research driver is only achieving 2 to 3 Hz.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A developer group by the name of labscommunity has released a prototype driver for Windows 11 that renders the desktop using an NPU instead of the system’s (i)GPU. The new driver is called npu-driver, and the open-source (Apache 2.0) code is available on GitHub. It has been tested on Intel Panther Lake systems, and while it isn’t hitting its 30 Hz performance target yet, it is clever enough to fall back to GPU on request or on any fault.

The reasoning behind the development of this driver may strike a chord with those observing the 2020s processor landscape. “Every new laptop ships an NPU that sits idle while the GPU composes the desktop, runs the browser and, increasingly, runs local models,” notes the npu-driver readme file. “The question this project asks is narrow and testable: can the NPU take over desktop composition, end-to-end, without the applications or the compositor knowing?”

Thankfully, the answer to the question about whether the NPU can render the Windows 11 desktop - and apps like Notepad and Edge windows - is a yes. The npu-driver functions as intended and as demonstrated. Moreover, it seamlessly switches back to GPU rendering if/when it needs to.

The developer admits that this driver is strictly “a research prototype, not a product” at this stage, and the main issue currently is performance. The GitHub readme notes that the target 30 Hz performance isn’t very close right now, with actual screen refresh speeds of around 2 to 3 Hz. npu-driver is being developed on a Panther Lake laptop, and all these Intel processors feature NPU 5 architecture silicon with performance spanning 45-50 TOPS.

A Reddit post that appears to be penned by one of the labscommunity crew says that this project might render Windows 11 visuals rather slowly, but it can “bring 3D GPU usage down from 3.4% to 0.6%,” thus freeing up the GPU for other tasks, like inference.

Having a(nother) useful task for the system NPU to do is a welcome development, even if it seems of rather niche appeal for now. There are no signs consumer processors will abandon NPU integration, and these accelerators will likely remain alongside other now-essentials like hardware for video (de)coding, DSPs, and so on, even if the Copilot+ PC branding exercise has been abandoned by Microsoft and its partners.

#10400. ‘Cyclon’ uses an old Google Pixel to let AI do literally everything for you – who wants this?

As the AI era continues to find its footing in the consumer market, “Cyclon” is a new smartphone/software experience that, to start, is reusing old Google Pixel phones with a software experience that literally uses AI to do every little thing. And my biggest question is just… who actually wants this?

The pitch of Cyclon, the founders explain in a video on Twitter/X, is to “[build] an AI phone to replace the iPhone.” And the central experience seems to be… talking to a chatbot. Examples in the video include telling the AI to “handle my notifications” and letting it monitor a WhatsApp message and reply to a friend asking for photos by grabbing those photos from your gallery without you as the user needing to touch the device.

Other examples on the company’s website have mixed utility at a glance. “Order my usual on DoorDash” and calling a courier to let them into a building certainly could be useful, but “tell the group chat I’m running ten minutes late” only to have the AI send the message on WhatsApp “in your words” not only seems like a waste of time, but a pointless disconnect from reality (as is all too common with these AI ventures).

The one thing that caught my attention here is building apps on demand and being able to share them. The example given is a language learning app tailored to a specific vacation. It’s a really cool idea, though one I would imagine will have some rocky edges. Google is on a similar track with Gemini Intelligence’s “Create my widget” feature (on that note, when is that releasing on phones Google?), and I’d imagine Gemini will be able to do the same thing in time.

The actual hardware side of things is also… interesting.

Cyclon says that it has a working prototype of the idea running on Google Pixel 8a units, and it’ll ship “first-batch” backers one of those units with their $199 pre-order of a later “Cyclon Phone” that itself is expected to cost $699 – so you’re getting two phones for $699. That alone seems a little questionable in the midst of RAMageddon, but I digress.

That “final” phone hardware… sure is something.

There are some good ideas here, some big promises, but more than anything I just want to know who actually wants to use a phone like this.

I’m all for the idea of AI making my life a little easier (despite finding hardly any ways it can do that so far), but sending everything through a chatbot that does everything in the background and even generates “your words” for responses to my friends and family just sounds awful. And the state of social media isn’t exactly helping matters here. Twitter/X is 90% AI replies for engagement for this sort of thing, and Cyclon is paying influencers to boost that engagement too, so genuine reactions just seem impossible to find.

Sound off in the comments below if you’re an actual human with an opinion on this one, because I’m dying to know what real people think of this.

#10397. Cloudflare engineers optimize DNS build services for twice the speed and 85% lower RAM usage

Another massive improvement makes a hat trick for Cloudflare's engineering team

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Cloudflare is up to its optimization shenanigans again. Not too long after finding 100 TB of memory savings between the DNS cache's couch cushions, and another 100 TB of saved RAM by improving its task-server routing, the company's engineers are back with the hat trick. This time, they managed to halve DNS zone rebuild times and cut that procedure's memory usage by 85% across its network.

A "DNS zone" is the collection of records, settings, and metadata for a given domain like tomshardware.com. The records are the main part, containing IP addresses, hostnames, or text lines including website and mail server IPs, subdomains, domain redirections, and spam-control or certificate info. If you're a Cloudflare customer, when you update a record pointing your website to a new IP, or change one of the domain's settings, the service pushes this information onto its many thousands of DNS servers.

This sounds easy enough until you realize that Cloudflare's DNS fleet is huge, and that the company now gets 800 zone changes per second, up from 250 four years ago. All those changes add up to enormous amounts of RAM and network traffic. Consistency across the entire fleet must be guaranteed, too, as you can't have one DNS server saying your website is at one place, while another server says it's elsewhere — except during actual transitions, of course. Furthermore, some domains hosted at Cloudflare have over 20 million records, making them exceedingly difficult to build in memory.

The easy solution, then, is to only update the data that's needed, and that's precisely what the company did for domain records back in 2022. Updating records is simple: you just write the new value in and propagate it. Altering settings, though, is way trickier. For example, toggling Universal SSL for a domain can trigger a cascade of record updates.

Some metadata keys are shared across multiple zones, necessitating an update across all of them. Change events can come in from many Cloudflare services, too, and often didn't have a reason attached, so it was hard to know if other subsequent changes were necessary. Consequentially, setting updates required a full build for safety.

The engineers then carefully laid out a plan to optimize this process. First, they tweaked their internal software so that zone change events include a reason and additional information, so that said event can be properly classified. With that info in hand, specific setting changes can use smaller and shorter builds among given types.

For example, apex and metadata changes (like UniversalSSL) comprise one. Likewise, changing a domain's nameserver or wildcards also triggers a smaller procedure. Full builds are only used in cases where there is no partial build mechanism. Finally, domains with a large number of records are now updated piecemeal. Cloudflare saw a 85% drop in RAM usage for Zone Builder servers, from 50-79 GB to down to close to 7 GB, mostly due to these changes alone.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The second optimization pass was combining two formerly separate planning and implementation schedulers into just the one, and improving how it operates. The new scheduler detects if several changes apply to the same zone and combines them. Cloudflare uses Apache Kafka for event messaging, and the team changed how the scheduler acts when a message triggers a slow build.

Since Kafka only lets messages advance in sequence, the scheduler waited for that job to finish and didn't process requests that arrived in the meantime. Now, it'll acknowledge and run incoming smaller jobs arriving in the meantime. Before, there was always a steady long queue of requests waiting to be processed, as requests piled up and were completed in bursts.

Now, they're fulfilled very shortly after being issued, and in a consistent cadence. This significantly improved concurrency capacity, so the team chose to tighten the queue processing intervals. With all the improvements, the median time for a build dropped from 7.7 seconds to 3.3 seconds, too.

#10395. Google will disable Search for students unless schools accept AI features in ‘Guided Learning’ push

Google is today launching new AI settings for schools, but with the big catch that Search is fully disabled if AI features are turned off entirely.

In a blog post today, Google says it is “focused on building technology responsibly to help students thrive” and, as such, will tailor its AI efforts to help students. “Guided Learning” in Gemini, Search, and Classroom will include helping students “work through problems without skipping straight to answers.”

…if a student pastes a complex physics problem or asks for help writing a book report in Search, they’ll see prompts to help them think critically and work through the concepts, without going directly to the answer.

That’s launching in a pilot program this month in select US schools, with Guided Learning in Google Classroom launching globally today. Google describes this as a “1-1 study tool, anchored in class material to help [students] stay on task” while also giving the teacher insights into how their class is handling the material.

Beyond this new Guided Learning push, Google also says that it will now turn off “Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search” for K-12 student accounts by default “until school administrators explicitly turn them on.”

As The New York Times points out, having those features fully off has a big side effect: it cuts students off from Google Search entirely.

Google says that this is because Search and AI “are deeply integrated” and confirms that students in schools with AI features turned off (which, to reiterate, is now the default setting) “will not have access to search.”

As a reminder, Google Search’s AI features can largely be turned off using a simple modifier.

Google’s post says that the “goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit.” Yet, speaking to The New York Times, Google further explained that two options will be available to schools in “early 2027,” one that has AI Overviews and other AI features turned on, the other with Guided Learning in their place, leaving schools with no choice other than to accept AI features or give up access to Google Search for their students entirely.

NYT points out repeatedly that Google’s prior AI pushes in schools have been met with fierce opposition from educators and parents alike.

Chinese technology giants ByteDance, Alibaba and Tencent, among others, are set to receive only a limited number of Nvidia chips that they've ordered. Bloomberg is reporting that, according to unnamed sources, only "some tens of thousands, or roughly 1%" of RTX Pro 5500 workstation GPUs will be delivered over the next few months.

Demand for the RTX Pro 5500 has far outstripped Nvidia's planned production. According to earlier reports, Chinese firms were suggested to have spent $35 billion on orders for over 3 million of the GPUs, far beyond Nvidia's ability to produce them. This new report suggests that amount has risen to $70 billion, at a cost of $11,000 per unit.

Chinese demand for the RTX Pro 5500 is based on its performance and its unique status for shipping. The RTX Pro 5500 has the same CUDA cores as its flagship gaming GPU, the RTX 5090, but with a boosted 84GB GDDR7 memory count, so it's a good option for AI.

Unlike the RTX 5090, or other AI-focused workstation GPUs like the H200 and B300, there are no export limits, meaning it can be shipped to China without restrictions. China is in an arms race with the U.S. to produce better AI models, with GPUs like the RTX Pro 5500 being core to its success. Nvidia has reportedly halted RTX 5090 production to pivot towards its AI-focused enterprise base.

The report suggests that Chinese developers expect a first batch of several thousand RTX Pro 5500 GPUs to be delivered by the end of 2026. In total, 700,000 RTX Pro 5500s are expected to be delivered by the end of 2027 as Nvidia increases its production, although this could change.

Tom's Hardware has approached Nvidia for comment. In a statement to Bloomberg, an Nvidia spokesman declined to comment on shipment numbers, but did criticize U.S. export controls, stating that "outdated U.S. export controls, which cover gaming products released nearly half a decade ago" continue to restrict it, along with "China's own limits on U.S. imports."

#10392. A 10-Point Checklist for Legacy Application Modernization

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Legacy applications often support critical business operations, but keeping them running can become increasingly difficult. Outdated infrastructure, growing technical debt, security vulnerabilities, and limited integration capabilities can make these systems expensive to maintain and harder to adapt to changing business needs.

Modernizing them involves more than upgrading technology. You need to understand what the existing application does, identify which components need improvement, and manage the transition without disrupting essential operations.

This 10-point legacy application modernization checklist walks you through the process, from assessing your existing systems and selecting a modernization strategy to migrating data, testing applications, and measuring results.

Legacy application modernization is the process of updating existing software applications, their architecture, infrastructure, or underlying technologies to meet current business and technical requirements.

Depending on the application's condition, modernization may involve moving it to a new platform, improving its code, redesigning its architecture, or replacing it with a new solution.

The goal is to improve performance, security, scalability, maintainability, and the application's ability to support future business requirements.

Modernization isn't necessarily the same as cloud migration. Moving an application from on-premises infrastructure to the cloud changes where it runs, but it may leave its underlying architecture and technical limitations unchanged.

An organization can modernize an application without moving it to the cloud or combine both approaches as part of a larger transformation.

Use this checklist to track the ten essential steps involved in modernizing legacy applications.

1. Define modernization goals: Identify business problems and establish measurable success criteria.

2. Assess existing applications: Review infrastructure, technical debt, code quality, and performance limitations.

3. Map application dependencies: Document critical workflows, integrations, and business rules.

4. Choose a modernization strategy: Select an approach based on business value, cost, complexity, and risk.

5. Develop a roadmap and budget: Prioritize activities, estimate costs, assign responsibilities, and establish milestones.

6. Plan architecture and security: Design the target environment and address security, compliance, and availability requirements.

7. Prepare data migration: Plan data transfer, integration changes, and validation procedures.

8. Modernize incrementally: Validate the approach with a pilot and implement changes in manageable phases.

9. Test and deploy: Verify functionality, performance, security, and data integrity before production rollout.

10. Monitor and optimize: Measure results, support users, and continuously improve the modernized application.

Let's examine what each step involves and how to determine whether you're ready to proceed.

Before selecting technologies or planning development work, identify what the modernization initiative needs to achieve.

Clear business objectives help prioritize investments, guide technical decisions, and establish whether the project delivers meaningful improvements.

Start by examining the problems affecting your application's performance, reliability, or business value.

For example, a legacy order management system might still process transactions reliably but require extensive manual work whenever the business adds a new sales channel.

In this situation, improving integration capabilities may be more valuable than replacing the entire application.

An application's age alone doesn't justify a major technology investment. Whether legacy modernization makes sense for your business depends on the problems the existing system creates, the cost of maintaining it, and whether smaller improvements could resolve those issues.

Once you've identified the problems, translate them into measurable objectives.

If slow performance is the primary concern, establish a target for response times or transaction processing speed. If maintenance expenses are rising, determine what reduction in operating costs would justify the investment.

Relevant key performance indicators (KPIs) may include:

These baseline measurements will help determine whether the changes deliver the expected results.

Completion check: You have a documented business case identifying the problems to address, expected outcomes, measurable KPIs, and stakeholders responsible for approving success.

A legacy application assessment provides a clear picture of your current technology environment.

Without this assessment, teams may underestimate project complexity, overlook critical components, or choose a modernization approach that doesn't address the application's actual limitations.

Begin by creating an inventory of your applications and their supporting technologies.

Document each application's purpose, business owner, primary users, operating environment, programming languages, frameworks, databases, and infrastructure requirements.

Also identify software versions, third-party dependencies, licensing restrictions, and components approaching or past their end-of-support dates.

If your organization operates several legacy applications, use a consistent assessment framework. This makes it easier to compare their business importance, technical condition, and modernization urgency.

Review the application's codebase to identify issues that make maintenance and development difficult.

Look for tightly coupled components, duplicated logic, outdated libraries, insufficient automated tests, and undocumented configurations.

Pay particular attention to areas where small modifications regularly cause unexpected problems elsewhere in the system.

For example, if payment processing, inventory management, and customer records share tightly connected code, modifying one function could require extensive testing across the entire application.

Your infrastructure assessment should also cover hardware limitations, database compatibility, backup capabilities, operating systems, and unsupported dependencies.

Analyze how the application performs during normal operations and periods of increased demand.

Review response times, database queries, resource utilization, transaction volumes, and recurring failures.

Existing monitoring data and incident records can reveal problems that aren't immediately visible during routine use.

Determine whether performance limitations originate from inefficient application code, infrastructure capacity, database design, or external dependencies.

This distinction matters because adding computing resources won't necessarily resolve problems caused by poor application logic.

Completion check: You have an application inventory and technical assessment report documenting major components, technical debt, operational risks, and performance limitations.

Legacy applications rarely operate independently. They exchange data with other systems, support interconnected business processes, and often contain business rules that aren't fully documented.

Understanding these dependencies is essential because changing one component can affect several other applications or workflows.

Document the processes that rely on the application, including the steps users follow, the data they need, and the expected outcomes.

Work with business users, application owners, operations teams, and developers familiar with the system.

Identify processes that cannot tolerate significant interruption, such as payment processing, order fulfillment, customer authentication, or financial reporting.

Don't overlook activities that happen less frequently. Month-end financial reports, annual calculations, and scheduled data exchanges may depend on functionality that isn't exercised during everyday operations.

Map how the application communicates with databases, APIs, third-party services, and other internal systems.

Include scheduled jobs, file transfers, message queues, authentication services, and reporting tools.

Some legacy applications contain years of accumulated business logic.

Rules for pricing, approvals, calculations, or customer eligibility may exist only in source code, database procedures, or undocumented workflows.

Identify these rules before modifying the underlying implementation.

Where possible, create automated tests that capture existing expected behavior. These tests can help verify that the modernized application preserves required functionality.

Completion check: Critical workflows, system dependencies, integrations, and business rules are documented and reviewed by the relevant business and technical owners.

The appropriate modernization strategy depends on the application's technical condition, business importance, budget, and future requirements.

Not every application needs to be rebuilt. Some systems require targeted improvements, while others may be better replaced or retired.

Common legacy application modernization strategies include:

Different applications within the same organization may require different approaches.

Evaluate each option against your technical constraints and business requirements.

Consider implementation effort, expected benefits, ongoing maintenance costs, required expertise, and potential disruption.

For example, replatforming may be suitable for an application that operates reliably but depends on aging infrastructure. Rearchitecting may be necessary when tightly coupled components prevent independent updates or scaling.

A complete rebuild requires particular caution. Recreating undocumented functionality can be expensive, and important business rules may be lost if the existing system hasn't been thoroughly assessed.

The right strategy should solve the identified problems without introducing unnecessary cost or complexity.

Completion check: Each application has a selected modernization strategy, documented rationale, estimated effort, and an approved decision on whether to modernize, retain, replace, or retire it.

Once you've selected a modernization approach, convert it into an actionable plan.

Your roadmap should establish what will change, in what order, who will be responsible, and how progress will be evaluated.

Prioritize modernization work according to business value, technical complexity, urgency, and dependencies.

Applications with unsupported components or significant security risks may require early attention. However, starting with the most complex application isn't always the best choice.

A smaller, lower-risk application can provide an opportunity to validate tools, workflows, and team capabilities before modernizing more critical systems.

Dependencies also affect the sequence. For example, a shared authentication service may need to be updated before applications that rely on it can be migrated.

Prepare a budget covering the full modernization process rather than development alone.

Compare upfront implementation expenses with expected operating costs over time.

A modernization approach with a lower initial cost may still require significant ongoing maintenance.

Allow contingency for unexpected issues, particularly when legacy documentation is incomplete or system dependencies remain uncertain.

Divide the project into phases with defined deliverables and approval requirements.

Assign responsible owners for architecture, development, security, data migration, testing, and business acceptance.

Establish how project risks will be reported and who can approve changes to scope, budget, or deployment schedules.

Keep the roadmap adaptable. Findings from pilot implementations or migration rehearsals may require changes to the original plan.

Completion check: Your modernization roadmap includes prioritized activities, estimated costs, responsible owners, dependencies, milestones, and approval checkpoints.

The target architecture determines how the modernized application will operate, scale, integrate with other systems, and remain maintainable.

Security and compliance requirements should be incorporated into the design from the beginning.

Choose an architecture that fits the application's actual requirements.

Possible approaches include a modular monolith, service-oriented architecture, microservices, or event-driven components.

Microservices aren't automatically the best option. Although they allow independent deployment and scaling of components, they also introduce complexity in communication, monitoring, deployment, and data management.

For smaller applications or tightly integrated business processes, a well-structured modular architecture may be easier to maintain.

Document architectural decisions covering application components, data storage, interfaces, infrastructure requirements, and operational responsibilities.

Identify security weaknesses that should be resolved during modernization.

Review authentication, authorization, encryption, secrets management, logging, and access to sensitive information.

Evaluate third-party dependencies and establish processes for vulnerability scanning, patching, and security testing.

Regulatory and contractual requirements should also influence the target design.

Security controls should be applied throughout development and deployment rather than added shortly before release.

Define how the application will remain available and recover from failures.

Establish recovery time objectives (RTOs) and recovery point objectives (RPOs) based on business requirements.

Plan backup procedures, restoration testing, appropriate redundancy, and monitoring for critical components.

Validate that the target environment can support the required availability and recovery objectives.

Completion check: The target architecture has documented design decisions, security controls, compliance requirements, availability objectives, and recovery procedures.

Data migration is one of the most sensitive stages of legacy application modernization.

Legacy databases may contain inconsistent records, outdated formats, duplicated information, or relationships that newer systems handle differently.

A migration strategy needs to explain how data will be transferred, validated, and made available without compromising business operations.

Begin by reviewing the quality and structure of existing data.

Identify missing values, duplicate records, inconsistent formats, obsolete fields, and relationships that could create migration problems.

Document how existing data structures correspond to the target system.

For example, a legacy customer database might store contact details in a single field, while the new application requires separate fields for email addresses and phone numbers.

The migration process needs defined transformation rules to handle these differences.

Determine which records must be migrated, archived, retained for compliance, or excluded.

Choose a migration approach based on data volume, application availability requirements, and acceptable downtime.

Depending on the system, this may involve a scheduled migration during a maintenance window, staged transfers, or synchronization between old and new environments.

Identify integrations that will need modification.

Where applications exchange information through APIs, files, or messaging systems, verify that the modernized implementation supports the required data formats and business behavior.

If the legacy and modernized applications will operate simultaneously, define how data updates will be synchronized between them.

Test the migration process using representative datasets before transferring production data.

Validate record counts, field values, relationships, and critical business calculations.

For sensitive or business-critical information, use appropriate reconciliation methods, including integrity checks where applicable.

Document how inconsistencies will be identified and resolved.

Also establish what happens if migration fails or the application needs to return to its previous environment.

Completion check: Data mapping rules are approved, migration rehearsals have been completed, and reconciliation procedures can verify data accuracy.

Modernizing an entire application in a single major release can increase project risk, particularly when the system supports critical business operations.

An incremental approach allows teams to validate decisions, identify problems earlier, and limit the impact of failures.

Select a manageable component or workload to validate your modernization approach.

The pilot should test an important assumption, such as whether the target platform supports the application, whether a legacy integration can be replaced, or whether an architectural change improves performance.

Define success criteria before implementation.

A useful pilot should provide evidence that the approach is technically feasible and that the team can manage its operational requirements.

Use the findings to improve estimates, identify missing skills, and refine the broader implementation plan.

Divide the application into manageable migration or development units where its architecture allows.

One approach is the Strangler Fig pattern, which gradually replaces legacy functionality while the remaining parts of the original application continue operating.

For example, an organization might modernize customer notifications first, followed by reporting and then core transaction processing.

This can reduce the risks associated with replacing an entire system at once.

However, incremental modernization also requires careful coordination. Temporary integrations, duplicated components, and parallel systems can introduce complexity.

Each phase should have defined boundaries, validation requirements, and completion criteria.

Establish repeatable processes for developing, testing, and deploying application changes.

Continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines can automate builds, execute tests, and promote approved changes through environments.

Use version control, consistent configuration management, and infrastructure as code where appropriate.

Include security and quality checks in the delivery process to reduce the risk of introducing defects during frequent releases.

Completion check: The pilot has validated the selected approach, implementation is divided into manageable phases, and repeatable build, test, and deployment processes are established.

Modernization isn't complete when the new application runs successfully in a development environment.

It must perform the required business functions, communicate correctly with connected systems, and meet operational expectations before production rollout.

Develop a testing plan based on the application's critical workflows and identified risks.

Automate repeatable tests where practical, especially for critical business processes.

Compare results against the performance baselines and acceptance criteria established earlier.

Business users should verify that the modernized application supports their actual workflows.

User acceptance testing (UAT) should include routine operations, exception handling, reporting requirements, and tasks that depend on specialized business knowledge.

Use representative migrated data during testing.

Confirm that critical records, calculations, and reports remain accurate after the transition.

Resolve issues affecting essential operations before approving production deployment.

Prepare a documented deployment plan outlining the sequence of activities, responsible teams, communication channels, and expected service interruptions.

Choose a deployment method appropriate for the application, such as a phased rollout, canary release, or scheduled cutover.

Establish measurable conditions that would stop deployment or trigger a rollback.

Rollback planning must account for data changes made after cutover. Restoring an earlier application version doesn't automatically restore database consistency, especially if new transactions have already been processed.

Rehearse deployment and recovery procedures in a suitable non-production environment.

Completion check: Required tests have passed, business owners have approved acceptance criteria, and deployment, cutover, and recovery procedures have been validated.

The initial deployment marks the beginning of ongoing operation rather than the end of modernization.

The application needs continued monitoring and maintenance to ensure that expected improvements are sustained.

Compare post-modernization results against the baseline established at the beginning of the project.

Track metrics linked to your business objectives, such as application availability, maintenance costs, response times, deployment frequency, security vulnerabilities, and user satisfaction.

Use monitoring tools, logs, traces, and alerts to identify problems that may not be visible through basic availability checks.

Review performance after deployment and at agreed intervals to determine whether the modernization initiative is meeting its objectives.

Technical improvements may introduce changes to established workflows.

Provide relevant training, updated documentation, and support resources for users and operational teams.

Ensure that administrators and developers understand the new architecture, deployment processes, monitoring tools, and incident response procedures.

Collect user feedback to identify usability issues or missing functionality.

Use production data and operational feedback to guide further improvements.

Optimization may involve updating dependencies, improving database queries, adjusting infrastructure capacity, strengthening security controls, or simplifying application components.

Once replacement functionality is stable and approved, begin decommissioning components that are no longer needed.

Before retirement, confirm that dependent applications have been migrated, necessary records are retained, and archival or compliance obligations have been met.

Removing unused infrastructure and software can reduce maintenance costs and unnecessary security exposure.

Completion check: Monitoring and support responsibilities are established, modernization KPIs are reviewed, and unused legacy components have an approved retirement plan.

Organizations without the internal resources to manage legacy modernization may need support from an external software development company.

Depending on the project, a development partner can assist with application assessments, code refactoring, architecture redesign, system integration, data migration, and implementation.

Companies offering custom software development and application modernization services include:

Idea Maker: Provides custom software development and legacy application modernization services, including application assessments, code refactoring, architecture redesign, and cloud migration.

ScienceSoft: Offers custom software development, legacy software modernization, application reengineering, cloud migration, and system integration services.

Itransition: Provides custom application development, legacy application modernization, system integration, software migration, and post-modernization support.

Simform: Offers digital product engineering, software modernization, cloud and platform engineering, and data integration services.

These are examples of providers rather than a ranked comparison.

When evaluating a development partner, consider their experience with your existing technology stack, ability to handle complex integrations, approach to data security, and experience delivering projects with similar requirements.

Ask prospective providers how they assess legacy systems, manage migration risks, preserve critical business logic, and validate application functionality before deployment.

The main steps are defining business goals, assessing existing applications, mapping dependencies, choosing a modernization strategy, developing a roadmap, designing the target architecture, planning data migration, implementing changes, testing and deploying, and continuously optimizing the application.

The exact sequence may vary depending on the application's complexity and business requirements.

Start by evaluating the application's business value, technical condition, dependencies, and future requirements.

Rehosting or replatforming may be sufficient when the application functions well and infrastructure is the primary concern.

Refactoring or rearchitecting may be necessary when code quality or architectural limitations affect maintainability and scalability.

Rebuilding or replacing becomes an option when the existing implementation no longer meets business needs or would be too costly to maintain.

Compare expected benefits, implementation costs, risks, and ongoing maintenance requirements before making a decision.

Common risks include undocumented dependencies, data loss or corruption, unexpected downtime, security vulnerabilities, integration failures, and loss of essential business functionality.

Projects may also experience delays or budget overruns when technical complexity is underestimated.

These risks can be reduced through early assessment, phased implementation, migration rehearsals, comprehensive testing, and validated recovery procedures.

The timeline depends on the application's size, architecture, technical debt, data complexity, integrations, and selected modernization approach.

A limited rehosting or refactoring project generally requires less effort than rebuilding a large application or modernizing several interconnected systems.

A reliable estimate requires an initial technical assessment. A pilot or proof of concept can help validate assumptions about implementation effort and potential challenges.

Measure success against the business and technical objectives established before implementation.

Relevant indicators include application performance, availability, maintenance costs, security improvements, deployment speed, and user satisfaction.

For example, if reducing maintenance effort was the primary goal, lower support costs and faster resolution of application issues may be more meaningful than infrastructure performance alone.

Modernization success should be evaluated over time rather than only immediately after deployment.

Legacy application modernization works best when it follows a structured plan based on business needs and the realities of existing technology.

The ten steps in this checklist cover the key decisions and activities involved, from assessing legacy systems and selecting a modernization strategy to migrating data, validating functionality, and maintaining the resulting application.

Careful dependency mapping, realistic planning, incremental implementation, and thorough testing help reduce risks throughout the transition.

Start by assessing your existing applications and defining what modernization needs to achieve. Those findings will help you identify which systems deserve attention, where to invest resources, and how to approach modernization without introducing unnecessary complexity.

#10390. ChatGPT has hit roughly 20% of Google Search volume, OpenAI says

According to OpenAI, ChatGPT has managed to grow its search use to around 20% of Google Search’s total volume, a staggering figure.

Google Search has had a massively dominant lead in the search space, with Microsoft’s Bing a distant competitor with a single-digit share at any given time. But, following its push into AI-powered search about 2 years ago, OpenAI says that ChatGPT is catching up in a big way.

Speaking at an OpenAI press event/chat yesterday, ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry confirmed that search volume in ChatGPT is “roughly one-fifth (20%) of the volume of queries of Google.”

There’s a lot of wiggle room there, as the source of Google’s search volume wasn’t revealed. OpenAI’s measurement of what a search query is boils down to when a user inputs a prompt/query that results in a web search – “Every time you ask a question and go search the web, that’s a search.” Again, a lot of wiggle room here.

But, in any case, this would be the biggest rival Google Search has had yet, and certainly explains Google’s massive push to expand AI in Search. Google admits it is facing “lower monetization” with that push, something OpenAI is also surely dealing with in its own efforts. The Verge notes that Fry said that “most people do not seem to mind ads” in ChatGPT and that “we could be doing this more, actually.”

Fry also said that “people really love” ChatGPT’s personalization and understanding.

#10389. PHP vulnerability post-mortem reveals bug was known two years ago

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Long-standing technological debt is being charged with interest.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The phrase "when it rains, it pours" applies both to weather and computer security, as proven the week of October 19, 2026, when OpenAI's GPT 5.6-Sol discovered critical vulnerabilities in the open-source programming language PHP. As published in a post-mortem, a small team of maintainers knew of the issue two years ago. Security experts had warned for decades about mounting technical debt, as agentic AI exponentially sped up vulnerability scanning, to the point that the usual 90-day disclosure window for security exploits became pointless. PHP, one of the internet's ubiquitous software packages, was unlucky enough to in the crosshairs of bug-hunting AI agents.

PHP hosts around 70% of websites on the internet, and the vulnerability allows for remote code execution with enough attacker knowledge. That combination is enough to sound alarms, but the situation got worse once the collective internet realized that, at the time, 73% of PHP installations online were running end-of-life versions with no active support — eliciting face-palms from many techies, and an exasperated sigh from anyone familiar with the ecosystem, myself included. So ultimately the problem wasn't really the vulnerability, but the general complacency.

The root cause of the vulnerability was an ancient architectural decision in the PHP engine's error handling. When a PHP process runs out of memory, it'll call the longjmp() C function, which means "immediately jump the execution to another place, no questions asked." Using longjmp() without a very good reason is generally unadvisable, though that by itself wasn't the problem.

The real issue is that if that jump happens while building a data structure (like adding an array entry, or initializing an object), that half-baked data remains in the memory heap. When the PHP engine moves execution to its cleanup-end-exit mechanism, that code will execute expecting good, complete data, but instead acts on inconsistent leftover bytes.

With some clever injection of just the right content in a web request, this mechanism can be exploited to run any code with PHP's permissions, as shown by the ongoing PHP2Shell exploit.

Gaining a remote shell does require an attacker to know the exact version and build of PHP used, so it's not as trivial as just pressing the "Hack" button. Yet the wide blast radius, with tens of millions of vulnerable servers, many of them running the same exact version, is enough to give anyone pause. After the vulnerability was made public, many sysadmins disabled version number reporting on their PHP installation, so as not to aid prospective hackers.

The PHP Foundation's post-mortem indicates that the team had known about the issue for two years, but didn't immediately see it as a cause for concern, and went back and forth several times on the best way to fix the problem while maintaining compatibility with third-party extensions.

The team admitted it had made management mistakes, but it's a small outfit with one full-time employee and 14 part-time contractors, tasked with maintaining one of the most widespread pieces of software on the internet. This is a common pattern: open-source software that's of critical importance to modern computing, yet undermanned, underfunded, or both.

The standard patch process is reasonably trivial, needing only a point update and perhaps requiring a restart of some services. The currently maintained branches of PHP are 8.1 through the current 8.5. Sysadmins rarely install PHP directly, though, relying on their Linux distribution of choice or community repositories for updated packages.

Those 73% of sites still stuck with PHP 7 are now stuck between a rock and a hard place. When PHP moved from 7.4 to 8.0 in late 2020, there were a number of changes to the language, most of them minor, but still requiring revision work. Many folks likely expected their distro to keep backporting fixes, especially for LTS (Long-Term Support) variants still in their support period, with PHP 7.4 in tow. That excuse is arguably always a little thin, as even before the AI age there were already automated tools to upgrade existing PHP code with relatively little work.

Now, depending on their specific situation, those relying on ancient versions like 7.3 or older may be forced to bring their websites' code up to speed, given that not all distros are bound to have backported fixes. For reference, PHP 7.3 came out in 2018. Making matters worse, nearly 8% of PHP installations are of version 5.x, which came out in 2005. Plus, a number of network appliances, NAS boxes, and similar hardware carries PHP within, and that too will need updating. Palo Alto Networks issued an advisory for products powered by its PanOS system, for example.

It's not all doom and gloom, however. Since the PHP Foundation published source code patches for 7.4 up to 8.1, most anyone can devise a patch for the older versions. Many entities did so, and there are packages available for Debian/Ubuntu flavors from Ondřej Surý (Debian developer) covering PHP 5.6 to 8.1. CloudLinux and AlmaLinux have likewise produced backported fixes.

This is one occasion where paying for extended support comes in handy, as commercial vendors like Ubuntu Pro and Zend released packages for positively ancient PHP versions, down to 5.5. Old website code is likely to have vulnerabilities other than the PHP engine, so a wholesale update-and-upgrade is much preferable, but very few CTOs will ever listen to that song.

Given how broad the problem is, and how it'll be a while before every machine is protected, there are a number of other means to mitigate the issue — though the proper fix is to update to a patched PHP version. Web application firewalls (WAFs) like Immunify360 may block malicious requests from even getting to the server in the first place.

Content delivery networks (CDNs) like Cloudflare or Fastly universally offer options for adding a WAF to their setup. Both firms immediately issued their respective notices and added detection of PHP2Shell to their respective rulesets. Finally, server-level security suites like CrowdStrike can also detect and block malicious activity by the PHP binary itself, in case an attacker tries something funny. It's reasonable to guess that these protections have slowed exploitation efforts.

Right now, the PHP2Shell exploit is doing the rounds, with at least one high-profile case involving a campaign that chained the PHP vulnerability together with Fragnesia (aka Dirty Frag) to eventually get kernel-level access to 25 servers seemingly linked to the U.S. government. As of October 29, security firm BitSight had recorded copious attempts of PHP2Shell intrusion across its monitoring fleet, from many distinct origins.

BitSight's dark web forum sleuths indicate that there was no one-size-fits-all exploit package available at that date. This may indicate that hacking campaigns for now are likely to be specifically targeted, but a proverbial one-click red button is bound to appear at some point, likely with a deeper exploit in tow, as with the Fragnesia example above.

Meanwhile, several cybersecurity firms kept a close eye on patching progress since the October 19 reveal. Censys has a real-time view, updated hourly, and suffice it to say that things could be going a lot better. The initial count estimated the number of vulnerable PHP installs at around 45.7 million, out of 119 million total.

The number of unpatched services has mercifully dropped to around 29 million as of this writing, seemingly indicating a patch rate of around 1 million per day, depending on how you count. It's worth noting that fix backports may not change the version number, so those instances could still count as unpatched.

But remember how most everyone toggled off their server's PHP version number reporting. According to Censys, 62% of PHP servers it's consulting aren't reporting that datum at all, so there's no telling what the actual totals might be. If history is any indication, a significant percentage of those machines remain unpatched today.

He's obsessed with detail and has a tendency to ramble on the topics he loves. When not doing that, he's usually playing games, or at live music shows and festivals.

#10387. Peak Design’s new wireless charging stand pulls double duty, literally

The three new charging accessories from Peak Design hit the high notes, like (dual) Qi2 wireless charging and wired fast charging without bulky bricks.

Last year at CES, Peak Design gave us a sneak preview of its upcoming accessory lineup. Obviously, the company is known for its premium travel bags and accessories, and they make some awesome gear. But they have mobile accessories, too, and the latest release looks pretty cool.

All three new products are charging accessories that work with Android. More specifically, they support the right protocols, like Qi2 25W wireless charging and 65W fast wired charging.

In fact, the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station supports dual, simultaneous Qi2 charging. In essence, it’s an adjustable charging stand that either lays flat or props up for a more traditional experience. Premium build materials like aluminum are used for the base, top cradle, and stem. There’s a recessed USB-C port for the adapter you’ll need to source yourself, and a secondary USB-C output port for charging a third device.

To get full power from the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station, you’ll need at minimum a 75W charging adapter and cable. The box includes a 100W cable, but charging two devices at 25W wirelessly takes a lot of power. Still, it’s pretty cool that it packs two Qi2 pads, not just one paired with a slower coil.

If that’s not your use case, a 65W charger is enough to power one pad at the full 25W. The absolute minimum required power to charge anything is 45W, Peak Design says on its website.

That might be why Peak Design also launched a slim 65W travel charger. It’s a fantastic little charger that slips into a tech pouch or backpack without much trouble. I carry a slim 65W charger wherever I go, and this easily fits the bill for portability.

It has two USB-C ports. Only one is rated for 65W, and if both are in use, you’re either getting 30W/30W or 45W/20W output. Still, I don’t demand much from a travel charger, and 65W is plenty fast enough to charge a MacBook Air or Pixel 11 within the hour.

The other option is a slim battery. Peak Design also launched a Qi2 Kickstand Travel Battery, capable of 25W wireless fast charging as well. It’s rated for 5,000mAh. Because of the kickstand that wraps around and acts as a frame for the charger, it isn’t as slim as some of the batteries we’re seeing recently. Still, I wouldn’t call it “thick” by any means.

It’s solidly built and has a really nice faux leather finish on both sides, accented by status LEDs on the bottom edge. It also sits really nicely on the Pixel 11 I used it on, which is a point of failure for some. This one feels really nice to use.

Peak Design’s new charging accessories don’t come cheap. The new travel 2-in-1 charging stand is available for $99.95, but that’s really not that bad if you value dual fast wireless charging or have a secondary device or partner to use it with. The battery comes at the same price of $99.95, and the slim 65W charger is available for $64.95.

North Korean hackers targeted two Binance employees on the way to a $6.3 billion heist

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Binance has revealed how North Korean hackers spent months preparing to steal approximately $6.34 billion in cryptocurrency last week. According to a November 3 security report by the exchange, the attackers used a malicious Homebrew installation to compromise one engineer and a fake recruitment process to trick another into installing malware disguised as a coding assistant. "Our current investigation indicates that this was a sophisticated, multi-stage campaign. It was not a single isolated breach or a simple phishing incident," the exchange said.

Binance says the first known compromise occurred in June, when an engineer in Binance’s Wallet DevOps team searched online for a popular AI coding tool and downloaded an adulterated Homebrew installation. Homebrew is a package manager, used by developers to install and manage various software tools on macOS.

Binance says it detected the unusual activity, replaced the engineer’s device, and rotated their associated credentials. However, those moves proved to be too little, too late, as the attackers had already learned about the employee’s role and identified colleagues working on the exchange's wallet infrastructure

The incident follows the compromise of another Binance software engineer who was contacted in September through a legitimate job hunting platform by a malicious actor posing as a project director. The fake recruiter arranged two interviews. One involved a purported HR representative who conducted a live video call and shared a social media account containing personal photos and videos to establish credibility, while the second involved someone posing as a CEO of a fictitious company.

Near the end of the interview, the engineer was asked to download a coding assignment containing a malicious software repository onto his work computer—a popular social engineering scheme. That malicious code executed during the setup process. At the time, Binance said the malware replaced a legitimate component of the Python version-management tool "pvenv" with a malicious version capable of executing commands and communicating with an attacker-controlled server. The exchange's endpoint security tools did not detect the malware at the time.

Those compromises began bearing fruit on October 20, as the attackers used the compromised computers associated with the fake interview and an internal AI tool to gain control over two wallet engineers' devices. They also discovered a container deployment permission token stored on the computer of the engineer initially targeted in June.

Later, on October 29, the attackers used stolen credentials to access another engineer's computer, deploy a container, and execute a malicious script. This gave them access to Binance's internal wallet signing service, allowing transactions from five hot wallets outside the exchange's normal withdrawal process.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The U.S. FBI directly attributed the theft to North Korea on November 1. ”The FBI is releasing information advising the Democratic People's Republic of Korea (North Korea) was responsible for the theft of more than $6 billion USD in virtual assets from cryptocurrency exchange, Binance, on or about October 29, 2026. The FBI refers to this specific North Korean malicious cyber activity as "TraderTraitor," the agency tweeted. Pyongyang has distanced itself from the hack, describing the U.S. claims as "preposterous nonsense.”

Binance said it had since frozen or intercepted approximately $2.85 billion in compromised assets, while 98% of the affected funds have been replenished.

The tactics resemble previous North Korean-linked campaigns. WaterPlum attackers previously used fake job postings and coding tests to infect more than 30,000 devices across 100 countries, compromising over 7,000 crypto wallets in September. A 2024 campaign that targeted Python developers with fake job offers and malware hidden in code was attributed to the Lazarus group, which was also linked to the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in 2025. Attackers posed as potential employers and sent coding tests containing Base64 obfuscated malware. Running the tests could infect developers' computers, giving attackers remote access.

#10384. Google Messages redesigning pinned chats and moving search

In addition to the new app icon, Google Messages is testing a redesign of pinned chats and search on the homepage.

Instead of pinned chats just appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is trying a redesign that significantly elevates them above all conversations.

This redesign takes advantage of the existing background layer that’s currently just home to the logo/wordmark, search, and account menu.

You’ll see large contact photos appear above all chats, which pushes the list of other conversations down the page. Like iMessage on iOS, this layout has the downside of not showing any history or last sent information.

Meanwhile, Google is testing another change where search becomes a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside new chat. When you’re scrolling down the page, you’ll see “Search” in a pill-shaped button, with the other button minimized.

This is a nice change for one-handed usage. It’s unclear if Google is removing the Gemini FAB with this update.

These updates are currently in the beta channel and not yet widely rolled out to testers.

#10382. Pin vs Noon in 2026: Which AI Recruiter Fits Your Team?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Buyers are moving from pilots to purchases: 52% of talent leaders told Korn Ferry they plan to add autonomous agents to their teams in 2026 (1,674 leaders, October 2025), and SHRM's 2026 research puts recruiting at 27% of organizations using AI, the most of any HR function. For a wider field, see our guide to the best AI recruiting agents.

Noon details come from Noon's own pages or a named third party, checked October 14, 2026.

Pin (pin.com) is an AI recruiting platform that assists recruiters by finding candidates across the 850M+ profiles Pin says it covers across North America and Europe, writing outreach from each person's profile signals and booking interviews. Candidate data comes from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. You search in plain language, paste a job description, upload ideal résumés or import a role from your ATS. Each ranked candidate has a fit score and an AI-written reason, and the model learns from your team's accept and reject feedback.

The Pin recruiting agent has two modes. In Assist you approve each step. In Autopilot it sources, screens and ranks, drafts outreach, sends the email steps, follows up on silence, schedules interviews and updates your ATS. LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks with an AI-drafted message, and you send them. Closing and offers stay human. The agent asks clarifying questions ("Is remote considered?"), shows every action in a feed and works 24/7 alongside your team. Josh Volinsky, Co-Founder of Synergy Search, says: "Pin makes sourcing candidates so natural and I love how you can just talk to it like it's a recruiter on your team and it goes hunting."

Noon (noon.ai) is an AI recruiting platform from Portal Inc. in New York whose agents source across the open web, run email and SMS outreach and book interviews. You start from a pasted job description or posting URL. Noon extracts the title, location and "non-negotiables," and its Autopilot sourcing agent keeps searching as new people enter the market. Candidates land in a ranked feed with reasoning, and thumbs-up and thumbs-down feedback calibrates the search per role.

Noon also extends further down the funnel. Its AI Interviewer runs voice screening calls, its Notetaker joins live interviews, and Inbound Screening ranks ATS applicants and flags suspected fraud. Those tools sit downstream of sourcing. Pin concentrates on the outbound workflow, from search through scheduling, and adds the team layer around it: a shared inbox, a dedupe layer so two recruiters never contact the same person, funnel dashboards and a multi-client CRM for agencies.

Pin gives you natural-language filters plus advanced filters you set yourself, and paid plans include unlimited searches.

Noon steers the search through criteria set per role. Its pages describe titles, locations, years of experience, target-company lists and must-haves, plus non-negotiables Noon says it never relaxes. You adjust by chat and feedback. We found no public description of an open-ended filter panel like Pin's, so ask for one in a demo if ad hoc searching matters.

On consistency, Pin returns a ranked shortlist with a reason for every candidate from the first search, then improves as your team accepts and rejects. Noon says its feedback loop calibrates the model per role, but it doesn't say how long that takes. Compare day one with day ten yourself (see the trial plan below).

Pin writes each message from the candidate's profile signals and calibrates to your writing voice. Its sequences run across email, LinkedIn and SMS: automated email steps plus AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence, which the recruiter sends. Sequences adapt per candidate, and any reply pauses the sequence. Free is email-only. Replies go to a shared team inbox, and a dedupe layer stops two recruiters contacting the same person.

Noon writes an intro for each candidate and drops it into sequence templates you control. Sequences run on email and SMS.

The practical difference is how contact data is packaged. Every Pin plan includes contact credits (5 a week on Free, 500 a month on Solo, 1,000 per user a month on Professional, unlimited on Business), and a personal email, work email or phone number uses 1 credit. Noon says contact enrichment is unlimited. Revealing email and phone for 300 candidates a month uses 600 of the 1,000 credits included with each Professional seat.

Pin's AI interview scheduling handles time zones, hiring panels and reschedules, and confirmations land in your ATS. Pin lists 120+ ATS integrations, including Greenhouse, Lever, Ashby, Bullhorn, iCIMS and Jobvite. ATS integration is an add-on on Solo and Professional and is included on Business. Teams without an ATS can track candidates in Pin's Kanban pipeline, a mini ATS from first touch to signed offer.

Noon's AI Coordinator answers candidate questions and books interviews through your Calendly availability, then handles reschedules. Its integrations page lists 40+ systems, including all six above plus Workday, SAP SuccessFactors, UKG, Loxo and Recruiterflow, and Noon says ATS sync is part of its plan. Several other Noon pages still say "20+," so confirm your ATS is live before you commit.

Pin publishes its pricing on its pricing page (checked October 14, 2026). Free is $0 (5 contact credits a week, 1 job, 1 agent, email-only sequences, 3 searches a day). Solo is $99 per user per month billed annually, for one seat, with 500 contact credits a month included. Professional is $135 per user per month billed annually or $179 month to month, with 2 agents and 1,000 contact credits per user each month, a shared inbox and 5 mailboxes per user. Business is custom, with unlimited seats, credits and agents and ATS integration included.

Noon sells one plan, quoted per team. Its pricing page lists unlimited sourcing, email enrichment, contacts, agents and seats, with no credits or per-seat fees. Its get-started page invites teams to create a free account, yet most "Start free" buttons open a demo-booking calendar, and Noon does not say what the free account includes.

Worked example: three recruiters on Pin Professional

With Noon, you won't know the number until after a sales call. Noon prices one flat plan per team and says adding seats doesn't change it, while Pin's cost is known before you talk to anyone and scales predictably per seat. Get Noon's quote in writing and compare it against these figures, keeping in mind that Noon's bundle includes screening and note-taking tools you may not need.

Noon also offers a 10x ROI guarantee on annual plans. According to Noon's guarantee terms (checked October 14, 2026), it values recruiter time at $60 an hour, relies on Noon's own measurement of time saved and requires minimum weekly use.

Pin is SOC 2 Type 2 audited by a third party, encrypts data at rest and in transit, and is GDPR and CCPA ready, with documents in its trust center at trust.pin.com. SSO/SAML and SCIM come with the Business plan.

Noon says it holds SOC 2 Type II and GDPR compliance, runs an annual third-party penetration test and supports SSO and SAML. According to its security page (updated September 3, 2026), Noon also offers data residency options for enterprise agreements and makes the SOC 2 report and pen-test summary available under NDA. The page does not mention CCPA.

Pin is built for agencies scaling outbound, and for recruiters and teams of any size that want a known price and hands-on control. It brings a multi-client recruiting CRM, per-seat pricing that scales predictably and a Business plan with unlimited seats. Pin assists recruiters rather than replacing them. Noon's homepage claims 40% faster time to hire, a vendor-reported figure.

Noon is good for teams with heavy inbound application volume that want voice screening and interview notes bundled with sourcing, and that are comfortable buying through a sales quote. The caveats are an unpublished price and free-account details, and only three reviews on Trustpilot.

When Pin is not the right fit: teams that only need high-volume inbound applicant screening.

When Noon is a harder fit: buyers who need a price before a sales call, teams that want a free plan with published details, and anyone who wants independent reviews to check (three on Trustpilot, no G2 listing found).

Choose Pin if you: - want a published price and a free plan with no credit card - want to start in Assist mode and hand steps to Autopilot as you build trust - run a team where several recruiters share candidates and need dedupe and a shared inbox - run an agency and need pipelines per client

#10380. Announcing DPoP Support for Google OAuth: Securing Refresh Tokens

Traditional OAuth bearer tokens operate on a "finders-keepers" model, making them vulnerable to Infostealer malware (e.g. RedLine, NodeStealer) and Adversary-in-the-Middle (AiTM) attacks.

DPoP (Demonstrating Proof-of-Possession) is an application-layer OAuth 2.0 security profile (RFC 9449) that neutralizes token theft and replay attacks by cryptographically binding tokens to a client-generated asymmetric keypair at the application layer, ensuring that even if a token is stolen, it cannot be replayed without the corresponding private key.

This article is meant to inform you on where and how to adopt DPoP and describes Google's specific DPoP implementation. A DPoP adoption SKILL.md is also available to help implement DPoP.

Some optional features and implementation decisions in RFC 9449 are delegated to Google as an authorization provider. Google Identity, and its OAuth Platform, function as a developer-facing service where engineers implement OAuth 2.0 and cryptographic security controls like DPoP to protect API integrations. Separately, Google Workspace Context-Aware Access (CAA) leverages DPoP to secure enterprise AI workloads on the Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform by cryptographically validating agent identities at the Agent Gateway.

Google's OAuth Platform supports DPoP binding specifically for refresh tokens. This design choice reflects a pragmatic engineering tradeoff: by sender-constraining the long-lived refresh token, we neutralize the primary vector for persistent offline impersonation. Meanwhile, short-lived access tokens remain standard Bearer tokens, ensuring that high-QPS resource APIs do not incur the per-request asymmetric cryptographic verification overhead of DPoP. This approach delivers robust protection for your most sensitive credentials while maintaining the high performance and low latency of Google API integrations.

Unlike standard Bearer token flows, DPoP requires client-side cryptographic key generation, strict timestamp and nonce handling, specific claim computation (htm, htu, iat, jti), exact header construction, and DPoP proof JWT construction. These changes may introduce additional system constraints to support secure computation, handling, and storage.

The IETF’s Best Current Practice for OAuth 2.0 Security (RFC 9700) discusses sender-constrained refresh tokens. Additionally, the OAuth 2.0 for Browser-Based Applications IETF draft discusses three models: Backend-for-Frontend (BFF), Token-Mediating Backend, and Browser-based OAuth Client.

Following this guidance, Google recommends using DPoP to sender-contrain refresh tokens in these models.

Unlike transport-layer token binding (e.g., mTLS/RFC 8705) which often breaks or terminates at modern cloud proxies and load balancers, DPoP operates at the HTTP header level. This makes it highly resilient for complex, multi-hop environments, including emerging Model Context Protocol (MCP) and autonomous agent architectures.

A common question from security architects is: If DPoP is designed to sender-constrain tokens, why are Google's access tokens still standard Bearer tokens?

The answer lies in operational scaling and performance at Google's scale. Verifying asymmetric cryptographic signatures (such as ES256) requires significant CPU cycles. If every API call to high-throughput Google resource servers (like Gmail, Calendar, or Drive APIs) required verifying a DPoP proof, the compute overhead at the API Gateway layer would be massive, introducing measurable latency and scaling costs.

By constraining DPoP binding strictly to the refresh token, Google's architecture achieves a balance:

Long-Lived Credentials Protected: The highly sensitive, long-lived refresh token (which can be used to obtain new access tokens indefinitely) is cryptographically bound to the client's key pair. If exfiltrated, it is useless to an attacker.

High-Performance Serving: Short-lived access tokens (typically valid for 1 hour or less) remain standard Bearer tokens. This ensures that the vast majority of API requests incur zero cryptographic verification overhead, maintaining the high performance and low latency developers expect from Google APIs.

DPoP is a powerful tool for securing OAuth 2.0 tokens, but it is important to understand its boundaries.

As autonomous AI agents and Model Context Protocol (MCP) architectures emerge, securing tool-use tokens becomes critical. While DPoP is highly resilient in single-hop client-to-server connections, multi-agent workflows (where Agent A delegates a task to Agent B, which calls Resource C) introduce delegation challenges.

In a multi-agent chain, Agent B cannot sign a DPoP proof on behalf of Agent A without access to Agent A's private key—and sharing private keys violates the core security principles of DPoP. Because Google's core consumer OAuth endpoint (oauth2.googleapis.com) does not support OAuth 2.0 Token Exchange (RFC 8693), developers building multi-agent or federated cloud workloads should consider Google Cloud Secure Token Service (STS). By routing delegation through STS, the intermediate agent can exchange the upstream token for a new, short-lived federated token cryptographically bound to its own local key pair, preserving the sender-constrained security model across the entire agent chain without key exposure.

See our DPoP Adoption Guide, our DPoP adoption SKILL.md, and improve your platform security today.

#10378. AI data centers comprehensively defeated at US midterm elections

Americans are saying no to data center developments.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Several counties in the U.S. have put data center concerns on the midterm ballot, and early results show that Americans are overwhelmingly voting against them. According to Data Centers on the Ballot, 33 measures restricting data center development have already won, with six more questions leading. Only one measure in Harwood, North Dakota, which adds an extra review process for industrial projects, including data centers, has failed, while a further five have no posted results. This is a major setback for the Trump administration, which has been pushing for AI development in the U.S., but not a surprising result, as one database has shown that “America hates data centers,” with polls showing that 70% of Americans oppose AI data center build-outs.

👨‍💻 THREAD on how data center-related ballot measures are faring tonight data centers are on the ballot in ~40 localities across 13 states, including proposed bans, votes on specific projects, and recalls of pro-data center politiciansNovember 4, 2026

Note that the issues put to a vote are varied, ranging from outright permanent data center bans to moratoriums, time-limited actions, limitations based on size or consumption, and more. The mid-term elections also resulted in the recall of the mayor and two council members in Piqua, Ohio, and the mayor of Yukon, Oklahoma, for previously supporting data center bans. A second tracker of some 46 data center measures across 10 states reveals that of 33 measures to limit data centers, every single one is set to be passed. Of a further 13 positive measures to permit data centers and their construction, 11 look set to be defeated.

Despite the overwhelming opposition to AI data centers, it’s unclear if they will affect projects that are already underway. In fact, out of the 300-plus local data center bans enacted across the U.S., only three developments totaling 2.3 gigawatts have been delayed, with 38 gigawatts expected to come online by 2027, and that investment into the infrastructure is still on track to hit $32 trillion by 2050. Nevertheless, these votes could affect future developments, especially as the U.S. Senate accuses Big Tech of misleading the public on the advantages of AI data centers. It’s also the reason why OpenAI and Anthropic are scrambling for small data center projects that range in capacity from 20 to 30 megawatts, especially as their hyperscale gigawatt sites face delays.

President Donald Trump has taken steps to reduce the impact of data centers on the average citizen, like forcing AI data centers to pay an energy dividend to local communities and making tech companies, state governors, and utility companies promise to pay their own way via the “ratepayer protection pledge.” However, it seems that his effort was too little, too late, to change the minds of voters who are afraid of utility price hikes, noise pollution, and other negative effects that data centers have had on communities where they’ve been built.

Google is now rolling out an option to disable background music entirely.

We are introducing a new setting across all platforms that allows you to disable background music added by Photos to Memories.
