#10375. Micron details ambitious DRAM and NAND roadmap — 3D NAND, High-NA EUV, new materials, and custom silicon makes a comeback

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Custom memory is about to become mainstream.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Last month, Micron disclosed its DRAM and NAND memory roadmaps for the next several years, revealing plans to use wafer bonding, new materials that enable making NAND cells smaller, High-NA EUV lithography, and detailing its moves toward 3D DRAM. Perhaps the most revealing part of Micron's story is that the company plans to expand its portfolio of custom DRAM and NAND products as AI developers increasingly require memory and storage solutions optimized for their unique processor architectures and specific workloads.

" DRAM is nearing atomic limits at this point: although we have confidence in several more planar nodes, we are very focused on making sure that we introduce 3D DRAM at the optimal time from a cost and performance point of view," said Scott DeBoer, president and chief technology and products officer at Micron, at the company's event earlier this month. "[With 3D NAND, we are] really focused on new materials densifying the cell architecture, and continuing to focus on that XYZ scaling going forward."

In a surprising move, Micron decided to abandon plans to produce DRAM with 4F2 memory cells, unlike its rivals at CXMT, Samsung, and SK hynix, citing the lack of a viable scaling path beyond where Micron is today. Instead, Micron's roadmap for the coming years includes wafer bonding, High-NA EUV lithography, new materials, and eventually 3D DRAM. The company plans to begin mass production of its next-generation 1δ memory in the second half of 2027, while subsequent 1ε, ζÅ, and ηÅ technologies are at various stages of development. In parallel, Micron is developing two generations of 3D DRAM that could eventually succeed conventional planar memory.

Micron's current 1γ (1-gamma) DRAM process technology, which is in high-volume manufacturing (HVM), is the company's first DRAM node to use EUV lithography. Previously, Micron had used multipatterning with DUV lithography and remained competitive with Samsung and SK hynix, which relied on EUV for critical layers.

Micron's next-generation 1δ (1-delta) fabrication process is currently in the yield ramp stage and is scheduled to enter mass production in the second half of 2027. The company expects the technology to increase bit density by mid-20% compared to 1γ, while advanced high-k metal gate (HKMG) CMOS, new materials, angstrom-level process control, and equipment intelligence should improve performance, power efficiency, and ultimately yields as well as costs.

Micron plans to manufacture 1δ DRAM in the U.S. and use the technology for next-generation DDR, LPDDR, graphics memory, HBM5, vertical HBM (vHBM), and vertical Edge Inference Memory (vEIM) products. Meanwhile, to make HBM5, vHBM5, and vEIM products, the node will also support additional features like through-silicon vias (TSVs) and other elements to improve thermal performance.

The subsequent 1ε (1-epsilon) process will rely on wafer bonding, which will enable Micron to produce different structures on separate wafers and then bond them together. This approach could increase flexibility in memory design and manufacturing, but Micron has yet to disclose its plans to implement wafer bonding, which structures it will bond, or what improvements in density, performance, and power consumption the technology will provide. The process is currently under development, and there is no announced production schedule.

Micron's ζÅ (zeta-angstrom) production node is currently in early development and will introduce High-NA EUV lithography to memory manufacturing, which will enable further reductions in critical dimensions. The company has not disclosed how many layers will require High-NA lithography, the expected density improvements, or when the technology will enter mass production. However, it is obvious that the node will not be here until at least 2030, years after Samsung and SK hynix adopt High-NA EUV at their DRAM fabs (planned for 2028).

The ηÅ (eta-angstrom) process represents the most distant generation on Micron’s disclosed planar DRAM roadmap and is currently in the pathfinding stage. After Micron inserts High-NA EUV lithography into its DRAM production flow, the company intends to bet on other methods of scaling. Although Micron mentions new materials, it hasn't identified them or explained how they will affect device structures. These could include new photoresists, deposition precursors, dielectric materials, or conductive materials that would enable the company to manufacture smaller transistors and capacitors, and ultimately increase DRAM cell density.

In any case, Micron's roadmap is focused on continuing development of DRAM based on 6F² cells, which differs from the plans of CXMT, Samsung, and SK hynix.

In parallel with conventional DRAM development, Micron is working on two generations of 3D DRAM, currently called 3D1 and 3D2. Unlike planar DRAM, which primarily relies on smaller memory cells to increase bit density, 3D DRAM would use the vertical dimension to accommodate more memory cells within a given area. Micron intends to introduce the technology when it becomes competitive with conventional DRAM in terms of manufacturing costs and performance. The company has not disclosed architectures, layer counts, or density targets.

In fact, while Micron has outlined its future memory architectures through 2030, its DRAM manufacturing roadmap provides a firm production window only for the 1δ process, which is scheduled to enter high-volume manufacturing in the second half of 2027. The company has not disclosed production dates for subsequent planar DRAM nodes or either generation of 3D DRAM.

In addition to conventional DRAM, Micron plans to use its future manufacturing technologies for HBM4E, HBM5, HBM5E, and new vertically integrated memory architectures. Starting with HBM4E, the company will offer both standard and custom HBM products, which will be followed by custom HBM5E, vHBM, and vEIM (vertical Edge Inference Memory). All of these will mark the re-emergence of custom memory in mass-produced products.

Custom HBM4E (cHBM4E) will enable Micron's customers to shrink memory I/O width and/or integrate additional logic into the memory's base die to free up valuable die space for compute and optimize performance and power consumption for specific AI accelerators. According to Scott DeBoer, Micron’s president and chief technology and products officer, some of these functions would otherwise reside in the ASIC itself, so moving them closer to memory should improve overall system efficiency.

"When we co-design a base die, and the certain logic elements that are desired to be put in that base die are generally an offload from GPU circuitry anyway onto the base die to put it closer to the memory," said DeBoer. "Moving what makes more sense onto the base die will still be a reality in [the] vertical HBM timeframe as well."

To support these capabilities, Micron intends to use advanced foundry process technologies for the base dies of both standard and custom HBM products, although it has not disclosed specific nodes. For custom designs, the company will select the most cost-effective process that meets customer requirements. For example, Micron expects HBM4E to require more advanced CMOS technologies to meet its performance and power targets.

Further down the road, Micron intends to introduce vertical HBM (vHBM), which will stack memory directly on top of ASICs and CPUs rather than next to them on an interposer. This architecture promises substantially higher bandwidth, thanks to dense vertical connections (as well as significantly lower latencies) thanks to adjacent placement, but it is also set to create considerable challenges related to power delivery and heat dissipation. To address them, Micron plans to use hybrid bonding for advanced interconnects, integrated heat spreaders, and thermally aware designs. Interestingly, the company expects conventional HBM and vHBM to coexist in some designs, where vHBM provides maximum bandwidth, while traditional HBM serves capacity requirements.

Another technology under development is vertical Edge Inference Memory (vEIM), which shares many of its packaging and integration technologies with HBM, but targets applications that require power efficiency more than maximum bandwidth. Micron's 1st Generation vEIM aims to deliver formidable HBM4-like 2 TB/s of bandwidth while consuming less than 1 picojoule per bit of data transfer energy.

Both architectures will require close cooperation with customers to optimize memory, logic, packaging, and thermal characteristics for particular applications, so Micron will need to work with its clients to make vHBM and vEIM successful. Interestingly, Micron's customer-specific development efforts extend beyond HBM and EIM.

For example, the company is collaborating with Meta to optimize LPDDR6 for data center applications, which primarily include reliability, serviceability, and availability. Micron expects LPDDR6 to reduce power consumption by approximately 35% compared to LPDDR5, which will be very important for dense memory deployments. The company also intends to develop DDR6 — which targets 2X DDR5 bandwidth — in cooperation with major customers to better understand and ultimately meet their needs.

Speaking of customer cooperation, DeBoer stresses that Micron now collaborates with customers from the earliest stages of product definition through joint design, development, validation, and volume production. According to the executive, this approach differs substantially from how the company worked with customers previously.

"Across our AI co-invention opportunities we are co-imagining, co-designing, co-developing, co-validating, and then delivering at scale," DeBoer said. "This opportunity is truly unique. It is very different than the way we interacted with customers even three or four years ago."

In addition to shrinking DRAM cells and developing vertical memory architectures, Micron is developing advanced CMOS technologies for peripheral circuitry. The company's roadmap includes several generations of DRAM CMOS logic with high-k metal gate transistors, followed by 1st and 2nd Generation 3D CMOS technologies.

Micron expects these advances to increase its CMOS logic performance by more than 10X between 2020 and 2030, which should help the company address the growing bandwidth and power requirements of AI-oriented memory products.

Although Micron's event was mostly dedicated to the future of DRAM, the company also shared some details about its upcoming NAND innovations, which include 11th Generation 3D NAND with 400 layers, post-G11 3D NAND, and AI-oriented NAND memory that either offers high bandwidth or high IOPS.

Micron's next-generation G11 (N170) 3D NAND technology represents a substantial departure from its current G9 (N160) process. The company plans to increase the number of active memory layers from 276 to more than 400, introduce wafer-on-wafer bonding, and implement an industry-leading memory cell pitch.

These improvements are expected to double the bit density of G11 3D NAND compared with G9 3D NAND, presumably from 21 Gb/mm2 to 42 Gb/mm2 in the case of 3D TLC devices, which should substantially reduce manufacturing costs per bit once the technology reaches mature yields.

In addition to increased storage density, G11 also targets a 50% increase in I/O speed compared with G9, which implies a potential transfer rate of approximately 5,400 MT/s. Furthermore, Micron expects to reduce data transfer energy consumption by more than 25% per bit, an important improvement for high-capacity SSDs and AI storage systems that tend to move huge amounts of data.

One of the most important changes with G11 is the adoption of wafer-on-wafer bonding, which lets Micron manufacture memory arrays and the periphery and I/O circuits on another wafer, then bond them together. Chinese manufacturer YMTC has used this method to maximize storage capacity and performance, so it is not surprising that Micron and other memory manufacturers are adopting the technique as well.

Micron expects to start mass production of G11 3D NAND in the second half of 2027.

Micron intends to continue improving 3D NAND beyond 11th Generation through a combination of new materials, denser memory cell architectures, and more energy-efficient devices and data paths. Specifically, Micron is investigating high-mobility materials that could improve the electrical characteristics of future memory devices, as well as ultra-dense cell architectures intended to increase storage density beyond what an increase in active layers provides.

Meanwhile, more energy-efficient circuitry and data paths should help offset the growing power consumption associated with higher NAND capacities and faster interfaces.

"Beyond G11, we have a strong roadmap, not going to go into great detail on it today, but really focused on new materials densifying the cell architecture, and continuing to focus on that XYZ scaling going forward," said DeBoer. "[…] The current industry direction and Micron’s direction is scaling both by adding layers [and] also by shrinking the cell dimensions."

The company has not identified the materials it intends to introduce or explained how they will affect memory cell structures. Nor has it provided quantitative density, performance, or power targets for NAND generations beyond G11.

Although Micron is not ready to share details about its G12 or G13 3D NAND generations, the company disclosed that it is developing two specialized NAND architectures for AI applications.

The first — High-IOPS AI NAND — architecture focuses on maximum random I/O performance and uses SLC-based designs with small blocks to accelerate workloads such as vector searches, graph analytics, and AI context management. Micron envisions products capable of delivering approximately 25 million to 100 million IOPS, depending on their configuration, and expects to bring products based on this approach to market over the next couple of years.

The second — High-Bandwidth AI NAND — architecture prioritizes bandwidth over random I/O performance. Micron is investigating vertically stacked NAND devices connected through TSVs and equipped with a logic base die that resides close to AI accelerators, so from many points of view this concept resembles SanDisk's HBF. However, Micron does not believe that simply placing NAND stacks alongside AI accelerators on an interposer offers sufficient advantages. Instead, the company is exploring alternative integration approaches that bring NAND closer to compute resources, but don't necessarily consume valuable shoreline (the perimeter, or edge) of AI accelerators.

"[The High-Bandwidth AI NAND is] more costly and has to be implemented in partnership with customers in a way to ensure that they are meeting the mark and the cost point to drive the value proposition," DeBoer said. "We are enabling TSV-stacked NAND with a logic base die to bring memory closer to accelerators. We do not think placing it along the shoreline of the GPU has merit. Instead, we want to position it closer to the compute itself to take advantage of this high-performance NAND."

These specialized AI-focused NAND architectures will complement Micron's conventional SSD roadmap, which includes plans for drives with capacities of up to 512 TB in the foreseeable future.

Anton Shilov is a contributing writer at Tom’s Hardware. Over the past couple of decades, he has covered everything from CPUs and GPUs to supercomputers and from modern process technologies and latest fab tools to high-tech industry trends.

#10373. Google will fix Nest devices that didn’t adjust for Daylight Savings, breaking alarms and more

Google says it will roll out a fix for Nest devices that were impacted by problems with Daylight Savings adjustments due to changes in Canada.

A number of Nest Hub and Nest Thermostat users on Reddit noticed this week that their devices were showing the wrong time following the Daylight Savings time change.

This isn’t just a display bug, with real impacts including late alarms, delayed or broken automations, and altered schedules, among other impacts. The problem is specific to certain parts of Canada, user reports show, as changes in the country left some provinces skipping Daylight Savings changes while others kept the status quo. Google previously issued guidance on how to handle these changes on Android phones.

On Nest devices, though, adjusting time zones isn’t quite as easy, so this was a bigger headache.

Google is working on a fix, though, the company confirmed to Android Authority. In a statement, Google says that it is aware of the problem and working to resolve it.

Google is aware of an issue affecting a small subset of devices in some provinces in Canada where daylight savings is no longer observed. We apologize for any inconvenience and are actively working on a fix.

Google also replied to some affected users to confirm a fix is coming but, as of November 4, the fix does not appear to be in place.

#10371. How to Build a Typed AI Pipeline With Jev and Clairic.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A support email lands: "Order NW-47790, the tent poles snapped on the first night, I paid $389 and I want every cent back today or I'm disputing the charge."

To act on that, your code needs answers to a handful of small questions. Is it a refund request? How urgent is it? Should a person handle it? What's the order number? How much money is involved? It's the same problem whether you're sorting a support inbox or building a custom chatbot that has to work out what each customer is asking for.

The usual move is to send the whole email to a chat model with a prompt that ends "respond in JSON", then validate whatever comes back. It works, but you're paying a model built to write essays to fill in five fields, and you're parsing its guess at a schema.

System One models are built for exactly this kind of job. In this tutorial we'll build a small support inbox pipeline with two of them: Jev from TypeSafe makes the decisions, and Clairic from Cloudflare reads the values. Plain TypeScript then turns their answers into an action. No prompt engineering for output formats, and no JSON repair.

The name comes from Daniel Kahneman's Thinking, Fast and Slow. System 1 is the fast, intuitive kind of thinking you use to read a face or a sign. System 2 is the slow, deliberate kind you use for long division.

A System One model is an AI model that understands natural language but doesn't generate text. You give it some input (the state) and a set of typed questions, and it gives back typed answers with probabilities. There's no free text to parse, because the model can only answer in the shape you asked for.

That's different from asking a chat model for structured output. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema shows how to constrain a chat model and validate its JSON with Zod. With a System One model, the type is the interface: a yes/no question comes back as a probability, a pick-one question comes back as one of your options, and a date comes back as a date.

Jev and Clairic are both System One models, but they do different jobs, and that's what makes them a good pair.

Jev can't hand you an order number, and Clairic has no way to pick from a list of options or answer yes or no. Give each one the job it's built for.

If you've read SitePoint's Jev and LangChain tutorial, you've seen Jev deciding which model handles an agent's request. Here we'll use it on the incoming text itself.

Five made-up emails for a made-up outdoor gear store go in. For each one:

Both models read the same email, so they run at the same time.

Put the three values in your environment:

No SDKs are needed. Both models are a single fetch call.

Here's the test inbox. Each email is there to test a different path through the rules:

Jev takes a state (the email) and a set of named questions. We'll ask three, one of each type:

All three questions go in one request. Jev reads the email once and answers every question in parallel, so adding a question costs very little:

We pin jev-1.14.0 rather than the jev-latest alias. Your thresholds get tuned against one version's probabilities, so you want to choose when that version changes.

For the angry tent email, Jev came back with:

(Trimmed: the real response also includes the probability of every option and level.)

Clairic takes the same state, plus a set of typed fields instead of questions:

reference_date anchors relative dates like "the 14th" or "last Monday". In production, pass the date the email arrived.

Each answer comes back with three things worth using:

For the "where is it?" email, Clairic read the deadline like this:

"The 14th" became a full date, worked out from the reference date. Text fields like order_number also come back as character spans of the original email. The value is copied from the input, not generated, so it can't invent an order number that isn't there.

The two models don't depend on each other, so send both requests together:

The time for each email is whichever model is slower, not the two added together.

This is the part a chat model usually ends up doing badly: the business rules. Here they're ordinary code you can read, test, and change without touching a prompt:

Notice that the model's confidence sits right next to the business rule. A $18.95 refund is only automatic if Clairic is sure about both the order number and the amount. Raising the refund limit or the confidence bar is a one-line change, and you don't need to call either model again to see what it would do. Save the answers and replay them.

Every email went where it should. The whole run used 2,703 input tokens on Jev and 2,292 on Clairic, about US$0.0003 in total. At that rate, 10,000 support emails would cost roughly US$0.70.

The interesting part of the run wasn't the right answers. It was the two wrong ones.

"From last Monday." The run used 2 November 2026 as the reference date, which is a Monday. "Last Monday" means 26 October, but Clairic answered 2026-11-02, the reference date itself. Its confidence on that answer was 0.54, compared with 0.99 or higher on almost every other value in the run.

"A couple of weeks ago." Clairic turned this into 2026-10-19, which is a fair reading but still a guess. Its confidence was 0.43.

Neither date changed any action, because the rules only trust values at 0.9 or above. That's the habit to build: a value under your bar counts as unknown. A chat model returning JSON would have handed you "2026-11-02" with the same certainty as everything else.

One more catch: Clairic copied the order number as #NW-48213, hash included, because that's how the customer wrote it. Text values are spans of the input, so normalising them is your code's job. That's one line of code.

System One models handle the reading and the routing. They don't hold a conversation. Once an email is routed, something still has to write the reply, ask a follow-up question, or explain a returns policy in the customer's own words. That's where a chat model earns its cost.

The split that works is: System One models decide, code applies the rules, and a chat model only gets the cases where words matter.

We built a support inbox triage pipeline from two small models and some ordinary code:

Each email took about half a second and cost less than a hundredth of a cent. Most importantly, the two answers that would have been wrong came back flagged as unsure.

The full code is about 160 lines with no dependencies. Try adding a fourth Jev question (does the customer mention a photo?) or an estimate field in Clairic (how many days until the trip?), and see how little the rest of the pipeline has to change.

Easier installation and improved thermal conductivity.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Thermal Grizzly has announced the PhaseSheet PTM Pro, an improved version of its popular phase-changing thermal pad. According to the manufacturer, the new thermal pad offers increased thermal conductivity alongside a redesigned carrier film that makes the PhaseSheet PTM Pro easier to handle and install. While this may seem like a relatively minor change, the new carrier film is designed to peel away more easily from both sides, reducing the risk of tearing the sheet during installation.

Like the original PhaseSheet PTM, the Pro version uses a phase-changing material that alters its physical properties depending on the temperature. At room temperature, the sheet remains solid, much like a conventional thermal pad. However, at temperatures above 45°C, the material transitions into a very low-viscosity liquid that behaves similarly to thermal paste.

This phase-changing behavior helps minimize the pump-out effect commonly associated with traditional thermal paste. Pump-out occurs when thermal paste gradually moves out from between a heatsink and a GPU die or CPU IHS due to repeated thermal expansion and contraction of the contacting surfaces. Over time, this can reduce the amount of thermal paste covering the contact area and potentially degrade cooling performance.

The PhaseSheet PTM Pro is also reusable and electrically non-conductive, meaning it can be safely used on components without an IHS, including mobile CPUs, GPUs, and other internal components such as memory and storage. According to a performance graph shared by Thermal Grizzly, the new PhaseSheet PTM Pro outperforms several popular thermal pastes and thermal pads in controlled testing. Using a 30 x 30mm contact area, 300N of mounting force (48 PSI), and a 240W heat load, the PhaseSheet PTM Pro recorded a temperature difference of 5.83°C, the lowest among all the tested materials. This puts it ahead of the KryoSheet (6.13°C), Duronaut Pro (6.29°C), and the original PhaseSheet PTM (6.36°C).

The PhaseSheet PTM Pro can be purchased through Thermal Grizzly's website for $23.28 per 40 x 40mm sheet. That's considerably more expensive than the original PhaseSheet PTM, which costs $13.92 for a larger 50 x 40mm sheet.

#10367. Three months in, Galaxy Z Fold 8’s cameras aren’t enough, and Fold 9 might not fix it [Gallery]

I’ve been enamored with my Galaxy Z Fold 8 since I started using it roughly three months ago, but its one big downside has been the cameras, so I’m not exactly thrilled to hear that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 might now make any progress here.

In my review of the Galaxy Z Fold 8, I called the cameras “the biggest mistake in this whole package,” and that opinion hasn’t changed after three months. The duo of 50MP sensors get the job done, but not as well as I want for such an expensive smartphone.

What I’ve found as I’ve continued to use this device that is that I don’t want to use these cameras. I’ll use them for cases where quality isn’t as important, like snapping pictures of my meals for my nutrition app, scanning documents, and things like that, but I’m always reaching for the phone in my other pocket for any other snaps. And that’s a privilege that not everyone has, nor should they be required to do so on their pricey foldable.

The downsides I’m most frequently running into include a lack of any quality zoom range – quality falls off a cliff beyond 2x – and problems with framing. The short design of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 means that, often, I’m capturing my finger at the bottom of the shot unintentionally.

The results through the main lens are perfectly fine, and I’m actually rather enjoying Samsung’s saturated colors as the Fall foliage cranks up, but I’m still constantly underwhelmed.

Foldable cameras have always been lesser compared to standard smartphones, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s lack of a third lens just makes that a tiny bit worse.

And yet, it sounds like Samsung is happy with the status quo. Some leakers are claiming that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 won’t make any changes to the camera sensors. A 50MP main and a 50MP ultra-wide. Fold 9 UItra might get a new main sensor, but no major changes to the hardware either.

To say that’s disappointing is an understatement, but if I can make one plea to Samsung, it’s to at least change the camera layout next time around. “Core identity” excuses aside, constantly needing to readjust my grip on the phone just to avoid taking a picture of my finger is frustrating, and having a new layout also opens the door to improvements on the table wobble and the whole Qi2 debacle. If we’re not getting new sensors, at least let’s get some physical improvements to the layout.

#10366. Logitech G Pro X3 Superstrike Review: $200 for the illusion of a click

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The G Pro X3 Superstrike brings a new chipset and rebalancing to Logitech's revolutionary mouse. But at $200, it's probably not worth it for most gamers.

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

It only launched at the beginning of this year, but Logitech's most innovative, game-changing mouse, the Logitech G Pro X2 Superstrike, has already gotten an upgrade.

The new Logitech G Pro X3 Superstrike looks just like its predecessor (okay... the colors are different), but it's what's inside that counts. The new mouse features Logitech's new NxLOGI chipset, which improves performance and efficiency and supports 8,000 Hz polling over both wired and wireless connections. The mouse also weighs two grams less and has been rebalanced to be more center-weighted rather than front-heavy, like the X2 Superstrike.

The upgrade doesn't come without an upcharge, of course: The new G Pro X3 Superstrike costs $199.99, which is... kind of crazy for a mouse. The X2 Superstrike debuted at $179.99, which was already higher than average, but $200 seems like a line most will not want to cross. The mouse does come in two colorways now — black and "magenta eclipse," a black and pink colorway that matches Logitech's other pink G Pro peripherals.

The G Pro X3 Superstrike has the exact same shape as the original G Pro X2 Superstrike — which has the exact same shape as the G Pro X Superlight and Superlight 2. The mouse has a pseudo-symmetrical design, with sides that taper at the middle, and a centered hump with a mild slope. It's not a terribly exciting or revolutionary shape, but it's comfortable, easy to handle, and quick to get used to. The mouse measures 4.92 inches (125mm) long by 2.5 inches (63.5mm) wide, and is 1.57 inches (40mm) high at its highest point.

The X3 weighs just 2.08 ounces (59g), which makes it 2g lighter than its predecessor (2.15oz / 61g). While that's not a big difference, Logitech has rebalanced the weight in the X3 Superstrike to make it feel more centered — and it does feel more centered, though it still felt slightly front-weighted to me. But still, it feels noticeably different (and better) than the very front-heavy feel of the original X2 Superstrike.

Fancy chipsets and HITS technology aside, the X3 Superstrike is a pretty basic five-button mouse, with two large primary buttons that have slight finger grooves, two large side buttons, and a clickable, notched scroll wheel covered in textured rubber. The mouse has the same smooth matte finish as the X2 Superstrike and the Superlights, which feels fine but is prone to fingerprints. Of course, the mouse still has the Superstrike branding all over it, with crosses on the mouse buttons, "Superstrike" on the right side, and a giant "X3" on the left.

The bottom of the mouse is also pretty simple, with large UHMWPE mouse feet at the top and bottom, plus a strip around the sensor. There's also a power switch and a magnetic mouse puck that you can swap out — either for the included extra puck, which is covered in UHMWPE for even more glide, or for the charging puck that comes with Logitech's PowerPlay 2. Under the mouse puck, there's a slot for storing the mouse's wireless dongle, which is a nice touch — other ultra-lightweight, performance-focused mice don't always have this.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

In the box, the X3 Superstrike comes with a rubberized 6-foot (1.8m) USB-C to USB-A cable, a 2.4GHz wireless USB-A dongle, an extra mouse puck, pre-cut grip tape, and a USB converter. The cable is lightweight and flexible — while it's not covered in paracord, it's fairly usable as a mouse cable with the X3 Superstrike. It's worth pointing out that the X3 Superstrike does support 8,000 Hz polling over both its wireless and wired connections, now (the X2 Superstrike only supported 8,000 Hz polling over wireless).

4.92 x 2.5 x 1.57 inches / 125 x 63.5 x 40 mm

The G Pro X3 Superstrike features Logitech's Hero 2 optical sensor, which has a maximum resolution of 48,000 DPI and a maximum speed of 930 IPS, and can handle up to 90 G's of force. If those numbers seem different from the Hero 2 optical sensor in the X2 Superstrike, that's because they are — while both mice have the same sensor, apparently the new NxLOGI chipset in the X3 Superstrike unlocks the true potential of said sensor, and allows for higher resolution, speed, and acceleration. That said, it is a great sensor — accurate, consistent, and able to track on virtually any surface without skipping.

Like the X2 Superstrike, the X3 Superstrike features Logitech's Haptic Inductive Trigger System (HITS), which means there are no switches under the primary buttons — instead, there's a combo of haptic motors and inductive sensors that allow for adjustable actuation and create the illusion of a mouse click. This is the most intriguing part of the Superstrike line, since raising the actuation point genuinely does translate to faster, more responsive clicks.

The haptic click feedback is interesting. I found the feedback to be surprisingly convincing on the X2 Superstrike, even though it's still pretty obvious that it's not a "real" switch. The click feedback on the X3 Superstrike is still... fairly convincing, but it's definitely softer than the feedback on the original. You can still adjust the feedback level (five levels), but feedback just feels firmer on the original — I guess Logitech sacrificed some haptic power for efficiency, or something. The side buttons still have regular switches, and they're a little mushy but otherwise inoffensive. The scroll wheel has a very soft scroll and muted click, but at least it's not loud. In fact, this is probably the quietest mouse I've ever used — even with click strength set to the highest (5), it's barely audible.

In addition to adjustable actuation, the X3 Superstrike also allows for Rapid Trigger, which resets the mouse button's actuation as soon as your finger lifts, allowing for even faster clicks.

The G Pro X3 Superstrike is configurable via Logitech G Hub, which is the company's universal peripheral software. You can use G Hub to adjust the mouse's settings — DPI, DPI steps, and polling rate (both wired and wireless) — calibrate the mouse, and remap buttons (though you're probably not remapping a ton of buttons on a five-button mouse). You can also enable BHOP for the scroll wheel, which helps prevent accidental movement when you bump the scroll wheel.

And, of course, you can set up the HITS features in the HITS tab. These include adjustable actuation, Rapid Trigger, and click haptics, and each mouse button can be set independently. There's also a bunch of presets from professional competitive gamers, in case you're not sure where to start (you can also add your own custom presets to this list).

Logitech rates the battery life of the G Pro X3 Superstrike at 135 hours in constant motion, which is actually a pretty big improvement over the X2 Superstrike's 90-hour rating. This is over a 1,000 Hz polling rate, of course, and the number drops to under 40 hours with an 8,000 Hz polling rate (which is still not terrible, I suppose). Thankfully, the X3 Superstrike can get an 8,000 Hz polling rate over both its wired and wireless connections — the X2 Superstrike has an 8,000 Hz wireless polling rate but maxes out at 1,000 Hz when plugged in — so it won't be the battery life that does you in.

The G Pro X3 Superstrike isn't too different from the X2 Superstrike — but the difference in balance, at least, is absolutely noticeable. This doesn't make me think the X3 Superstrike is revolutionary, though it makes me think that Logitech should have waited and launched the Superstrike line with this mouse. The click haptics are weaker here, but the mouse is better overall. However, is it better enough to justify the $200 price? Probably not.

If you're at the top of your game and you're absolutely certain that the only thing holding you back is the minuscule latency from non-adjustable actuation, sure, get the X3 Superstrike. If you already have the G Pro X2 Superstrike, the upgrade probably isn't worth it — in the time it took you to get used to the click haptics, you probably also got used to the balance, and that's the only noticeable difference. And if you're just looking for a symmetrical, ultra-lightweight mouse, you can always pick up the G Pro X Superlight 2, which can often be found for around $100.

#10364. The first Snapdragon phone with Android Linux Terminal support is here

The Redmagic 12 Pro is the first Snapdragon-based Android phone to support the Linux Terminal introduced in Android 16 for Pixel phones.

Android power users who want a new Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6-powered phone like the Redmagic 12 Pro now have another reason to be excited about the latest Qualcomm processors: the Android Linux Terminal.

Thanks to tipster Dylan H, who wrote in asking about compatibility. After enabling the Android Linux Terminal and running a few commands to check compatibility, I found out that the Redmagic 12 Pro does, indeed, support the Linux Terminal app. I’ve written Qualcomm asking about wider Snapdragon support for Linux Terminal and will update this article if I receive a response.

Google introduced the Terminal app as a power user feature in the Android 16 update for Pixel phones, and Samsung added support in One UI 8.5 for Exynos-powered Galaxy S26 models. So far, however, Snapdragon phones have not appeared on the compatibility list.

this is super cool! i guess my first question is if it is subject to the Android 32 subprocess limit that effectively cripples the terminal on Googlebooks.

Linux Terminal support has been slowly building on Google platforms. Googlebooks sort of support it, and now it appears that more Android phones will support it as well. The implementation on the Redmagic 12 Pro seems slightly different from what’s on Exynos-powered Samsung phones, as you cannot adjust the disk size (it’s locked at 132GB available), but you can adjust the amount of memory available for the terminal to use.

By default, Terminal has access to 1GB of memory, although the Redmagic 12 Pro lets you increase that to 10.6GB. You can also adjust the display resolution and enable Keep Awake in advanced settings. The Redmagic 12 Pro also supports the GUI feature, so programs like GIMP should work if you can get them installed (I kept running into 404 errors using the standard installation command).

The Redmagic 12 Pro goes up for preorder on November 12 and includes several freebies and a launch discount before November 17. See our full review to find out if it’s right for you.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Team Group CEO Chen Ching-wen said during the company’s November 2 earnings call that the memory chip shortage is expected to become worse from the end of 2026 until the end of 2027. BigGo Finance quoted Chen as saying that there is “no downward price trend whatsoever,” and that some purchasers could face a scenario “even money can’t buy goods.” He also noted that the spread between spot and contract prices for memory chips ranges from 30% to 50%, meaning short-term supply is significantly more expensive than long-term contracts.

"We have contracts but can't get enough volume, so we have to go to the spot market and pay a premium to grab supply," the Team Group chief said during the briefing. Contract prices for 16GB DDR5 chips climbed from $8.70 in October 2025 to $48 by September 2026. This is an increase of more than 550% in the past 12 months, and it seems that the price hikes are not letting up. He also estimated that supply and demand will not balance until the latter half of 2028 or perhaps even later. “How can prices fall when there’s nothing left?” said the CEO.

The Chairman also noted that even though CXMT and YMTC have increased their market share to almost 10%, most high-end DRAM and NAND flash chips still come from Kioxia, Micron, Samsung, and SK hynix. Even then, the prices for the chips that these Chinese companies make still track the market closely, meaning they’re not the budget savior that many were expecting. As such, he expects that pricing for budget components will not ease until 2029, while high-end chips will continue to remain in short supply.

These estimates go against Acer CEO Jason Chen’s prediction that component pricing will reverse by the latter half of 2027. Chen suggests that only high-end chips, like LPDDR5X-9600 and Nvidia N1-series SoCs, will remain in shortage while mainstream parts will finally go down. PCs will still experience hikes between 5% and 20% until the end of this year, plateau during the first half of 2027, before finally going down. Chen claims that most major suppliers want to hype the shortage for as long as possible to preserve their margins.

Unfortunately, there’s no way for us to know which of these predictions will come true. While consumers would gladly want to have a break from the nearly continuous price hikes that PC parts and components have been experiencing over the past months, the AI data center build-out is apparently continuing, even as many Americans voted against it.

#10359. Porting the NDK “Endless Tunnel” to iOS: A Masterclass in Cross-Platform C++

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developers talk about high-performance mobile games, the Android NDK samples by Bruno Oliveira are often cited as the gold standard for clean, low-level C++. But as an iOS developer, I wanted to see if I could take that same “pure” C++ logic and make it sing on an iPhone.

My latest project, TunnelFeveriOS, is the result of that journey. It isn’t just a port; it’s a blueprint for building a custom game engine.

The original Endless Tunnel is built to live within the Android lifecycle (JNI, Activity, and NDK). Moving this to iOS required more than just hitting “compile.” I had to build a Platform Abstraction Layer.

To make the C++ engine talk to the iOS UI, I utilized Objective-C++ (.mm** files)**. This allowed me to keep the game's high-speed math and geometry in native C++ while using Cocoa Touch to manage the windowing and touch input. In TunnelFeveriOS, you’ll see how the view controller manages the game loop, feeding time deltas and touch coordinates directly into the C++ core.

Since the original game uses OpenGL ES, I leveraged iOS’s GLKit framework. The trick was ensuring that the shaders and vertex buffer objects (VBOs) originally written for Android were correctly initialized within the iOS EAGLContext. The result? 60 FPS performance that feels native because, at the machine level, it is.

A port is only “production-ready” if it handles modern app requirements. I integrated Google Mobile Ads (AdMob) to trigger Interstitial ads during game-over states.

This presented a unique threading challenge:

I implemented a messaging system that allows the C++ core to “request” an ad, which the Objective-C wrapper then catches and executes on the main thread — preventing the game from hanging or crashing.

This project proves that you don’t need a heavy engine like Unity or Unreal to achieve cross-platform results. By keeping the “brain” of your game in C++ and building thin “shells” for each platform (Android NDK or iOS/XCode), you gain:

If you’re interested in how to bridge the gap between low-level C++ and high-level iOS development, check out the full source code here:

Now that the foundation is laid, I’m looking into implementing a Metal-based renderer to fully embrace the future of Apple graphics. If you’ve worked on C++ to Metal bridges, I’d love to hear your thoughts in the comments!

5 Gbps service sits above the company’s 2 Gbps, 1 Gbps, and 300 Mbps fiber plans

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Just over a year ago, T-Mobile launched its home fiber internet service in the United States, buoyed by its acquisition of Lumos. At the time, T-Mobile’s fiber service reached half a million customers across the country, with speeds of up to 2 Gbps. This week, T-Mobile introduced a new 5 Gbps tier for customers who crave even more speed.

The 5 Gbps tier is priced at $90 per month and comes with a 10-year price guarantee. Given how often legacy cable internet providers would raise prices, forcing customers to jump through hoops to keep the status quo, a 10-year price lock could be very appealing to a large segment of the population. But as with most things, there is some fine print to the offer, which reads, “Taxes & fees, voluntary equipment or speed upgrades, select limited-time promotions, per-use charges, third-party services, and network management practices aren’t included.”

To get the $90/month price, you’ll also need to set up AutoPay with T-Mobile. However, to further sweeten the deal, customers will also receive a mesh Wi-Fi extender that broadens the coverage of the included Fiber gateway.

For new customers, T-Mobile is also offering two months of free service (via bill credits) and a $300 virtual prepaid debit card. T-Mobile’s pricing for the 5 Gbps plan is an aggressive deal in the home fiber market. For example, GFiber (Google) costs $100/month for 3 Gbps speeds, while the company’s 8 Gbps tier is $150/month.

T-Mobile’s new 5 Gbps plan joins the existing 2 Gbps ($70/month), 1 Gbps ($60/month), and 300 Mbps ($45/month) plans. All of the plans feature symmetrical upload/download speeds, which is a big upgrade for anyone coming from a traditional cable internet provider.

For people who don’t have access to T-Mobile’s growing fiber footprint in the U.S., the company also offers its unlimited 5G Home Internet service, with prices starting at $50/month.

#10354. ‘Enough Phone’ is an Android phone roughly the size of iPhone 13 Mini, launching in 2027

Everyone says they want small Android phones back, and 2027 might be your chance to actually buy one. The “Enough Phone” has a mere 5.3-inch display, decent specs, and a price of $599 and less.

Launching in May 2027, the “Enough Phone” is a 5.3-inch Android phone that simply does want to be “enough.”

The company says the 5.3-inch LCD display comes in at 1520×720 and that the phone will be powered by a MediaTek Dimensity 7500 chipset, 8GB of RAM, and offers 128GB of storage. It’ll come with either “stock Android” or OdysseyOS, an Android skin used by SLEKE and designed to be minimal and act like a modern dumbphone. SLEKE currently sells repurposed Pixel phones running this software.

Android 17 will run out of the box with updates through 2032.

“Enough Phone” measures in at 132.8 x 63.8 x 12mm and will have a battery between 3,500 and 4,000 mAh which will be user-replaceable. While it’s thicker, the physical size is roughly the same as iPhone 13 Mini.

Rounding out the package would be a fingerprint sensor in the power button, a single 50MP (Sony IMX766) camera on the back, and a 32MP selfie camera up front.

The only redeemable aspect of this phone is the small display but nothing about it makes it a good value in the 2nd half of 2027 when you have much more capable phones like the Pixel A series for nearly the same money or a bit more but at least you're getting a lot more phone for your money.

The company says that these specs are “mostly final” and that pricing is planned for $599.

“Enough Phone” is funding on Kickstarter with $399, $449, and $499 early bird reservation tiers ahead of the May 2027 shipping estimate.

#10353. Nintendo ROM chip supplier Macronix quits to focus on chips for AI data centers, reports indicate

Nintendo, one of the last in the physical game market, is losing one of its key ROM chip suppliers to AI data centers.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The death of physical games could be getting closer as Macronix, one of Nintendo’s key ROM chip suppliers for Switch and Switch 2 game cartridges, is reportedly shutting down its ROM business to focus on manufacturing NOR and NAND flash chips to supply data centers, multiple reports claim. Storage is already becoming expensive, and Macronix’s move may force Nintendo to increase cartridge prices, produce more Game-Key cards, or lean more towards digital purchases.

"Nintendo's main memory chip maker is quitting the Switch business," MST Financial David Gibson reports on X. "Macronix, which makes the ROM chips in Switch 1 and Switch 2 game memory cards, is phasing out that business. There will be no new ROM products, and Q3 is the last quarter it reports ROM sales separately.

1/4 Nintendo's main memory chip maker is quitting the Switch business Macronix, which makes the ROM chips in Switch 1 and Switch 2 game memory cards, is phasing out that business. There will be no new ROM products, and Q3 is the last quarter it reports ROM sales separately.November 4, 2026

He notes that Macronix ROM sales in Q3 of this year dropped to 5%, compared to last year’s 26%. Furthermore, Gibson says that the NT$37.8 billion capital expenditure is allocated to NOR and NAND Flash, leaving none for ROM as the company shifts its manufacturing capacity to supply AI needs — Taipei Times corroborated the report, stating, "At the same time, Macronix is gradually phasing out of ROM production, Wu said. It also reports that Macronix’s capital expenditure is split into two phases, the NT$ 22 billion for expanding manufacturing capacity, followed by an additional NT$15.8 billion for NAND flash used in eMMCs and 3D NAND.

Gibson expects that Nintendo’s digital margins will grow through more Game-Key Cards or digital-only sales, but as Macronix isn’t the only source of ROM chips, “physical cards will likely carry on for some time.” Gibson further reports that digital margins have been slowly rising, with numbers fluctuating depending on the quarter due to major physical game launches.

3/4 My view: Macronix isn't Nintendo's only game memory card maker, so physical cards will likely carry on for some time. But at the margin, digital will grow, through more Game-Key Cards (which hold a download key, not the game) and digital-only sales.November 4, 2026

ROHM group companies ROHM Semiconductor and LAPIS Semiconductor still supply ROM chips to Nintendo, so it’s unlikely that Nintendo will eventually go digital-only in the near future like Sony and Microsoft’s recent plans. With one less supplier, however, Nintendo may be forced to increase prices on physical games.

The biggest loser in all of this (besides the gamers), however, may be fabless chip design company MegaChips. Nintendo doesn’t buy ROM chips from Macronix directly; MegaChips designs the ROM chips according to Nintendo’s needs and, having no manufacturing capacity, outsources it to Macronix. MegaChips’ 2022 company report states that Nintendo accounted for 87.8% of its sales, while Macronix accounted for 67.8% of its outsourced manufacturing purchases. The report even states that if Macronix could manufacture ROM chips, “MegaChips’ operating results may be impacted.” The 2022 document further says that there was no sign that the risk would become apparent; however, the risk just became very real, and we’re not aware of MegaChips’ contingency plan to deal with this situation. However, Macronix and MegaChips have likely talked amongst themselves about Macronix’s plans to stop ROM chip manufacturing, and have given MegaChips some time to find another manufacturer before Macronix shuts down the ROM chip business.

#10351. Google’s latest smart home products make the perfect gifts, and they’re on sale at Best Buy

Google Home is one of the most user-friendly smart home ecosystems, and Google has built out an extensive collection of cameras, speakers, and displays. That positions them as great gifts to expand someone’s smart home.

It’s pretty common to get one or two smart home devices and call it a day, but ecosystems like Google Home begin to come alive when there are several devices working in harmony with each other. For instance, you can start out with the Google Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen), as many do. It makes A/C and heat controls easy, and even saves you money along the way.

But adding something like the Google Home Speaker with Gemini built-in means you get even more control of your home’s indoor environment. You could go a step further and add a Google Nest Cam (indoor or outdoor) or a Google Nest Doorbell, whether wired or battery. It’s a bit pricier, but I love the Google Nest Cam with Floodlight as a smart home gift option for someone, since it provides more utility.

In any case, adding a couple more foundational pieces to your smart home makes it more useful. And as Google adds features with Gemini for Home, users get access to smart alerts and notifications that those cameras didn’t provide a year or two ago, all within the Google Home app.

If you’re gifting for someone who doesn’t have the beginnings of a smart home setup, Google’s Nest products are a good start. You could start with the Google Home Speaker, depending on their needs. If they like listening to music and want a smart home assistant at the ready, Google’s latest speaker delivers.

The Google Home Speaker has a 360-degree speaker that throws audio in every direction, filling the room. It controls other devices as that smart home fills out, and it can even act as part of a stereo setup for the Google TV Streamer.

Right now, the Google Home Speaker is at its lowest price on Best Buy, sitting at $79.99 – $20 off. It comes in some great colorways, too, like Berry and Jade. Of course, there’s Porcelain and Hazel for more toned-down looks.

Another great smart home starter gift is the Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen) or Nest Thermostat. The difference between the two comes down to look and capabilities, but both work very well.

The Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen) works with the majority of HVAC systems and converts them into one manageable system through the Google Home app. Over time, it learns your heating and cooling habits to form better schedules that save you money and stress.

Right now, the Nest Learning Thermostat (4th) gen is down to $219 from $279 at Best Buy – $60 off. That’s the best price we’ve seen from the store, and it’s a good deal overall.

Best Buy is also offering a couple of bundles if you want to go further. You can add Nest Cams to your cart with the Nest Learning Thermostat for $100 off the order. If you want to bundle a couple of items into a gift for someone –or yourself – that’s the way to go.

Of course, you can’t go wrong with the Nest Doorbell cameras (wired or battery). Go for the battery edition if you’re limited on wiring options or want to use it at an apartment. Wired is the better choice for homes, since it makes 24/7 recording possible for more security.

You can also check out Best Buy’s other offers on Google’s smart home products. It might be worth bookmarking as deals appear during Black Friday.

#10348. How Product Teams Can Use AI User Research to Test Assumptions Before Sprint Planning

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint planning is on Thursday, and the top of the backlog rests on three things nobody has checked. Users want the feature, they will find it, and they will understand what it does when they do. Each belief is plausible, and each has already been written into a ticket.

AI user research uses language models to simulate or analyze user conversations, so a team can put a question to a modeled audience in minutes instead of recruiting people for weeks. Used well, it gives a product team a cheap first check on backlog assumptions. Used badly, it produces confident-sounding agreement. This guide covers a two-day routine that gets the benefit and avoids the trap.

An assumption is worth testing when the plan depends on it and a wrong answer would change the ticket. "Users like clean interfaces" fails that test. "Analysts re-apply the same filters every session" passes, because if it is false the saved-filters feature shrinks or disappears.

The timing matters because planning leaves little room for research. The Scrum Guide says Sprint Planning is timeboxed to a maximum of eight hours for a one-month Sprint, and the first question the team answers is why the Sprint is valuable. Checks run before that meeting inform the answer. Checks run during it eat the time meant for deciding.

Write each risky item as a short card that names the belief, the consequence of being wrong, and the decision it feeds. A card forces the team to say what would change its mind, which most tickets never do. Here is an example:

Aim for three to five cards. A team that tries to test the whole backlog will test none of it, and the cards on the riskiest items give the best return.

AI user research comes in two forms. The first analyzes data you already hold: clustering support tickets and summarizing interview transcripts. It works on real evidence, so its main risk is a sloppy summary.

The second form interviews simulated people. AI user research of this kind builds a panel of modeled personas matched to your target profile, asks them your questions, and reports the themes. It needs no recruiting and returns results quickly, which fits a two-day window. Its output comes from a model of users and not from users, and that distinction should appear in every planning document that cites it.

A simulated audience helps most when you want to sharpen a question, and least when you need proof. Nielsen Norman Group's 2024 review of synthetic users found them useful for generating hypotheses and preparing interview guides. It warned against using them for final decisions or concept validation, and for niche populations, because AI tends to please and "every idea is often seen as a good one."

Spend Tuesday collecting answers and Wednesday reading them skeptically. On Tuesday, put each card's question to the modeled audience in neutral wording, and ask what would make them not use the feature. A question phrased as "Would saved filters help you?" invites a yes, while "Walk me through setting up your weekly report" does not.

On Wednesday, compare the answers with what you already know from support tickets and analytics. Mark each assumption as supported, contradicted, or unclear. Treat a unanimous answer with suspicion, because real audiences rarely agree on anything. Also be wary of lists in which the modeled users "seem to care about everything," which is how Nielsen Norman Group describes their weak prioritization.

Whatever stays unclear or high risk goes to real people. Jakob Nielsen's classic analysis found that five users find about 85% of usability problems, assuming comparable users, and he recommended several small rounds over one large one. An AI check is a good way to decide what those five people should be asked.

Each mark maps to a different decision. A contradicted assumption means the ticket is cut or reduced to its smallest useful version. A supported assumption that rests only on simulated evidence is not yet safe, so schedule a real-user check as the first task in the sprint, ahead of the build work that depends on it. An unclear assumption should not receive full scope.

The cost of skipping this step is real. A 2019 analysis of 615 product subscriptions by a product analytics vendor found that about 12% of features generate 80% of daily usage volume, and 80% of features are rarely or never used. That is one vendor's customer base, and the figures cover features that customers chose to track, so treat them as a rough guide. They still suggest that most of a backlog is a bet.

AI user research cannot replace talking to real users. Nielsen Norman Group found that synthetic users gave overly optimistic accounts of their own behavior, such as claiming to finish every course in a study, and that they cannot supply real past experience. It also called presenting synthetic findings as real-user findings "an unethical way to share what you've learned."

Two habits keep a team honest. Label every piece of evidence in the planning document as modeled, observed, or reported, and require that any feature built on modeled evidence has a real-user check scheduled. The review also warned that teams starting with synthetic research may get comfortable and skip the real kind, so put the real check on the calendar before the first AI result arrives.

Can AI user research replace interviews with real users? No. It is a cheap first pass that helps you write better questions and spot weak assumptions. Anything that drives a significant build still needs real people.

How many assumptions should a team test before planning? Three to five. Pick the ones where a wrong answer would cut or reshape a ticket.

How do I stop simulated users from just agreeing with me? Use neutral, open questions, ask what would make them avoid the feature, and treat unanimous answers as a warning sign.

What should I do with results I cannot verify? Mark them as modeled, give them less weight than observed evidence, and schedule a real-user check before committing full scope.

Sources: Scrum Guide, Sprint Planning; Jakob Nielsen, "Why You Only Need to Test with 5 Users" (March 18, 2000); Nielsen Norman Group, "Synthetic Users: If, When, and How to Use AI-Generated 'Research'" (June 21, 2024); 2019 Feature Adoption Report (615 product subscriptions, vendor-reported).

#10345. Meet the 20 first-place apps from RevenueCat’s Shipaton 2026

A cozy habit tracker, a smarter snippet library, and a rooster that wants you out of bed: this year’s Shipaton winners offer plenty of new apps to explore.

RevenueCat’s annual app-building competition drew 3,985 project submissions in 2026, according to its winner announcement. Across the 20 first-place categories, developers tackled everyday frustrations, built inventive games, and found new ways to connect people.

Here’s a look at every first-place winner:

Read RevenueCat’s full winner announcement

Keeping a routine is easier when there’s something to look forward to. Mio pairs self-care and habit tracking with a pixel companion that grows as you make progress. Check off habits, earn coins, and unlock new companions and collectibles, with friends and groups adding a social reason to keep going.

Mio took Shipaton’s Grand Prize, with RevenueCat highlighting its revenue and growth in a crowded habit-tracking market.

That dog you met on your morning walk can become part of your collection. Boop identifies cats and dogs and turns their photos into stickers for a digital book, giving pet lovers a playful reason to pay attention to the animals around them.

Creator Taiwo Omisore’s “100 Boops” challenge took the app into the real world, where printed pet portraits and conversations with owners helped shape the product. That hands-on approach earned Boop first place in #BuildInPublic.

If you have fond memories of futuristic arcade racers, Nitelore has a familiar appeal: hovering vehicles, neon tracks, and plenty of speed. Chase your own ghost in time trials, take on combat races, or compete online.

Free tracks offer a starting point, while paid unlocks and daily and weekly challenges give players more to explore. RevenueCat’s Best Game judges singled out the art direction, effects, and haptics.

Explaining how you see color can be surprisingly difficult. See as Me: Colorblind makes that conversation visual, using a split-screen camera to compare different types and degrees of color blindness. A shareable link lets someone else see the comparison without installing the app.

Developer Javier Jiménez drew on his own experience with protanopia. That personal perspective helped the app earn the RevenueCat Peace Prize.

Clucky’s rooster is determined to get you out of bed. Turning off an alarm can require solving a problem, moving around, or finding an object, bringing a little friction to the temptation to snooze.

Its personality runs through the entire interface, including a time picker that uses the rooster’s scrolling eyes and yellow beak. That consistent character, animation, and attention to detail earned Clucky the RevenueCat Design Award.

Adversary starts with a familiar challenge: flip cards and find matching pairs. The catch is a hidden Danger card with no matching partner. Reveal it, and an ad becomes part of the board you’re trying to remember.

That idea earned Niv Ben-porath’s game the Catvertising Award. Players can continue with ads after an initial ad-free period, or choose a permanent unlock that removes ads and adds difficulty options.

Put a few phones on the table, and Bubble Games turns their screens into a shared game board. Objects move between devices as friends compete in minigames, including a virtual hot potato.

Built by four software engineering students at Université Laval, the Next Gen Award winner uses the phones people already have to create a shared activity. Its Shipaton submission includes ten minigames. The winner announcement links to the Android release on Google Play.

GRIDLOCK takes a game everyone knows and adds up to four players, online rooms, and eleven special moves. The result offers more ways to disrupt an opponent’s plans than the familiar three-by-three board.

Its HAMM Award win recognizes how the business model fits the game: a shared Credits currency connects rewarded ads, optional packs, and a subscription, while core modes remain free. Players can choose the option that fits how often they play.

Just Add begins with a craving. Type what you feel like eating, describe it aloud, or take a photo, and the app suggests three meals built around it, adding protein, fiber, and healthy fats to round out the plate.

Shopping support, hands-free cooking, and remembered preferences help carry the idea through to dinner. The winner of Abbey’s Kitchen’s nutrition challenge also impressed judges with a dinner-host feature that accounts for other people’s preferences.

The reply you send every week, the PDF you keep hunting for, and the image you paste repeatedly can all live in SnipStash. It organizes reusable text, images, and files across iPhone, iPad, and Mac, with a keyboard, Mac quick palette, hotkeys, and Shortcuts bringing them within reach.

Private iCloud sync connects the library across devices. Its speed and native interface helped it win Christopher Lawley’s productivity challenge.

Knowing what you want to say and saying it aloud are different things. Rehearse – Hard Conversations gives you an AI counterpart to practice with before a difficult workplace discussion.

Choose from 33 prepared scenarios or bring your own situation, then review feedback on clarity, empathy, assertiveness, and structure. A prep card helps you take the useful points into the real conversation. The app won Leadership Heather’s career-coaching challenge for its focused, straightforward approach.

You see a game in a short video, mean to look it up later, and forget its name. Game Magnet lets you share the clip to identify the games and save them to your backlog.

Processing happens in the background, with a notification when the result is ready. Shelves, ratings, and ranked lists help organize what comes next. The app won Mr Lewis Blogs Gaming’s challenge by making discovery and keeping track of games easier.

FeelGood: Movement Menu starts with a short check-in about your energy, available time, and how you want to feel. It then offers a small menu of movement, with an appetizer, main, and optional sides.

The options span yoga, Pilates, strength, walking, stretching, and breathing. Rest is part of the experience, too. Yusra Salam and Ameena Malik’s app won Simone Sharice’s yoga and fitness challenge, with judges praising its calm, inviting design.

BoatBrawl brings arcade boat battles to a group of up to eight players. Steer, fire, and scramble through interactive arenas, with bots and shared-screen play offering more ways to join in.

Rehaan Cubes and Ashwin K M used Kotlin Multiplatform and Compose Multiplatform to bring the game across phones and computers, including iOS, Android, Mac, Windows, and the web. Its smooth browser play helped it take JetBrains’ Ship Kotlin Everywhere award.

Trazo: Golf Ball Tracer adds a ball-flight line to your own golf videos. Select the ball twice to start, and the processing runs on the phone.

Julian Praest’s app won Most Viral App for the way its team introduced the product through organic content. Golf humor and practical advice set up the tracer reveal, making the finished video part of the pitch. For golfers, the appeal is simple: make the shot easier to follow when you share it.

One Piece Card Game collectors know that similar-looking cards can be very different finds. Grandfolio helps identify artwork variants, manage duplicates, and track collection prices and progress.The Best App for Galaxy winner impressed judges with layouts that adapt to folded, tabletop, and open-screen use. There’s an App Store release for Apple users, too, making this a relevant discovery even if your collection starts on an iPhone.

Let’s Get Support by 3Dadz is designed for partners, parents, and friends affected by someone else’s addiction. Check-ins, journaling, boundary-setting tools, and local resources give those supporters a place to focus on their own needs.

Joshua Burgess and Aaryn B. built the app from community conversations in British Columbia, using Replit to turn the idea into a live product. It won Replit’s Idea to Income award. The announcement links to its Android release.

PoquitoTalk: Bocas del Toro turns English into local Spanish voice notes for WhatsApp and helps translate the replies. The recipient doesn’t need to install the app, keeping the conversation in a tool people already use.

Creator Dorien Van den Abbeele drew on years of life in Bocas del Toro. Its Growth Loop Award recognizes extensive growth experiments and an honest account of what the measurements could show. The announcement links to Google Play.

Parky! combines wait times, dining availability, and memories from Disney and Universal visits. Built by former Disney Cast Member Chase Van Derrick, it aims to offer useful help throughout a park day.

Dining alerts can become conversations on the Lock Screen, where you can ask about another time or restaurant. Alongside queue Live Activities and recap messages, those interactive notifications helped Parky! win the Keep Them Coming Back Award.

In Your Own Words offers short, real-world speaking scenarios and feedback on pace, hesitation, and filler words. Speech analysis takes place on the phone, giving users a way to practice the words before they need them.

Natasha Wilson’s app won the Funnel Vision Award for connecting a web quiz, a relevant practice deck, and a web purchase that unlocks access in the app. The introduction to the product begins before the download.

Friendpire turns the idea of a board-game night with friends into a shared hex-tile world. Gather resources, build farms and docks, and grow an empire together. RevenueCat employee Erik MacInnis built the game to make it easier to stay connected with friends who rarely find time to play.

Friendpire won the separate Conflict of Interest Award, which was open to RevenueCat and sponsor employees and judged by Shipaton participants. It is included here as an optional sidebar and is not part of the 20 first-place count above.

From a useful tool for the workday to a game for your next night in, Shipaton’s winners show how much can come from taking an idea all the way to a shipped app. Explore the winners above, or browse RevenueCat’s full announcement for more apps from the competition.

#10343. Anthropic loosens Claude's game cloning ban

The developers must balance Claude's capabilities with the need to avoid 'pirating' games.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The explosion of cloning and reverse-engineering games with AI has forced Anthropic to completely block its AI tools from executing these tasks. However, this has also negatively impacted game developers, who have started complaining that they can no longer use the tool for their own legitimate purposes. The Anthropic team has updated the rule so that Claude will now offer to build an original game in the same genre when asked to copy an existing one, according to an X post by technical staff member Addy Osmani.

"Last week some of you ran into Claude Code pushing back on game projects it should have just built. We'd added a short rule to the Opus 5.5 prompt to steer Claude away from recreating specific existing games, but it was also kicking in when you referenced one just to describe the mechanics you wanted," Osmani wrote. "We've updated that rule in 2.1.317. You can name a game to describe what you want, and Claude should now build an original game in that genre. If you ask for a copy of the original, it'll offer to build something similar instead."

Game decompiling used to be difficult task — often taking teams of dedicated and skilled volunteers months, if not years, to complete. The availability of AI tools has made this process so much faster and easier, although that doesn’t automatically mean that the decompiled version comes out clean. But what’s worse is that some bad actors have been using the technology to reverse engineer game demos from small and indie developers and blackmailing them by threatening to release an AI-completed version of their game — holding the developers’ games hostage and asking for ransom so that their work is not spoiled or destroyed.

Anthropic engineers are now trying to find a balance between preventing its tools from being used to copy games and earning the ire of software giants, publishers, and game developers while also ensuring that its paying customers can continue to use Claude for their own games.

Osmani also said on his post, "We want Claude to be as helpful as possible when you're building something original, but also to protect your work if someone else tries to copy it."

Frontier AI is becoming increasingly powerful, allowing programmers and developers to get things done more quickly and efficiently. But its unprecedented capability is also leading to unintended consequences, such as the ability to create rough clones of expensive software, such as Adobe Creative Suite and Microsoft Office.
