#10340. Evaluating AI agents: moving past the vibe check

Your agent can loop, burn tokens, or give a completely hallucinated answer and still look fine. A vibe check won't catch that. This post is the playbook behind our AI Agent Clinic episode How to Actually Evaluate (Any) AI Agent: Beyond the Vibe Check, where we build an eval pipeline for a LangGraph agent in 60 minutes. Here's what's inside:

When teams start building an AI agent, testing usually looks the same. You wire up a model, hand it a few tools, run three or four prompts in a terminal, and eyeball the output. If the answer looks reasonable, you ship it.

In practice, this breaks down fast. You tweak a system prompt to fix an edge case in one workflow, and suddenly the agent stops calling a required tool in another.

Worse, agents fail differently than standard software. An agent can get stuck in a retry loop, fire off multiple tool calls, yet still return a polite, plausible-sounding final answer.

In production, that hidden failure is costly:

Integrating a reliable evaluation suite into your development lifecycle can seem like extra overhead during initial prototyping, right up until modifying your system prompt causes unexpected breaking changes. The key is recognizing that evaluation is a progressive journey: any repeatable evaluation system (no matter how simple) is better than relying on ad-hoc spot checks or no system at all. You don't need an elaborate automated grading architecture on Day 0. Instead, start with simple, repeatable checks and evolve your evaluation into a more complex, automated framework as your agent's capabilities and production demands grow.

Establishing a repeatable evaluation system is made substantially easier by integrating these decisions into an evaluation framework. Transitioning beyond simple subjective checks relies on two core pillars: human oversight and targeted tools. In this article, we explore how to set up an evaluation framework using the open-source agent-eval toolkit. We'll demonstrate how combining manually reviewed traces with automated verification helps detect silent regressions early in development and scales seamlessly as your agent transitions to production.

Before writing test code, you need a shared vision of what a good outcome actually looks like. Anchor your tests in critical user journeys (CUJs) rather than abstract benchmarks.

A CUJ defines what a user expects the system to achieve, structured around their persona, goals, constraints, and verification conditions:

Every clause in that statement becomes an evaluation point: Did the agent resolve the correct transaction ID? Did it check the ledger before drafting? Did it map internal IDs back to the external nomenclature?

Defining these expectations upfront forces product stakeholders, downstream users, and engineers to agree on defining how “success” is measured.

You do not need six months of production logs to build your initial golden dataset. Instead, break each CUJ down into concrete scenarios (both happy paths and edge cases) and feed them into a user simulator LLM (we like ADK user simulations). Primed with a persona and behavioral constraints (for example, an impatient analyst giving incomplete ticket numbers, or a developer asking a multi-part debugging question), the simulator mirrors the messy phrasing, typos, and multi-turn complexity of real users.

By testing your agent against a synthetic user and realistic scenarios, you can record comprehensive execution traces (including tool logs) prior to user interaction. This enables you to pinpoint and resolve any performance gaps against your release standards. As live traffic begins to flow, you can progressively replace these synthetic setups with real production traces.

A single top-line score will never tell you why an agent failed. To isolate root causes, layer four types of checks: unit tests, deterministic metrics, LLM-as-a-judge rubrics, and integration tests.

Setting up evaluation metrics involves trial and error, and it is tempting to delete a noisy test or swap out sample queries between runs. Every time you add or remove a test, though, you invalidate your baseline: you cannot tell if a score changed because the agent got better or because the test suite moved. Keep your golden dataset frozen while you tune a feature, change one variable at a time, and only retire a test when you are certain the underlying requirement has changed.

LLMs are notoriously inconsistent at grading qualitative 1-to-5 scales; the boundary between a "3" and a "4" is fuzzy and often shifts from run to run.

Whenever possible, turn your judge rubrics into boolean (pass/fail) questions rather than open-ended rating scales. Rather than asking "How helpful was this answer from 1 to 5?", ask "Did the agent verify the account ID before calling the refund tool? (Yes/No)". (We like Adaptive Rubrics)

A response that passes every quality check is still a production failure if it took 40 seconds, made twelve redundant tool calls, and cost sixty cents per query.

Always evaluate the whole system rather than just the final text. Track latency, token consumption (prompt, reasoning, and completion tokens), API cost, and tool-call counts right alongside answer quality.

Switching orchestration frameworks often forces teams to scrap and rewrite their entire testing suite. While most frameworks emit OpenTelemetry traces, they don’t use the same semantic conventions for tool calls, steps, and tokens. Standardizing your traces around open specifications like OpenInference solves this by decoupling evaluation from your orchestration framework. Tools like the agent-eval toolkit support this natively, with minimal setup.

You do not need to build an observability platform from scratch. You can stand up the evaluation pipeline in a couple of hours using open-source utilities like the agent-eval toolkit which pairs OpenTelemetry trace capture with the Agent Evaluation service on the Gemini platform (formerly Vertex AI Evaluation). But building your actual evaluation strategy requires more thought.

Start small and stay close to the raw data:

Why the red pen matters: Manually reading twenty traces feels slow compared to auto-generating a chart, but it saves days of wasted effort. Instead of guessing at hypothetical failure modes and writing generic rubrics that miss the mark, an hour with a red pen shows you how your system actually breaks in reality. Aggregating those notes gives you a high-signal list of the exact deterministic assertions and boolean judge checks you need to build next.

Once you have that initial list of checks, tools like agent-eval toolkit takes over the heavy lifting and standardizes execution. Helping you with things like:

Establish a regular cadence of runs with your eval pipeline:

Once your Day 1 framework is active, optimization becomes a disciplined, mechanical loop:

In a real deployment, reaching production quality often takes 50 to 60 small iterations. You are not rewriting the entire system prompt every morning; you are gradually turning individual knobs. One tweak clarifies a tool parameter description. The next adds a negative constraint. The next adjusts the JSON output schema.

For instance, upon completion of each evaluation run, the agent-eval framework constructs an interactive, all-in-one HTML report. This report aligns pass/fail boolean metrics alongside key operational metrics, including latency, token usage, API expenditure, cache utilization, and detailed tool-call execution paths. Additionally, it incorporates automated Gemini root-cause analyses for failed conversational turns, pinpointing the exact prompt directive or parameter setting that triggered the failure to guide your next adjustment.

That data replaces hunches. A prompt change might feel much smarter in manual spot-checking, only for the test battery to reveal that latency doubled and tool-call hallucinations ticked up by 15%.

The lesson for teams building agents: small, measured changes beat big rewrites every time.

Once you trust the signal coming out of your eval pipeline, turn it into a permanent regression safety net:

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Running three or four prompts in a terminal only shows you the final text response. An agent can loop on retries, hallucinate tool parameters, burn token budgets, or trigger duplicate side effects while still returning a polite answer. Automated trace evaluation checks intermediate tool calls, state transitions, latency percentiles, and API cost alongside answer quality so silent regressions don't ship.

Treat evaluation as a progressive rollout from Day 0 to Day 2 rather than an all-or-nothing jump. On Day 0, break your critical user journeys (CUJs) into a handful of test scenarios and run them through ADK user simulations. Read those first 5 to 20 execution traces with a "red pen" to see where the agent hesitates, picks the wrong tool, or rambles.

On Day 1, turn those red-pen notes into automated checks so you can tune one variable at a time. Once your pass rates stabilize, lock those checks into Day 2 CI/CD pull request gates.

LLM judges don't grade 1-to-5 scales consistently because the boundary between a "3" and a "4" shifts from run to run. With Adaptive Rubrics, you split broad quality goals into strict Yes/No questions like "Did the agent verify the account ID before refunding?" Pair those boolean rubrics with deterministic computation metrics for tool parameters and reference checks, plus trace assertions for latency and token budgets.

Yes, if you decouple your evaluation pipeline from your agent framework by standardizing OpenTelemetry traces on OpenInference semantic conventions. The open-source agent-eval toolkit routes those traces straight to the Agent Evaluation service on the Gemini platform (formerly Vertex AI Evaluation). You don't have to rewrite grading code when switching orchestration frameworks.

Once your Day 2 pipeline exports traces and scorecards to BigQuery or Cloud Storage, you can compare live traffic against your pre-production baseline. When users hit edge cases your initial scenarios missed, ambient quality agents like The Outer Loop help you diagnose those failing production traces. You can then promote those real traces straight into your Day 0 golden dataset so your test suite evolves with live usage.

#10339. AI-Powered Pentesting for DevSecOps: 7 Checks Developers Should Make First

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Security testing used to happen once or twice a year, usually after a release had already shipped. That rhythm does not fit teams that deploy daily. AI-assisted tools now promise testing that runs continuously, chains steps together on its own, and writes up what it found.

For developers, the interesting question is not whether these tools are impressive. It is what has to be true in your pipeline before you let an automated attacker loose on your own systems. This article covers seven checks to make first, each with a pass condition, and one short code example you can run. Some checks, such as written authorization and cost tracking, usually belong to a security lead or manager. For those, the developer's job is to confirm they exist, not to own them.

Testing systems you do not have permission to test is not a gray area. Make sure someone with authority has approved the targets and the time window, and keep that approval with your records. Run the first tests against staging with test data. Production comes later, once you know how the tool behaves and what load it creates.

Pass: The approval is on file, and the SOC knows the window and source IPs.

An automated tester should never decide for itself what it is allowed to touch. Define scope in a file that your pipeline can check: which hosts are in bounds, when testing may run, and whether anything destructive is allowed.

SitePoint's article on security patterns for autonomous agents uses an open-source AI penetration-testing agent as its case study. It covers sandboxed execution, tool permissions scoped to each role, approval gates for high-risk actions, and audit logging. Scope enforcement belongs in that same family of controls. Here is a minimal check that refuses any target outside the allowlist or time window:

Call this before every scan job, and log the result. A tool that is out of bounds should fail loudly instead of quietly continuing.

Pass: An out-of-scope target fails the job, and egress outside the allowlist is blocked.

Findings are only useful if they reach the people who can fix them. Most teams already have a place where security results show up in pull requests or a security tab. SitePoint's walkthrough on automating DevSecOps static analysis with GitHub Actions shows how results written in the SARIF format appear as inline annotations and can block a merge when severity crosses a threshold. If your pentest tool can export SARIF or a similar format, send its findings to the same place.

Pass: The tool exports SARIF or similar, and results show up in PRs or the security tab.

An AI tool can produce a long list of findings. To judge whether they are complete and sensible, you need a reference for what good web security testing covers. The OWASP Web Security Testing Guide is a free, widely used checklist of test categories for web applications. It covers web applications only, so APIs and network services exposed to the internet need their own coverage. Map the tool's findings against it, and note which categories it never touched.

Pass: You know which OWASP WSTG categories you expect the tool to cover.

Vendors describe AI pentesting in similar terms: agents plan attack steps, try them, and verify what works. For a view of how a security vendor explains the approach, CyCognito's overview of AI penetration testing walks through a workflow from scoping to autonomous testing, validation and reporting, and notes that human review stays part of the process. All vendor claims need testing in a demo against your own systems before you rely on them.

Ask the same questions of any tool you evaluate: What does it test beyond known checks? How does it confirm a finding is real? What does it send to external services?

Pass: The vendor has clear answers, including where the target list comes from.

Automated findings need review. A person should confirm that a reported path is exploitable, judge its business impact, and decide the fix order. Make sure this is set up before the first run. Without it, a team can end up with a long backlog of unverified findings and little trust in the tool.

Pass: There is a named reviewer and time set aside each cycle.

Running agents continuously can add up, especially when they call large models. Make sure you can track what each run costs and how many real issues it finds, so you can compare it with other approaches. You also need to be able to rerun the specific test that found a problem after a fix ships and keep the result. A finding is only closed when the retest passes.

Pass: Both are possible before you commit to running the tool continuously.

Does AI pentesting replace manual penetration testing?

Not entirely. Automated testing can run more often and cover more ground, but people are still needed for scoping, complex attack paths and judging impact.

In staging, with test data, inside a written scope and time window.

What should I do with findings from the tool?

Send them to the same place as your other security results, have the named reviewer check them, and retest after fixes.

AI-assisted pentesting can give development teams faster and more frequent security feedback, but only when it runs inside clear limits. Before the first run, check that targets and the window are approved, scope is enforced by your pipeline, findings will land where developers work, you have a baseline to judge the output, you know what the tool tests and sends out, someone owns the review, and you can measure cost and retest. The scope check above is small, but it makes sure the tool stays inside the lines you set.

Elon Musk's venture does appear to be moving much faster than its competitors.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A scant two weeks ago, Elon Musk made yet another bold claim: he was "highly confident" that SpaceX could build 10 GW of data center capacity in 2027 alone. The grandiose statement raised a few eyebrows, but in a detailed write-up, the analysts at Semiaccurate explain how that's actually doable... if the stars align. That +10 GW figure over the existing 3 GW would mean the company had built in as much data center capacity in 1.5 years as Google did in its entire existence, according to the post. The site notes that should this forecast ring true, that would make SpaceX the fastest-building hyperscaler on the planet, far outpacing every other player.

As proof of progress, the analysts point to how quickly the Colossus 1 and Colossus 2 buildouts are progressing, estimated from aerial shots and available information. They note that Colossus 2's power generation capacity went from only 0.4 GW in January to a projected 2.4 GW by the time the year is out, at the rate that the relevant hardware is popping up. The site tallies up 9 GW of potential data center capacity across the sites it's tracking, including Colossus and Starbase facilities.

Notably, the facility includes 89 turbines capable of delivering approximately 2.5 GW, helped along by a total of 1377 Tesla battery Megapacks, all already on the campus. Due to emissions regulations, the facility will only eventually be allowed to operate roughly 1.24 GW of its own turbines.

Even with the 1.2 GW eventually coming in from the grid, there's a 300 MW shortfall to be filled by the batteries, which Semianalysis expects will top up with compute load variation and possibly external power. SpaceX has an agreement with the state of Mississippi that lets it temporarily operate the extra turbines, with a promised phase-out by July 2027.

As of September 25, Elon Musk had stated that Colossus 2 already had 550,000 GPUs working, presumably in the extant Macrohard and Macroharder facility. At the time, SpaceX was reportedly gunning to have its Gun Range and Project SPAM centers ready in short order. Judging by the photos, they appear to be progressing at a rapid pace, as there are visible facilities where there was nothing but loose dirt a few months ago — though nothing says the racks of GPUs are in there yet.

Semianalysis' team remarks that SpaceX's builds have become so fast that they kick off well before it decides how the facility will be powered, and the respective power supplies come in. We've all seen the news about the scarcity of GPUs and RAM, but for a data center, just having enough power is problem enough.

If SpaceX can keep its stream of gas turbines coming along, that's quite the achievement by itself. The analysts think that Tesla may be manufacturing mechanical and electrical equipment in modules, presumably ready to install directly from the factory, or even trucks. Heck, the aforementioned temporary turbines are mounted on truck flatbeds as "mobile" power generation, seemingly in a bid to skirt regulations.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

In the hyperscaler world, speed is the name of the game, particularly in these trying times when there are mounting suspicions that not all players will even make it to the finish line. Compute capacity being built and eventually becoming available is enticing for AI players, yes, but they're willing to pay through the nose for anything they can get their hands on right now, as current demand far outstrips supply.

Having facilities built doesn't mean that they're actually working, however, or that they're functioning at their expected rate. Zoning, permits, grid connections, power generation constraints, water supply, and public opposition are some of the few problems that have plagued builds most everywhere, none of which relate to the existence and installation of computing hardware.

Microsoft's Satya Nadella put it clearly: "You may actually have a bunch of chips sitting in inventory that I can’t plug in. In fact, that is my problem today." That's far from the only report that a significant percentage of AI accelerators are still stuck in warehouses, on hold until they can go fulfill their duties in a server rack somewhere.

Since building a datacenter involves many moving parts, even assuming that Colossus 2 wraps up as quickly as it appears to be, that doesn't necessarily mean that SpaceX will be able to replicate its success across its other sites. There is no luck involved in engineering, yes, but some factors like availability of hardware and materials, changes in legal winds, and public opposition are hard to predict, even for someone with resources as extensive as Elon Musk.

And yet, the folks at Semianalysis note that two data centers at Starbase are undergoing rapid construction, suggesting that SpaceX can indeed replicate Colossus' success. They attribute the speed to SpaceX having most of the infrastructure and power generation hardware effectively in-house, via Tesla's manufacturing ability. If that assessment holds, the firm could be bypassing supplier lead times that other hyperscalers have to contend with.

Even if SpaceX doesn't reach its "10 GW in a year" target, if the Colossus buildouts are anything to go by, the company may well be moving faster than anyone else in this space. Metrics aside, in the AI gold rush, coming first is all that's needed to win.

#10335. Google’s AI search fumbles leave schools in the lurch

Good morning, and happy Ocarina of Time day to all who celebrate. Oh, and speaking of celebrating: congratulations to the real winners of the midterms, YouTube and Twitch streamers.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

It’s hard to overstate how reliant US school districts are on Google. Almost every school board in the country purchases Chromebooks for their students, many of whom are able to take them home and treat them as personal laptops. IT and procurement managers like them because they’re relatively cheap and offer zero-touch enrollment, which lowers upfront and ongoing maintenance costs. Teachers also like them because Google Classroom is relatively intuitive and ChromeOS is optimized for most other web-based education platforms.

But this reliance on Google’s tech stack comes at a cost: school boards are forced to comply with the company’s capricious terms of service, in particular when it comes to the creeping proliferation of AI across its products.

Things came to a head earlier this week when the New York Times reported that changes to the Google Search experience in schools will require opting into Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and, perhaps most importantly, AI Overviews. The move actually comes after Google was pressured to make student AI usage opt-in. Google’s justification: search and AI are now “deeply integrated” in the tech stack, so disabling one means disabling the other.

Google’s alternative to AI Overviews is a new Guided Learning feature, which replaces search’s primary function — solving a problem — with a step-by-step guide that helps a student understand said problem.

Former FTC Chair Lina Khan took to X to decry the move: “Google is using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI,” she said. “This highlights the danger of allowing an unregulated monopolist to control an essential service. Multiple courts have found Google liable for violating antitrust laws, and this latest move shows it remains unchastened. As the public increasingly pushes back against the use of AI in schools, Google is making clear it will punish those who don’t fall in line.”

I have a feeling Google will need to walk back this decision in some form. As 9to5’s Ben Schoon points out, it’s comically easy to disable AI Overviews in the current version of Google Search, and it doesn’t seem to have a detrimental impact on the subsequent results. But Google is likely worried that if it is forced to carve out versions of the search experience for specific user bases, revenue from its burgeoning AI Overviews ad business may be impacted.

Even if Google sticks to its proverbial guns, the move exposes a rift between the public, particularly parents, and the tech giants pushing AI on their users. Furthermore, the AI boom has had a direct impact on educators’ ability to purchase new hardware and upgrade old Chromebook fleets, since component prices have increased by as much as 5x over the past year. Some lower-income districts are even recalling Chromebooks from students, reverting to a shared laptop pool to save money.

For a much less complicated relationship with Google Search, try its Ocarina of Time Easter egg. Search for “Zelda” or “Ocarina of Time,” then tap the Master Sword to travel back to a 1998-style results page, complete with Link’s sound effects. It’s a trip down memory lane to a time when, as even Google acknowledges, things were a whole lot simpler.

Was AI on the ballot? According to Ben Thompson at Stratechery, it was in a big way. Democrats and Republicans both rejected data centers, but Democrats want to actually pass some regulation reining in the hyperscalers’ and frontier labs’ ambitions.

First we were short on compute, but that ramped; now we are short on memory, but that will ramp; we were coming close to being short on power, but that will be rectified. The final constraint is political, and that may be the most imposing constraint of all. Not only is it harder to solve, but it actually interferes with solving the other constraints. Many of the theories about AI assume that it becomes a commodity; I think it’s just as likely we are looking at scarcity for much longer than we expected, which will have ripple effects on everything.

Is it possible to pass broad AI regulation that will slow down an industry that has been allowed to run unchecked since Trump’s reelection in 2024? And is it in the best interest of these companies to support it, even if it means a short-term slowdown at a time when the two biggest AI labs, Anthropic and OpenAI, are about to IPO?

Some fascinating developments in the $6.3 billion Binance hack: turns out it was yet another case of social engineering. A Binance engineer was lured into a fake job interview, before which he downloaded malware that let hackers breach his computer and, eventually, others on the network.

After my discussion with Will last week about the death of browsers, it came as no surprise to learn that Brave is being sued by the New York Times for copyright infringement. Brave is accused of scraping paywalled material from sites like nytimes.com and selling access to it through a search API to AI companies, which use it as training data. Yes, it’s difficult to earn money as a browser maker (see Arc for another compelling example), but this is particularly egregious, even for a company familiar with shady business practices.

You can also support us by becoming a 9to5Google Pro member, starting at just $5/month. You get bonus podcast episodes, access to our members-only Discord server, and lots more.

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Tuesday.

#10334. Marvell to buy Lumentum in $115 billion all-stock deal

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The deal is expected to close in the first half of 2028.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Marvell Technology Inc. has signed a definitive merger agreement to acquire Lumentum for an enterprise value of $115 billion, combining two of the biggest players in the data center optics market into one company. According to a joint press release on November 2nd, Lumentum shareholders will receive 4.5 Marvell shares for each share they own, which the companies put at about $1,132 per share. This figure represents a 23.9% premium over the company's unaffected closing price on October 28, the last trading day before reports of the deal affected prices.

The agreement — which leaves Marvell's current shareholders with about 67% of the combined company and Lumentum's with about 33% — is reportedly the second-largest tech transaction deal ever after the SpaceX-xAI merger, according to Bloomberg. Both boards have approved the deal unanimously, and it is expected to close in the first half of 2028, pending regulatory and shareholder approval.

The acquisition represents another significant expansion of Marvell's optical infrastructure portfolio, following its $10 billion acquisition of Inphi in 2021 and its $3.5 billion upfront purchase of optical interconnect startup Celestial AI, completed in February 2026. All three mergers and acquisitions involved companies handling different parts of one of the most critical AI infrastructures: optical connectivity.

As AI infrastructure continues to scale, optical connectivity is becoming increasingly important for moving vast amounts of data between processors, racks, data centers, and even on the accelerator board itself. With the acquisition of Lumentum, Marvell, which already supplies optical DSPs, silicon photonics, and high-speed SerDes, is positioning itself as one of the industry's major suppliers of optical interconnect technologies, spanning scale-across and scale-out networking to emerging scale-up and scale-in connections.

Modern AI data centers house thousands of interconnected accelerators exchanging enormous volumes of data. As data volume, speed, and travel distance scale, copper interconnects, the conventional medium for inter-rack connections, hit physical limits, becoming impractical at around one to two meters. The industry's solution has been a transition to optics, moving data as light instead of electrical signals.

First, optical transceivers and fiber optics replaced copper for inter-rack connections and beyond. Pluggable transceivers containing a laser chip, digital signal processor (DSP) chips, and several optical components convert the accelerator's electrical signals into light for transmission over fiber, then convert them back into electrical signals at the receiving end.

The industry is also looking to bring optics into the accelerator package itself with co-packaged optics and near-packaged optics. In conventional packages, signals from the logic chip have to travel inches along copper traces across the board to reach the transceiver. Co-packaged optics takes the optical engine out of the pluggable module and places it as a photonic integrated circuit (PIC) chip directly on the switch or accelerator package, shrinking the electrical path.

These shifts to optics have made optical interconnects one of the most critical AI infrastructure markets, driving several multi-billion-dollar deals and major partnerships among industry players across the different technologies and processes involved.

The optical interconnects market encompasses several critical technologies. On the electronics side, semiconductor chips such as high-speed serializer / deserializers (SerDes) and optical DSPs prepare and process electrical data signals. Electronics also include analog components such as drivers and transimpedance amplifiers (TIAs) that interface with the optical hardware. Photonic integrated circuits (PICs) use microscopic optical components such as waveguides, modulators, and photodetectors to manipulate light, encode information onto it, and convert received light back into electrical signals, combining all of this into a single chip.

Whether an optical engine sits inside a pluggable transceiver or directly on the board, it still needs a laser, either integrated into the assembly or supplied externally, which brings us to another specialized branch of semiconductor manufacturing: laser technology. Lastly, fiber optic cables and associated passive optical components transmit and distribute light while optical circuit switches redirect optical connections through a network.

Marvell already covers most of the electrical and electronics side, with a portfolio that includes custom AI accelerators, Ethernet switches, high-speed SerDes interfaces, DSPs, optical drivers, TIAs, and silicon photonics. Lumentum, on the other hand, supplies many optical components, including high-performance lasers, modulators, photodetectors, optical transceivers, and optical circuit switches. Combining these portfolios and processes allows Marvell to develop more complete solutions, designing and optimizing electrical and optical components as part of the same system.

Perhaps the most strategically important part of Lumentum's business is its expertise in compound semiconductors, particularly indium phosphide (InP), which is critical for laser and other optical chips. Unlike conventional silicon, which has an indirect bandgap and is inefficient at generating light, InP-based semiconductor structures can efficiently emit and detect light, making the material well suited for manufacturing high-performance lasers and other optical components used in high-speed communication systems.

Lumentum is also bringing its manufacturing experience to the merger. Unlike Marvell's predominantly fabless semiconductor business, Lumentum directly controls its front-end semiconductor fabrication processes, owning and operating specialized InP fabrication facilities, which its CEO Michael Hurlston described as the world's largest InP manufacturing footprint during the acquisition announcement. If the deal closes in 2028, Marvell will transition into a hybrid model, inheriting Lumentum's internal factories while keeping its chip side outsourced. The combined company would also gain access to gallium arsenide (GaAs) technologies.

Over the years, Marvell — already one of the biggest custom ASIC suppliers, with clients such as Google, AWS, Meta, and Microsoft — has also strategically positioned itself as a major player in the optical interconnects market, supplying the largest optical transceiver manufacturers. In March 2026, Nvidia invested $2 billion directly in Marvell in a partnership that brings Marvell’s custom XPUs and scale-up networking into Nvidia’s NVLink Fusion ecosystem.

Marvell's biggest optics competitor, Broadcom, also supplies custom AI accelerators, networking silicon, and optical connectivity components, offering both the electronic chips and the optical components. Acquiring Lumentum would give Marvell a more comprehensive portfolio similar to Broadcom's. Nvidia’s separate $2 billion stake in Lumentum would also convert into Marvell shares, consolidating its two $2 billion bets into a single holding.

A key question is how vertical integration affects existing customers, as Marvell supplies optical module manufacturers, while Lumentum sells optical components to companies that also compete with its transceiver business. Merging operations could create concerns among customers that depend on the companies for critical components. Marvell CEO Matt Murphy addressed this issue during the company's November 2 investor conference call, stating that the company intends to maintain its existing component supply model. Lumentum CEO Michael Hurlston also emphasized that supplying competitors is already an established part of the optical industry's business model.

Financially, Marvell expects the combined company’s total addressable market to reach about $550 billion by 2030 — roughly $60 billion more than the two companies’ standalone estimates combined. The companies also expect more than $1 billion in annual run-rate cost synergies, primarily from cost of goods sold. Combining Marvell’s current-quarter guidance with Lumentum’s latest results, the companies put the merged business at about $17.7 billion in annualized revenue.

According to Murphy, pairing Lumentum’s next-quarter guidance with analyst consensus for Marvell’s next quarter lifts that figure to more than $21 billion. Lumentum's recent performance shows the growing demand behind these projections. The company reported $1.26 billion in revenue for its first fiscal quarter of 2027, a 136.5% year-over-year increase. It also announced a second major near-packaged and co-packaged optics design win, which Hurlston said he expects to generate more than $5 billion in revenue.

However, the merger's huge valuation raises questions about whether future growth will justify the acquisition price. Investors reacted negatively to the announcement, with Marvell shares closing down 12.7% at $171.41 on November 2 and Lumentum falling 18.6% to $684.40. For now, the agreement is subject to a lengthy review, with an outside date of November 1, 2027, and options to extend to February 1, May 1, and finally August 1, 2028, if regulatory approvals are still pending. If the deal then fails on regulatory grounds alone, Marvell owes Lumentum $3.2 billion. However, if either board backed out or took a rival offer, Marvell would pay $5.8 billion, and Lumentum $2.9 billion.

#10332. Motorola Signature 27 goes official with true flagship specs and a $1,599 price tag

Motorola is officially back in the flagship space, with its new Signature 27 phone available for pre-order starting today, but it’ll cost a pretty penny.

Motorola Signature 27 is, in every sense of the word, a true and proper flagship smartphone, with specs that are more or less unmatched in the US and hold up even in international markets.

The full specs on Motorola Signature 27 include:

Add it all up, this is a true flagship like we’ve never really seen from Motorola, especially in the US.

Motorola also touts that Signature 27 is compatible with GrapheneOS, has an “ArcticMesh” vapor chamber cooling system with diamond-infused thermal gel, and reiterates just how bright that display is. The Tandem OLED technology in use is also mentioned to be 34% more energy efficient and have a 121% longer lifespan compared to traditional OLED panels. The speakers are also tuned by Bang & Olufsen, a name we’ve not seen in the Android space since LG was still in the game.

On the software side, Android 17 is available out of the box with the promise of 7 years of OS upgrades and security updates – no promises on how often those will be delivered, though. Motorola is also the first brand outside of Pixel to fully embrace Gemini Intelligence, with Gboard Rambler, “Create My Widget,” app automation, and Sign-To-Text ASL translation all supported on Signature 27.

There appears to be no Qi2 support either, sadly, even though Motorola supports the tech elsewhere in its lineup.

The cameras are not holding back either. The 50MP main shooter uses a Sony Lytia L910 sensor with LOFIC for improved color, brightness, and video, with two other 50MP sensors for the ultrawide and front-facing cameras. There’s also a 200MP periscope telephoto camera that’s only 3x optical quality, but goes all the way to 100x hybrid zoom. Motorola has also equipped the device with a dedicated EVS action sensor that is specifically used to improve clarity and sharpness on fast-moving objects while using an “Action Shot” mode.

Truly, a full flagship across the board, but oh boy it is not cheap.

Motorola Signature 27 is launching in the US for $1,599 on November 19, with pre-orders open today. That’s for the 512GB/12GB variant that’s also the only one coming to the US, with colors including “Coal Smoke” and “Capulet Olive.” There’s no availability coming to Canada at this time, but US buyers can get a free Moto Watch Ultra (the one with Wear OS) with their pre-order from Motorola.com for a “limited time.” That same offer also includes a free year of the “motocare” extended warranty. There’s no word of carrier launches yet, but Motorola says US units are “universally unlocked.”

Once again, the root cause is a lack of essential cybersecurity practices, rather than super-intelligent AI bots.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

European fridges are so hot right now. Right on the heels of repeated cyberattacks on supermarket freezers, a fresh wave of attacks has hit units used by France's blood service (EFS). Thankfully, no biomaterials were lost, as the attack triggered the EFS' alerts, prompting staff to move the affected goods to proper storage.

The French Health Ministry said that the situation is being closely monitored and that no assets were lost. The attack seemed focused on refrigeration units at three sites, with Burgundy-Franche-Comté reportedly among them. The EFS' communiqué says the intrusion was on a service provider, which some outlets are reporting as Coolworld.

Coolworld has posted a notice stating that the incident was due to "remote monitoring software" used in its systems, pinning the cause on "a component from a third-party supplier." There are no technical details available yet, so it's not yet clear if the hackers found internet-reachable equipment, as they did with the supermarket attacks.

It's possible that Coolworld keeps the controllers inside its own network and that malfeasants had to first gain entry to said network. However, it's worth noting that it was the EFS' monitoring and alarm system that noticed something was up — not the vendor's.

We don't know which brand of refrigerator controllers Coolworld uses, but the ongoing attacks seem to be happening on a per-vendor basis. Danfoss is the most-mentioned name — particularly, but not only, in the recent Nov. 1 - 2 attacks. Copeland/Dixell controllers were named in the Oct. 13 - 14 attacks.

A Danfoss datasheet recommends that its controllers are installed with "appropriately configured firewalls and secure LAN," and not "on any public or non secure network." That's not saying much, as punching holes in a firewall is nearly the same as having the unit directly reachable. The firm did issue a recent support bulletin clearly stating that the units should be installed with a VPN, but that document only appeared in October 2026. We couldn't find previous mention of this advice in our searches.

The manuals for the Copeland/Dixell controller units that were hit in earlier attacks advise installers to map ports 80 (web) and 22 (Secure Shell) to the internet so that the units can be reachable from the outside, suggesting a VPN only as an option. The firm has since published a security advisory recommending that the units only be reachable from inside a VPN. This was published Oct. 14, after the first wave of attacks.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Many news outlets claim to have seen materials — namely, in now-deleted Telegram posts — in which Russia-aligned hacking groups Noname057(16) and Z-Pentest are claiming responsibility for the attacks. Z-Pentest reportedly posted 2,500 lines of Danfoss code, but none of these alleged materials have been published. The purported hackers claim the attacks are about highlighting bad cybersecurity practices.

It's tempting to once again bring up AI agents as vehicles of cyber-destruction, but the reality is that, just like what happened with PHP, most of the time all bots are actually doing is revealing shoddy security practices and/or copious amounts of technological debt.

#10329. Retrying a send is harder than sending

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every integration that moves something out of your system has the same weak point, and it is

not the happy path. It is the second attempt.

Your code posts a document to a provider. The connection drops before the response arrives. You now hold no job id, no confirmation and no idea whether anything left the building. The timeout told you about your network. It told you nothing about theirs.

So you retry, and you either send the thing twice or you never send it at all. Pick one, because

without help from the API you cannot have both.

The first instinct is a client-side guard. Hash the payload, keep a table of what you have already

That works until the duplicate attempt comes from a different process, a different container, or a

queue worker that started after a deploy. Your table is local state defending against a distributed

The second instinct is to ask the provider whether the job exists. That means listing recent jobs

and matching on something, usually the recipient and a timestamp window. It is a search, and

searches have false positives. Two legitimate sends to the same recipient a minute apart look exactly like one send and one retry.

The third instinct is to make the operation naturally idempotent. That works beautifully for a database upsert and fails completely for anything that leaves your control. A document that has been transmitted cannot be un-transmitted by a second call.

An idempotency key, generated by the caller, attached to the request, and honoured by the

The contract is simple. The caller invents a unique string per logical operation. Every attempt

carries the same string. The first request to arrive does the work and records the result against that key. Every later request carrying that string gets the recorded result back instead of doing the work again.

The important part is where the state lives. It is on the server, which is the only place that knows

whether the job exists. Your retry loop stops needing to be clever. It just needs to reuse the key.

Stripe popularised this pattern and the reason it spread is that the alternative is unbounded.

Without it, every caller writes their own deduplication, badly, and the failure only shows up under load.

Document and messaging APIs are where it matters most, because the side effect is visible to

someone outside your company. Fax for Agents is a current example of the pattern taken

seriously. It requires an Idempotency-Key on every mutating call, so a retry hands back the

same job instead of creating a second one.

Idempotency solves the duplicate. It does not solve the other half, which is that you still do not know what happened.

Most integrations model this as a boolean. Sent, or not sent. That is wrong, and you discover it

the first time a transmission half completes.

A useful state machine for a send has more room in it. Fax for Agents publishes six states:

queued, active, partial, failed, unknown and delivered. Two of those are the interesting ones.

unknown means the provider has not said. Not failed, not delivered. The correct response to

unknown is to wait. A retry loop that treats unknown as failure needs rewriting, because that is

how you produce duplicates even with idempotency in place.

partial means some of it arrived. Resending the whole document would deliver the first pages

twice, so partial is a state that must never be retransmitted automatically. It needs a person, or a

And delivered should be the narrowest word in the vocabulary. In that API it is written only when

the provider's evidence for the attempt supports it, never when the request was merely

accepted. The gap between "we accepted your job" and "it arrived" is where most false

Four questions, answerable from the documentation before you write a line of code.

Does it accept a caller-supplied idempotency string on every mutating call, and for how long

How many terminal states does a job have? If the answer is two, the API is hiding something

Is there a state meaning "we do not know yet", and does the documentation say what you should do when you see it?

When a webhook arrives, can you verify it came from them? Signed delivery with an event id is

the difference between a webhook handler and an open endpoint.

None of that is exotic. It is the difference between an integration that degrades and one that

Starting with the Moto Buds Ultra 27, which Motorola calls its “most feature-packed pair of earbuds yet.” These sport Bose-branded ANC and SoundDesign tuning, plus six high-SNR microphones with two dedicated Voice Pickup Units in what’s now become a conventional wireless earbud design with tips. The buds deliver around 8 hours of continuous playback on a single charge, and the included case adds another 37 hours of use. The square design features a rotating crown and a customizable physical button below the crown, IP68 and 5ATM protection, Bluetooth connection, built-in Motorola Qira support, all in an aluminum frame. A 1.85-inch gOLED display sports ultra-thin 2mm bezels and Gorilla Glass 3 on top. The display supports always-on visibility, and the built-in Qira assistant gives you hands-free command of the watch’s abilities.

As for health metrics, this one supports SPO2 monitoring, stress insights, heart rate powered by Polar, menstrual cycle tracking, auto activity detection, step and distance counter, over 100 tracked workouts, and dual-frequency GPS for accurate tracking. The Polar engine also supports holistic tracking, including sleep, calories, inactivity, and active workouts. Moto Watch Fit 27 launches sometime in the future in three colorways: Pantone Stratified Sea, Pantone Pigeon, and Pantone Ballerina.

An explosion and fire at a PCB factory situated in Taiwan’s Silicon Valley have left two people dead and five injured, reports Reuters. The tragic incident took place at a Panasonic Industrial Devices Materials-owned facility on Wednesday. Panasonic is investigating the details and the cause of the accident. Initial reports suggested that five were injured in the post-explosion fires in the Hsinchu factory. However, an update today confirmed that two missing people had been found dead.

Local media have more details about this sizable accident. Focus Taiwan reveals that the fire broke out at 2 am local time on Wednesday. The five-story building where PCBs are produced had 20+ staff on the production lines at the time of the accident.

Five injured men were conscious and taken to hospital shortly after the incident occurred. However, as mentioned above, two were missing. 12 vehicles and 23 firefighters were dispatched to dig through factory debris using heavy machinery. Only then were the two missing employees found dead.

Panasonic’s explosion and fire-damaged facility “conducts research and development and manufactures printed circuit boards and semiconductor packaging materials,” notes Focus Taiwan. It had passed a safety inspection as recently as August. The news follows a Deadly Intel Fab 28 explosion in Israel, which killed two people last month.

Initial reports pointed to the use of flammable chemical solvents, including methanol and methyl ethyl ketone (MEK), in the plant as a probable accident vector. However, the precise cause of Wednesday’s incident is still being investigated.

While our thoughts are with the dead and injured, and their families, some will be wondering about the potential implications for the AI industry-induced PCB shortage that recently hit the headlines. We aren’t sure about how important this particular factory is to the wider electronics components supply chain. Moreover, no reports discuss how long the facility may be out of action in the wake of this tragic accident.

Give your wireless network a quick upgrade

There's a budget-friendly TP-Link router on sale and it's back down to the lowest ever price we saw it at over Amazon's sales event last month. Right now, the TP-Link Archer BE3600 wireless router is down to $79.98, a 33% saving compared to its list price.

You won't be surprised to find that this is one of the best Wi-Fi routers you can pick up on a budget, but that's before this discount. You'll see in our TP-Link Archer BE3600 review why this is such a good deal, as this router adds Wi-Fi 7 support to your network cheaply and easily, albeit with a few caveats. Those aside, you're still getting strong performance for 2.4 and 5 GHz with this router.

This TP-Link Archer BE3600 router is one of the best-value routers you can buy right now, offering some Wi-Fi 7 support and 2.5G LAN accessibility.

The BE3600 is not a flashy gaming router, but a no-nonsense option with a standard design that looks very much how you'd expect. It's a flat, rectangular box with four antennas that help deliver good wireless coverage. It has one 2.5 Gbps WAN port to hook it up to your internet connection, as well as an extra 2.5 Gbps and three additional 1 Gbps ports for your home devices.

You've also got one USB 3.0 port, which you can use to connect a printer or external hard drive to use across your network. As far as configuration is concerned, TP-Link's setup is easy to complete using the router's admin page through your browser.

On paper, this is a Wi-Fi 7 router, but there's a compromise you'll need to be aware of. Unfortunately, it doesn't support the full Wi-Fi 7 spec, as it's missing the 6 GHz band. While that is a downside, you do get excellent performance at 2.4 GHz, which is still the dominant Wi-Fi band and one that'll prove useful for any legacy devices you have, especially at close range.

The price is what sells this router, because you'll struggle to find specs and speeds this cheap from other brands. If you're settling for an ISP-issued router, or you need a secondary repeater to cure some Wi-Fi not-spots, then this TP-Link Archer BE3600 (also known as the BE230) is worth considering for its $79.98 sale price. This limited-time deal might not be around for too long, however, so pick it up while you can.

#10319. How to Build an AI-Assisted Workflow That Does Not Leak

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A generative AI tool can draft a client email, summarise a forty-message thread or refactor a function in seconds. That speed is real, and it explains how fast these tools have become part of the ordinary working day, used to summarise threads, draft replies and reformat data in a single paste. The same ease is the problem. It moves sensitive material into systems that were never scoped to hold it, quietly and at scale.

Here is the uncomfortable part for anyone running a lean team. The risk in that workflow is almost never the model being wrong. It is that nobody decided what should go into the tool in the first place, and nobody is accountable for where the data ends up. AI made the drafting instant. It did nothing on its own to make delegation safe.

This article is about the control that closes that gap. It is not a product and it is not a policy document. It is a person, placed deliberately in the workflow, making the one call that no model can make for you.

Samsung learned this in public. In 2023, engineers in its semiconductor division were given access to ChatGPT to help debug source code. Within weeks, staff had pasted proprietary code and internal meeting notes into the tool, and Samsung banned generative AI across the company soon after. The story ran everywhere, from Bloomberg to The Register. Nothing malfunctioned. People simply moved sensitive material into a system that was never scoped to hold it, and the material was gone.

Two things make this failure mode easy to hit, and both are structural rather than careless.

First, most of these tools run in the browser. The data loss prevention software a security team relies on was built to watch files, endpoints and network egress, and it often cannot see a paste action into a web app at all. Worse, much of this activity happens through personal accounts that sit outside the controls the organisation does have. The leak does not trip an alarm because nothing was downloaded and nothing was emailed. Text was typed into a box.

Second, consumer-grade accounts can retain what you give them. OpenAI's own documentation states that content from consumer ChatGPT may be used to improve its models unless the user turns that setting off. Paste a client list or a contract clause from the wrong account, and that material may now sit inside a training pipeline you do not control and cannot claw back.

For a developer or a founder, the lesson is not "do not use AI". It is that the point of failure sits one layer above the model, in how the work is handed off and who decided what was safe to hand over.

If any of the data moving through that workflow identifies a person, a client, a candidate, an employee or an investor, then GDPR treats the tools and people touching it as processors acting on your behalf. That sounds abstract until you translate it into design constraints, which is what a technical team can actually work with.

Under Article 28 of the GDPR, anyone who processes personal data for you must do so under a binding contract that sets out what is processed, for what purpose and for how long, and everyone handling that data must be under a confidentiality commitment. An AI vendor you have never papered is a processor you have not accounted for. A free account used quietly by one engineer is a sub-processor nobody signed off. The obligation does not disappear because the tool was convenient.

Location is the second constraint, and it is the one most teams miss. A person, contractor or tool processing your data outside the EU means personal data is being transferred outside the EU, which requires a specific safeguard such as the Standard Contractual Clauses on top of the Article 28 contract. Keep the procesing inside the EU and that entire category of transfer risk falls away before you have to manage it. For UK teams the position is the same: the Information Commissioner's Office sets out the identical core terms for a processor contract, documented instructions, confidentiality, security measures, rules on sub-processors, and deletion or return of data at the end of the engagement.

There is now a second regulatory layer as well. The EU AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds obligations on how AI systems are deployed and governed, on top of the data-protection duties GDPR already imposes. The detail is still settling, but the direction is clear: unmanaged, undocumented AI use is getting harder to defend, not easier. None of this is exotic. It is the same governance you would apply to any third party with access to your systems. AI simply introduced a new processor that most workflows never wrote down.

"Human in the loop" usually gets discussed as a quality measure: a person checks the output so errors do not ship. That is true, and it undersells the point badly. The human is also the only part of the workflow that can make a judgment about what should never enter the tool at all.

A model cannot decide that a board deck stays out of a consumer chatbot. A browser extension cannot know that one folder holds client data governed by a contract that forbids sub-processing without written consent. Those are judgment calls, and judgment is precisely the thing you cannot automate. The person doing the delegating is your policy enforcement point, sitting at the exact moment the decision is made. They decide what gets pasted, what gets redacted first, what runs only inside an enterprise account with training switched off, and what a human handles end to end because the risk is simply not worth the minutes saved.

This reframes the division of labour that actually holds up. The AI owns the mechanical layer: summarising a long thread, turning rough notes into a first draft, extracting action items, reformatting data. The human owns the judgment layer: what matters, how it is framed, and, above all, where the data is allowed to go. Remove the human and you have not automated the work. You have removed the only control that was reading the situation and deciding what was safe.

It is worth being specific about what that judgment looks like in practice, because it is a skill and not a job title. Someone exercising it knows which inputs are off limits without having to ask, keeps sensitive material inside controlled systems by default, and treats the question "should this go into the tool at all" as part of the work rather than an afterthought. Whether that person is an engineer, an operations lead or an executive assistant, the control is the same: someone with enough context to know what is sensitive, and enough standing to say no. That habit is what separates a workflow that is genuinely governed from one that merely has a policy document nobody reads.

The practical version, for a founder or a small technical team that wants the speed without the exposure:

Most teams can put the first four in place in about a week. The last two are harder, because they are not configuration changes. They are about whether the workflow still holds when the people in it change, and that is the part most teams discover they got wrong only after someone has already left.

The speed of AI is not in question. What AI cannot supply is the judgment about what should enter it, and the accountability for where the output goes. Treat the human in the loop as a security control, design the workflow around that control, and you keep the nine-second draft without the quiet data leak. Remove the human, and the only thing you have really automated is the leak.

#10317. Three layers, one repo: a tour of google/adk-recipes

A developer building on Google Cloud today can scaffold a long-horizon memory agent from the terminal, install a retail vertical skill into a coding assistant with one line, and deploy a fully-wired production service to Cloud Run, Agent Runtime, Agent Garden, or Gemini Enterprise app — all from the same curated source tree. That tree is google/adk-recipes, the restructured successor to google/adk-samples that now serves Agent Platform from a single source of truth.

ADK recipes are agent-friendly: we've made it easy for your agents to 1) discover relevant recipes for various agent use cases, and 2) install and use them automatically from your favorite harness.

google/adk-recipes splits its content into three top-level folders: core/ for reference patterns curated by the Agents CLI and DevRel teams, plugins/ for installable vertical skills packaged as Plugin Recipes conforming to the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 spec, and contrib/ for end-to-end deployable services across five languages. Every piece has been designed with machine readability as the primary consideration.

What follows is a walkthrough of the repository's shape, three worked examples (one per layer), and a reflection on what the new structure enables.

Three top-level roots, each anchored on one reader action:

Every recipe ships a validated manifest.yaml, an AGENTS.md that tells the coding assistant opening it what the recipe is and how to use it, and (for contrib/ and plugins/) a Dockerfile plus a runnability test. CI enforces the standards on every PR, and a suite of coding-assistant skills in the repo (prepare-python-recipe, generate-manifest, github-pr-review, and others) does most of the preparation, so your agent can scaffold, validate, and submit a recipe end-to-end.

Every AGENTS.md, every manifest.yaml, every SKILL.md is written primarily for the AI coding assistant that will open the repo on your behalf; README.md files are what humans still read. The handbook glossary captures the dual framing: "Recipe — a runnable agent example (or importable agent module) under core/, contrib/, or plugins/, consumed by ADK developers and coding agents alike."

adk-recipes serves as the single source of truth for ADK content across the three Agent Platform surfaces that consume it. Each reads the repository through its own protocol.

Agents CLI reads core/. When a developer runs uvx --from google-agents-cli agents-cli create my-agent, Agents CLI can scaffold on demand a core/ recipe. The CLI, launched earlier this year at Google Cloud Next, exposes the full scaffold → deploy → evaluate → publish lifecycle; the base content it serves comes from our recipes. Horizon, the reference harness we examine below, is an example pattern Agents CLI treats as a production template.

Agent Garden features a curated selection. The developer-facing path into the repository is a pull request to contrib/: a recipe lands in GitHub, becomes part of the public ADK ecosystem, and from that moment is discoverable by other developers and by coding assistants reading the repository. Appearing in the Agent Garden gallery is a separate editorial decision made by the Agent Garden team, which curates a subset of recipes and promotes them to the public catalog on their own cadence. Once an agent is in the gallery, any developer in a Google Cloud project gets a one-click install.

Beyond these primary surfaces, Google Cloud's own infrastructure components appear as building blocks inside the recipes themselves. Horizon uses Agent Platform Sandboxes for per-user sandboxing, Memory Bank for durable memory, and Agent Platform Sessions for session state. The retail product-search Plugin Recipe uses Vector Search 2.0 on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform paired with BigQuery. The retail virtual-tryon Plugin Recipe uses Gemini image models for still composites and Veo 3.1 Reference-to-Video for animated outputs. The recipes do not just live alongside Agent Platform; they document it in use.

For developers working with coding assistants outside Google Cloud's tooling (Claude Code, Antigravity, Cursor, Windsurf, Zed, and others), Plugin Recipes additionally install through the open npx skills CLI maintained by vercel-labs.

Building an agent that can handle a single conversation is a well-understood problem. Building one that holds its own across days of interaction, remembering what the user did yesterday and planning tasks for tomorrow, is a materially different undertaking. The hard parts of such a long-horizon system are the same regardless of what the agent is being built to do: cross-session memory, per-user sandboxing of tool execution, secrets management, guardrails against runaway behaviour, and the orchestration primitives that make multi-step tasks resumable. Horizon, which lives at core/python/long-horizon-harness, is the reference harness we built to collect these primitives in one place.

The shortest path from a fresh clone to a running Horizon instance:

make deploy rolls the backend to Cloud Run through Terraform; make destroy tears it back down. Horizon's evaluation suite lives in tests/eval/evalsets/ as eighteen named behavioural rubrics covering memory recall, injection-based exfiltration attempts, guardrail halting under adversarial input, tool-selection discipline, and a journey-level plot-artifact evaluation. The rubrics are executed with uv run pytest tests/eval.

A first chat turn against the running backend confirms the memory layer is wired correctly:

Horizon is the harness we give coding assistants to study before building something harder. When a developer asks their assistant to build an agent that must run across days of interaction, the right next step is uvx google-agents-cli create with Horizon as the scaffold, and the rest of the harness falls into place.

Horizon encodes a reference pattern. It does not encode a vertical. For that, the second layer of the repository holds Plugin Recipes, each addressing a specific industry domain. The first vertical fully covered in this launch is retail, available under plugins/retail/.

The Plugin Recipe format sits on top of the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 specification published at agent-plugins.org, an open standard for packaging installable AI skills across coding assistants. The specification defines a plugin as a bundle of one or more skills with a top-level plugin.json manifest declaring name, version, license, and ownership. Each skill is a self-contained folder with a SKILL.md conversational installer, a README.md, an EVAL.yaml of quality gates, and the scripts and assets the skill needs to do its work. The retail plugin's layout is:

Two skills ship in this plugin today. The first, retail-product-search, builds an ADK agent with a vector-search retrieval tool backed by Vector Search 2.0 on the Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform and a BigQuery catalog. The skill handles the entire pipeline: ingesting a CSV or JSON catalog into BigQuery, generating multimodal and text embeddings with Vertex AI Embeddings, building the Vector Search collection, scaffolding the ADK agent with a retrieve_docs tool, and deploying to Cloud Run. The agent is deliberately single-agent: one root_agent with one retrieval tool and no coordinator layer. The semantic complexity sits in the retrieval pipeline and the evaluator rubric, not in agent-to-agent communication; adding orchestration layers would obscure the pattern rather than clarify it.

The second skill, retail-virtual-tryon, generates a photorealistic composite of a model wearing or holding a product, with an optional eight-second studio catwalk video on top. It uses gemini-2.5-flash-image by default, falling back to gemini-2.5-pro-image when editorial quality matters more than latency. For video it uses veo-3.1-generate-001 in Reference-to-Video mode, conditioning the generated motion on the still produced by the image pipeline. The skill ships a pre-flight product-cutout classifier and configurable safety levels, operational details that become obvious after the third real production deployment.

Both skills install into any coding assistant that reads skills with a single command:

The --full-depth flag instructs the CLI to walk past its default top-level lookup and discover the nested plugins/retail/skills/ structure. The install lands in ~/.agents/skills// and is symlinked into every detected coding assistant on the local machine:

The skill ecosystem is designed to compose. The retail-product-search SKILL.md instructs the host coding assistant to pull two companion skills from google/skills if they are not already installed, keeping cross-cutting concerns like BigQuery basics and Gemini API usage in one place and reusable by multiple vertical plugins.

Once installed, the coding assistant reads SKILL.md and initiates a Q-MODE interview. For retail-product-search, Quick Start is two questions (GCP project ID and the path to product data, with sensible defaults for both); Full Setup adds two more covering product-fields level and GCP region. By the end of the interview the skill has written a ./design-spec.md reflecting the project's actual shape and executed scripts/setup.py to land the catalog in BigQuery, generate the embeddings, build the Vector Search collection, and run EVAL.yaml against the result. If the rubric and assertion checks fall below the 80 percent passing target, the skill halts and surfaces the specific failures.

An end-to-end setup run on the bundled five-product demo catalog produces:

A live retrieval against the collection the skill has just provisioned returns:

The virtual-tryon skill is faster to demonstrate interactively. A direct call to generate_tryon_image with a user portrait and a product image produces the photorealistic composite shown at the top of this section in roughly ten seconds. The same inputs piped through generate_tryon_video and Veo Reference-to-Video turn that still into an eight-second studio catwalk animation of the composite in motion.

The pipeline is category-agnostic. Swapping the product image for a pair of sunglasses, with product_category='eyewear', invokes the same generate_tryon_image call and produces a composite in the correct category without any further configuration:

Retail is the first vertical fully covered in this format. Additional verticals will land as their own folders under plugins/, following the same anatomy and the same Agent Plugins specification, so developers can expect a consistent install experience across industries.

The contrib/ layer is where ADK recipes become end-to-end deployable services. Twelve Python agents live there today, spanning the kinds of applications ADK is typically used to build in practice: finance (financial advisor, small-business loan processor), evaluation (LLM auditor), document and knowledge pipelines (high-volume document analyzer, multiformat hybrid RAG), content and brand (brand-aligned presentations, brand-search optimization, on-brand genmedia), retail (product search, virtual try-on), and framework examples (agent-skills tutorial, staged UI widgets). Java, Go, TypeScript, and Kotlin each carry smaller sets. Every entry in contrib/ is a worked example of how a core/ pattern or a plugins/ skill becomes a running service.

To make the relationship between a plugin and its deployable counterpart concrete, take contrib/python/retail-product-search as the example. The retail plugin from the previous section walked a developer through a conversational install that provisioned the Vector Search collection, the BigQuery catalog, and the embeddings. The contrib agent is the deployable counterpart: a long-running service that reads from that collection and answers natural-language shopping queries over it. The plugin owns the one-shot setup; the contrib agent owns the service shape. Both are maintained by the same team to stay synchronised, and the agent's README directs new users to run the plugin's setup first:

Once the collection (retail-skill-products-collection) is populated, the agent is cloned out of contrib/python/retail-product-search, pointed at a GCP project, and surfaced on one of five deployment personas controlled by boolean flags in .env.

Once the deployment is live, the agent exposes a hosted shopping-assistant endpoint. The same retrieve_docs tool we called directly from Python now sits behind a chat interface, reachable through the deployed Reasoning Engine or, during development, through the ADK Web UI. The exact same agent code answering the exact same shopping query, run locally for inspection:

The agent graph on the left of the UI confirms the single-agent architecture (product_search_agent → retrieve_docs). The right panel traces each tool invocation. The response grounds in a real catalog row from Vector Search: "Sony Wireless Headphones · Price: $199.99 · Brand: Sony · Rating: 4.5/5 (50 in stock) · Premium noise-cancelling over-ear headphones featuring an impressive 30-hour battery life." No products are hallucinated; the Vector Search collection on top of BigQuery is the source of truth.

That is what the retail plugin gives a team. The plugin encodes the data-pipeline setup once, through a conversational install any coding assistant can execute. The contrib agent is the running service, deployable in five shapes, that any organisation can take as a starting point and swap in its own catalog. The virtual-tryon counterpart in contrib/python/retail-virtual-tryon follows the same pattern: a smaller root agent that orchestrates try_on_product_image and try_on_product_video tools, deployable in the same five shapes, built on the virtual-tryon plugin's one-shot setup.

A reference harness without a vertical is a toy; a vertical skill without a deployable artifact is a slide; a deployable agent without a reference pattern is a prototype no one can repeat. Shipping all three in one repository, under a shared manifest schema and governance pipeline, lets a developer or a coding assistant walk the stack in either direction, with every Agent Platform surface reading from the same source of truth.

The practical result is a lower barrier from "I need an agent that does X" to a running production service: a coding assistant can scaffold a Horizon-shaped agent, install a vertical Plugin Recipe, and reach the deployment transcripts in contrib/ without translating between repositories or reconciling manifest formats. For teams building vertical solutions, Plugin Recipes package domain expertise once and distribute it to every coding assistant on the market with the same install flow and quality gates.

Not an officially supported Google product. Recipes are for demonstration and as starting points, not production use.
