#10294. Intel leaker claims Nova Lake desktop chips have 5.1 GHz clock limit beyond 90°C

Get started with free access to reviews, badges and discussions.

Unlock exclusive tools and insights for enthusiasts who want more.

Access Bench, Roadmaps, deep analysis and other exclusive tools.

Dive into our proprietary testing data and compare hardware with detailed benchmarks.

Go beyond the headlines with expert reporting on the hardware industry.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Dive into our proprietary testing data and compare hardware with detailed benchmarks.

Go beyond the headlines with expert reporting on the hardware industry.

Track upcoming CPUs, GPUs and tech releases before they arrive.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

According to the leaker, the chips will top out at 5.1 GHz if their core temperature isn't kept below 90°C.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

According to new information from reasonably reliable Intel leaker Jaykihn, Intel's upcoming Nova Lake desktop CPUs will have an unusual limiter applied. He claims that the next-gen desktop parts from Intel will "universally limit the maximum core frequency to 5.1 GHz when above 90°C." That doesn't mean the parts will top out at 5.1 GHz, which would be disastrous, but it does mean that you're going to need some darn good cooling to reach high clock rates on Nova Lake desktop CPUs.

Let's be clear about what he's saying. He's not saying that Nova Lake CPUs can't go above 5.1 GHz. He's also not saying that Nova Lake CPUs have their thermal limit at 90°C. While Jaykihn doesn't provide any clock rate information, he does imply that the chips will go above that, possibly quite significantly, as long as the thermals are kept in check, and he does say that the "Tjmax" value of Nova Lake is 100°C. Notably, that's a drop from 105°C in Arrow Lake, although prior-gen Raptor Lake CPUs had the same 100°C peak.

Preliminary. Nova Lake -S desktop processors will universally limit the maximum core frequency to 5.1GHz when above 90C. This is separate behavior from traditional TJMax thermal throttling at 100C. Alike TJMax, this temperature threshold cannot be shifted upwards.November 4, 2026

The mechanism that this most clearly resembles is Intel's own Thermal Velocity Boost. TVB, if you're not familiar, is the feature that allows high-end Intel CPUs to hit their highest clock rates. Put simply, TVB simply says that if the chip isn't overheating, it can go 100-300 MHz higher than its rated Turbo Boost Max speed. It's a little confusing, because these TVB clock rates are often quoted as the "Max Turbo Frequency"; Intel even does this itself. But TVB clocks are not the same as standard turbo clocks, and a lot of users who don't have exotic cooling probably never actually see these speeds in practice since they do require optimal conditions.

Does this mean that the practical limit of Nova Lake desktop CPU clocks is 5.1 GHz? Well, that depends on how hard these parts are to cool. Remember, we're dealing with chips fabricated on a 2nm-class process; in this case, it's reportedly TSMC's N2P for the compute tiles, while the peripheral chiplets are all on Intel's own 18A or 18A-P. This has consequences, as N2P is reportedly much denser than 18A, but it also lacks back-side power delivery, which means that the thermal resistance of the individual dies is considerably lower. Combined with much lower power consumption from a real node step, it may turn out that Nova Lake is actually quite easy to keep cool.

Ultimately, that is going to depend in part on how much Intel decides to push the "fMax", or maximum frequency. Rumors have put AMD's Zen 6 desktop Ryzen processors above 6 GHz for peak clock rates; if that's true, Intel could be looking at as much as a 20% clock rate disadvantage at 5.1 GHz. Obviously, as noted above, the chips are expected to have a higher fMax than that, but it remains to be seen how easily users can keep the parts cool enough to make use of the extra clock rate headroom given this new limit. Both Intel's flagship Core Ultra 9 285K and the Red Team's extant Zen 5-based Ryzen 9 9950X can reach 5.7 GHz before overclocking.

For tuners, a frustrating detail is that this 5.1 GHz hard clock limit apparently "cannot be raised." That means folks doing system tuning on Nova Lake will be strongly encouraged to undervolt to reduce the thermal output of their processors and help them maintain high performance. That's unusual, as historically Intel allows users to adjust the thermal throttle point of their processors when overclocking. That may potentially still be the case on processors unlocked for overclocking, of course; Jaykihn is reporting based on leaks and engineering samples rather than final released products.

Even if Nova Lake doesn't scale clocks much over Arrow Lake, it could still be quite a bit faster than the previous generation. Recall that the new chips will come with new core architectures and new technologies, including APX core extensions and bLLC, Intel's long-awaited response to AMD's 3D V-Cache. Forecasts are indicating that gaming performance could be as much as 33% faster than Arrow Lake, which would be huge. However, this 5.1 GHz limit could be a downer for people who are interested in the rumored 52-core version of Nova Lake. We'll find out how much it matters when the Core Ultra 4000 series launches either very late this year or very early next year, although we wouldn't expect the 52-core flagship or bLLC versions until next summer.

#10289. UPERFECT GR14BU portable monitor review: A 120 Hz 3K OLED touch display at a killer price

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Although it has some flaws, the GR14BU excels in value pricing, features, and performance.

Kickstand and overall design don’t support portrait mode

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

As the portable monitor category matures, a number of new players have entered the market to claim their share of the pie. One company that we haven’t had a chance to sample yet is UPERFECT. But the company is coming out of the gate swinging with the first review sample that it sent our way: the GR14BU.

As its name suggests, the GR14BU is a 14-inch portable monitor, but it doesn’t have a run-of-the-mill 60 Hz 1080p IPS panel like you’ll find on most of the best portable monitors in this segment. Instead, it features an OLED panel with a dense 2880 × 1800 (3K) resolution and a 120 Hz maximum refresh rate. On top of that, the GR14BU also supports touch input in Windows 11.

On paper, it’s a promising product with a street price of under $300. That’s roughly three times the price of a garden-variety 14-inch IPS panels, but is it worth the premium?

At first glance, the GR14BU impresses with its black aluminum frame that wraps around the 14-inch panel. The sleek design is enhanced by the glossy glass, which is typical for OLED panels.

Things take a downward turn from there, however. While the GR14BU features an integrated kickstand, which is much appreciated on a portable monitor, it’s tiny and is mounted directly in the middle of the back panel of the monitor. Because of this position, the GR14BU can only be used in landscape mode. Even if you could somehow prop the monitor up in portrait mode, this would be hindered by the two USB-C ports and HDMI port on the right side of the monitor, and the five OSD buttons on the left side.

Speaking of the OSD buttons, they feel cheap and wobble around like buttons you might find on a cheap plastic stopwatch you’d find at Dollar Tree. They’re shockingly bad for a device with a $399 MSRP. The five buttons are: Power, OSD, Up, Down, and Back.

UPERFECT included a plethora of cables and adapters in the box, which should satisfy all your connectivity needs. Our review unit included two USB-C-to-USB-C cables, one micro-HDMI to HDMI cable, two 180-degree USB-C to USB-C adapters, one 180-degree micro-HDMI to HDMI adapter, and one USB-C wall adapter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

One thing to note as we discuss cables and adapters is that when connecting the GR14BU to my test system using a single USB-C to USB-C cable, the monitor was locked at 20 percent brightness and would not go any higher. However, when using the second USB-C to USB-C adapter connected to the USB-C wall adapter, the GR14BU would automatically boost brightness to its maximum level.

2x USB-C (DisplayPort Alt Mode), 1x micro-HDMI

The OSD on the GR14BU is no-frills, offering just the basic controls that you’d expect to find on a portable monitor. Without even accessing the main OSD, pressing the Up button brings up the volume controls, with a subsequent press of the Up button raising the volume, while pressing the Down button lowers the volume. Likewise, pressing the Down button separately invokes the Brightness menu.

Pressing the dedicated OSD button brings up the full menu, where you can access picture and color settings. Pressing the Up and Down buttons navigates you through the OSD; pressing the OSD button again executes a menu item, while the Back button takes you back one level in the OSD.

The GR14BU features a 14-inch display with a dense-for-its-size 2880 x 1600 resolution, in a 16:10 aspect ratio. That pixel-dense panel is backed by a 120 Hz refresh rate, which is twice that of most portable monitors, along with multi-touch support in Windows 11.

UPERFECT claims that the GR14BU has a maximum brightness of 500 nits, but we measured a maximum of 368.6 nits when plugged in at 100 percent. While well short of the manufacturer’s claim, this is clsoe to peers like the Asus ZenScreen OLED MQ16AH (369.4 nits) and ViewSonic ColorPro VP16-OLED (369.8 nits).

Our instrumented tests also showed similar color performance to the Asus and ViewSonic. The GR14BU measured 132.2 percent coverage of the DCI-P3 color space, while sRGB coverage came in at 186.6 percent.

In real-world scenarios, I found the panel to be sufficiently bright in my well-lit home office, and even enough to overcome most reflections when sitting outside in overcast conditions. Colors looked vibrant, with especially punchy reds and greens while looking at nature footage on YouTube. As with all OLED monitors, black levels were impeccable, which I particularly took note of while watching the latest teaser trailers for Avengers: Doomsday.

For my usual productivity tasks, I was really disappointed that I couldn’t use the GR14BU in portrait mode, which is probably the biggest miss for me. I like having that versatility to switch between portrait and landscape modes at will.

I also tested out the 120 Hz refresh rate at native resolution for gaming. I played Battlefield 6 with the GR14BU hooked up to my desktop computer via HDMI and had no trouble until there was heavy action on the screen. It was then that frame rates momentarily dipped into the 90 fps range, and I noticed a bit of screen tearing. The GR14BU doesn’t support any kind of Adaptive-Sync technology, like FreeSync or G-Sync.

UPERFECT includes a pair of tinny speakers integrated into the GR14BU. There is quite a bit of audio distortion once you get past 75 percent, but if you keep the volume at a moderate level, the quality is passable. There’s no bass, and music sounds flat (I tried various genres, ranging from Jay-Z to Susanne Sundfør to Jai Wolf). You’re better off relying on your laptop’s built-in speakers than relying on the pair included on the GR14BU.

I came in expecting a lot from the UPERFECT GR14BU, given its specs. However, there are plenty of caveats. The GR14BU has a bright and colorful 3K OLED panel, along with a 120 Hz refresh rate. It even includes an integrated kickstand and internal speakers.

However, the kickstand doesn’t allow the monitor to be used in portrait mode, and the 120 Hz maximum refresh rate doesn’t have the backing of Adaptive-Sync. There are also some quality lapses, like the woefully cheap OSD buttons.

However, much of this can be overlooked considering the price. The GR14BU can be had right now for $299 from Amazon, making it an incredible value given the specs. For comparison, we recently reviewed the Asus ZenScreen OLED (MQ16FC), which has a 16.1-inch 1200p OLED non-touch display with a 60 Hz refresh rate. The GR14BU outclasses the MQ16FC in color performance, brightness, and specs while only commanding a $30 premium and including touch. That’s an impressive feat, despite its design and build deficiencies.

Brandon Hill is a senior editor at Tom's Hardware. He has written about PC and Mac tech since the late 1990s with bylines at AnandTech, DailyTech, and Hot Hardware. When he is not consuming copious amounts of tech news, he can be found enjoying the NC mountains or the beach with his wife and two sons.

Nintendo is going to need more games to convince gamers to buy the Switch 2.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nintendo Switch 2 sales have dropped 41% in the second fiscal quarter compared to the same quarter last year, as reported by CNBC and as predicted by Kantan Games CEO Serkan Toto at the beginning of June, selling only 2.67 million units from July to September. Nintendo is still sticking to its forecast of 16.5 million units sold by the end of the fiscal quarter, but is under pressure from investors to release more big hits to sell more Switch 2 units to hit its goal.

In Japan’s second fiscal quarter running from July 1 to September 30, Nintendo increased the price for the Switch 2 in the US, Canada, and Japan after an initial price hike in Japan earlier this year. Serkan Toto, CEO of Kantan Games Inc., which tracks and analyzes the Japanese game industry, expected that the price increase would lead to a decrease in sales. Despite the prediction coming true, Nintendo is assuring its investors that it would hit its forecast of 16.5 million units sold by March 31, 2027, the end of Japan’s current fiscal year.

Toto also stated, “Nintendo’s only real blockbuster this fiscal year is the Ocarina of Time remake. It’s not enough to make investors happy, especially after the device is now as expensive as it is.” The remake, set to release tomorrow, disappointed many fans, with its visuals being uncanny and overly photorealistic, instead of being true to the original art style with updated audiovisuals. The game also seems to have limited offerings to expand on the original Ocarina of Time, even missing the more difficult Master Quest mode, which many veterans were looking forward to. Overall, the remake may not generate as much excitement as Nintendo expects, with fans viewing it as more of a reskin than a release worthy of the hype. This could potentially dampen the Switch 2 sales Nintendo was hoping the remake would drive, but we will have to see how well it performs after its release.

It also doesn’t help that Macronix, one of Nintendo’s ROM chip manufacturers, recently stopped ROM chip manufacturing to focus its capacity on high-demand NOR and NAND flash chips for AI data centers, even banking on the memory price hikes for short-term gain. Currently, Nintendo is pressured to release more big games to make the Switch 2 more attractive, with new Pokémon games planned for 2027 — Who knows, maybe Nintendo might cave in and finally accept Palworld onto its platform.

#10285. How Developers Can Improve Their Learning and Productivity With AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how developers learn, write code, and manage their daily work. Tasks that once required hours of searching, testing, and manual effort can now be completed with help from AI. Developers can use it to understand complex programming concepts, find coding errors, learn new technologies, and complete repetitive tasks.

However, AI is not only useful for writing code faster. It can also help developers improve their technical knowledge, solve problems, and build better learning habits. When used correctly, it can become a useful assistant throughout the software development process.

The key is to understand where AI can help and where human skills are still needed. Here are several practical ways developers can use AI to improve their learning and productivity.

Learning a new programming language or understanding a difficult concept can take time. Developers often need to read long explanations, watch tutorials, and test different examples before they understand how something works.

AI can make this process easier by explaining technical concepts in simple language. Instead of searching through several websites, developers can ask direct questions and receive explanations based on their current knowledge.

For example, a developer learning JavaScript can ask AI to explain promises, callbacks, or asynchronous functions. It can break these topics into smaller parts and provide simple code examples to make them easier to understand.

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain the same concept in different ways. If one explanation is difficult to understand, a different example or comparison may make the idea clearer.

AI explanations are not always accurate, especially when dealing with complex programming concepts or unfamiliar technologies. Developers should verify important information before using it in their projects. Hallucination detection can help identify fabricated facts, incorrect technical explanations, and misleading information in AI-generated responses, making it easier to check the accuracy of what they learn.

This helps developers spend less time struggling with unfamiliar topics and more time building their technical knowledge.

Software development changes regularly. New frameworks, libraries, programming languages, and development methods continue to appear. Developers need to keep learning if they want to stay up to date.

AI can support this process by helping developers create learning plans based on their goals and current skills.

For example, a developer who wants to learn Python can ask AI to create a beginner-friendly learning plan. The plan can include basic syntax, variables, loops, functions, object-oriented programming, and small projects.

AI can also recommend practical exercises for each topic. Instead of only reading about a programming concept, developers can write code and test their understanding.

Here are some ways to use AI when learning new technologies:

However, developers should not depend entirely on AI-generated explanations. They should also read official documentation and test examples themselves. This helps them understand how technologies work in real development environments.

AI can help developers improve their coding skills by providing examples, explaining different solutions, and identifying areas that need improvement.

For example, a developer may write a function that works but contains unnecessary steps. They can ask AI to review the code and suggest ways to make it easier to read, maintain, or understand.

AI can also explain why one solution may be better than another. This allows developers to learn different approaches instead of always following the first solution they find.

Some practical ways to use AI for coding improvement include:

Developers can also ask AI to create small coding challenges based on their skill level. For example, someone learning Python can request exercises about lists, dictionaries, loops, and functions.

The important thing is to understand the code instead of copying it without review. Developers who examine the logic behind each solution can improve their programming knowledge over time.

Developers learn new information from many sources, including online courses, technical blogs, documentation, tutorials, and coding projects. Without a proper system, it can become difficult to find useful information when they need it again.

AI can help developers organize this knowledge and make learning resources easier to review.

For example, developers can use AI to summarize long technical explanations, create revision questions, and organize important programming concepts into smaller topics. They can also turn their learning notes into structured study guides that make it easier to revisit important information.

A well-organized knowledge system can be useful when learning a new framework or preparing for technical interviews. Instead of searching through old browser tabs or reading the same documentation repeatedly, developers can return to their saved explanations and notes.

Here are some useful ways to organize technical knowledge:

Technical documentation can contain detailed explanations that are difficult to remember. Developers can use AI to summarize important sections and turn them into short notes.

For example, when learning a new API, they can save information about its main functions, parameters, common errors, and practical examples.

Reading information does not always mean remembering it. Developers can ask AI to create questions from their notes so they can test their understanding.

For example, after learning about database indexing, they can create questions about how indexes work, when to use them, and how they affect query performance.

Developers should also save important information about their projects. This may include coding decisions, API details, database structures, and solutions to common problems.

Keeping these details organized makes it easier to return to a project after a break or help another developer understand the work.

Developers spend a large part of their working time on tasks that do not always require complex problem-solving. These may include writing basic code structures, creating test cases, preparing documentation, and handling repeated coding patterns.

AI can help reduce this manual effort by generating suggestions and automating parts of these tasks.

For example, when starting a new project, developers often need to create similar code structures, such as basic functions, configuration files, and reusable components. An AI coding assistant can generate an initial version that developers can review and adjust.

Automating these activities gives developers more time to focus on important parts of their projects, such as system design, complex features, and problem-solving.

However, generated code should always be checked before it is added to a project. Developers need to make sure it follows project requirements, coding standards, and security rules.

Finding and fixing bugs is an important part of software development. Even experienced developers can spend hours trying to understand why a program is not working as expected.

AI can help make debugging easier by explaining error messages, identifying possible causes, and suggesting solutions.

For example, when a developer encounters a Python error, they can share the relevant code and error message with an AI assistant. The assistant can explain what the error means and suggest possible ways to fix it.

AI can also help developers review code before submitting it. It may identify common issues such as:

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain why a particular bug occurs instead of only requesting a corrected version of the code.

This helps developers understand the problem and avoid making the same mistake in future projects.

Still, AI suggestions are not always correct. Developers should test proposed fixes, review the surrounding code, and confirm that the solution does not introduce new problems.

Managing several development tasks at once can be difficult. Developers may need to work on new features, fix bugs, review code, attend meetings, and meet project deadlines.

AI can help organize these responsibilities by breaking large tasks into smaller and more manageable steps.

For example, if a developer needs to build a user authentication system, they can ask AI to divide the work into separate tasks. These may include creating registration forms, setting up database tables, implementing login logic, adding password protection, and testing the system.

This makes it easier to understand the work involved and decide what needs attention first.

AI can also help explain technical requirements before development begins. This may reduce confusion and help developers identify missing details early.

However, AI-generated plans should be treated as starting points. Developers still need to consider their actual deadlines, technical limitations, and project requirements before following them.

Although AI offers many benefits, depending on it for every programming task can affect a developer's ability to solve problems independently.

If developers always ask AI to write code, fix errors, and explain every concept, they may miss opportunities to develop their own technical skills.

A 2026 Anthropic study found that participants using AI scored 17 percentage points lower on a programming knowledge quiz than participants who worked without AI in the experiment. This highlights why understanding the work still matters when using coding assistants.

Developers can avoid excessive dependence on AI by following a few simple practices:

When facing a coding problem, spend some time understanding it and trying possible solutions. If you get stuck, use AI to explore the issue rather than asking it to complete everything.

Never add AI-generated code to a project without understanding what it does. Review the logic, check the functions, and test the output.

AI can sometimes provide outdated information, incorrect code, or explanations that do not fit a particular project. Check important details against official documentation and reliable technical resources.

Regular coding practice without assistance helps developers maintain their problem-solving skills. It also shows whether they truly understand a programming concept.

The best approach is to use AI as a helpful assistant while keeping independent thinking and technical judgment at the center of development work.

Using AI occasionally can be helpful, but developers can get more value from it by making it part of a regular learning and working routine.

A simple daily routine can help developers balance their project responsibilities with continuous learning.

For example, developers can follow this approach:

Start with a learning goal: Spend some time understanding a new programming concept, framework, or technology. Use AI to explain difficult topics and provide practical examples.

Plan the day's development tasks: Ask AI to help organize important tasks and break complex work into smaller steps.

Use AI during development: Get help with unfamiliar code, debugging, test cases, and repetitive tasks. Review all suggestions before using them.

Save useful information: Keep important explanations, coding examples, and solutions in an organized knowledge base for future reference.

Review what you learned: At the end of the day, revisit important concepts and test your understanding through small questions or coding exercises.

Developers do not need to spend hours following this routine. Even a short period of focused learning each day can help them build knowledge while managing their regular development work.

AI is becoming a useful part of modern software development. It can help developers understand difficult concepts, learn new technologies, improve code quality, debug problems, and complete repetitive tasks more efficiently.

However, better productivity does not simply mean writing more code in less time. Developers also need to understand what they are building, improve their problem-solving skills, and keep learning as technology changes.

The most effective approach is to use AI for support rather than letting it make every decision. By combining AI assistance with regular practice, independent thinking, and organized learning, developers can improve both their technical skills and everyday productivity.

#10283. Step by Step guide: Build a Voice Agent That Never Goes Silent using ADK

A guide to streaming tools, non-blocking function calls, and response scheduling in the Agent Development Kit (ADK) Gemini Live API Toolkit.

In text chat, a 13-second database query or RAG pipeline shows a loading spinner. In a live voice conversation, 13 seconds of blocking tool execution produces 13 seconds of dead air, causing users to assume the call dropped and hang up.

You cannot always make backend queries sub-second, so the fix must be architectural: keep the agent conversational while slow tools run in the background. Rather than forcing you to wire up custom client-side WebSocket event loops to manage asynchronous tool state, the open-source ADK and gemini-3.8-live let you implement non-blocking voice tools natively in Python. In our benchmark sales-report workflow on gemini-3.8-live, switching from a blocking tool to a streaming tool cut the time to the agent's first words from 14.4 seconds to 1.2 seconds.

This guide covers how to use streaming tools, non-blocking FunctionTool definitions, and response_scheduling to build responsive, natural voice agents.

Watch the 1-minute ADK Live AI Concierge demo video to hear scheduled responses in action:

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Implementing non-blocking tool calls in ADK provides several immediate user experience benefits:

In standard agent development, a tool is defined as an async def function that completes its work and returns a single value:

When passed directly to an Agent in ADK, this function creates a blocking tool call. During a Gemini Live API bidirectional voice session (run_live), the execution loop awaits the tool's return before generating further model turns.

If query_sales_database takes 13 seconds, the agent produces 13 seconds of complete silence. Prompting instructions such as "tell the user you are working on it" cannot resolve this, because the model's turn is blocked waiting for the function response payload.

To keep the conversation fluid, you can choose between two main non-blocking patterns depending on the desired user experience.

When an operation consists of meaningful stages (such as connecting to a database, aggregating records, and formatting results), a streaming tool lets the agent narrate progress as it happens.

In ADK, turning a standard tool into a streaming tool requires changing only return to yield. By defining the function as an AsyncGenerator, ADK automatically marks the tool declaration as NON_BLOCKING and returns an immediate status acknowledgement to the model.

Continuous progress commentary is not always appropriate. If a user asks for a report and immediately pivots to another topic, an agent that continually interrupts with status updates can feel intrusive.

When the wait has no story to narrate, run the task quietly in the background.

An effective way to get an immediate verbal acknowledgment without ongoing progress chatter is an async generator that yields only once, upon completion:

Because this is an async generator, ADK issues an immediate pending response. The agent promptly acknowledges the request ("I'm pulling that report together for you now"), remains silent during computation, and presents the figures once ready or when the user finishes speaking.

If you prefer to keep standard coroutines (async def ... -> dict), wrap the function in a FunctionTool and configure response_scheduling:

Understanding the Trade-Off: When using response_scheduling with a return-once coroutine, the model receives no payload until the entire function completes. As a result, the model typically does not utter an initial verbal acknowledgment unless the user continues speaking. This pattern is ideal when the user immediately continues with other questions and you want the result woven into the dialogue at the next natural pause.

The response_scheduling attribute controls when the model reacts to an incoming tool response. WHEN_IDLE never cuts into a turn in progress: if the model is still speaking or the user is still talking, the result waits until that turn ends, and the model responds at the next natural pause. ADK supports three scheduling behaviors:

In benchmark sessions with gemini-3.8-live:

Execution shape (streaming vs. single-return) and delivery timing (response_scheduling) are independent and can be composed. Setting response_scheduling on a streaming tool stamps every partial update with that behavior.

Furthermore, in ADK 2.8 and newer, streaming tools can dynamically specify scheduling per update by yielding types.FunctionResponse:

The rule of thumb: If the wait has a story, stream it. If the wait is just a wait, run it in the background and let the result land when idle.

Non-blocking tools do not reduce backend execution time: a 13-second query still takes 13 seconds. What changes is the conversational presence of your agent.

A web application that takes ten seconds to load is perceived as slow; a conversational voice agent that goes silent for ten seconds is perceived as broken. By pairing ADK's streaming tools and flexible response scheduling with the real-time capabilities of gemini-3.8-live, you can build voice assistants that remain attentive, natural, and present throughout complex operations.

Try it yourself: the interactive demo runs the same 13-second query through every tool pattern and response_scheduling mode on gemini-3.8-live, so you can hear the difference.

#10280. Phone by Google 246 beta brings gradient icon redesign on Android

Following the Messages update earlier this week, Phone by Google has received its redesigned gradient icon in the beta channel.

Version 246 of Phone by Google — as the app is still officially called to presumably avoid the Pixel overlap that comes with “Google Phone” — is rolling out today to the beta channel.

Chinese AI labs are attempting to export their data since they cannot import Nvidia GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Moonshot AI, the Chinese lab that made Kimi, is reportedly looking towards Europe to gain access to the latest Nvidia Blackwell AI GPUs. According to CNBC, the company is in talks with at least one European data center to lease clusters with 8,000 or more GPUs for inferencing, and that it’s also interested in securing compute capacity for training.

While Chinese companies are banned from acquiring Nvidia Blackwell and other AI GPUs that offer the same or better performance containing U.S. technologies, a loophole has so far allowed them to gain access to compute by renting them from data centers abroad. The U.S. House is attempting to patch it and passed the Remote Access Security Act, but it must still go through the Senate and the White House before it can turn into law. In the meantime, Chinese AI labs are exploiting the loophole and training their models on foreign data centers that have acquired Nvidia chips.

The U.S. and China have been locked in an AI arms race, with the latter’s open-weight models behind frontier U.S. models by just a matter of months. More importantly, these models, like Kimi K3, are significantly easier to run and could even be much cheaper to use. These advancements happened despite Washington’s efforts to make it harder for Chinese AI labs to acquire hardware that uses American technologies through export bans. But even though President Donald Trump eventually made a 180-degree turn and allowed Nvidia H200 chips for export to China, Beijing has taken steps to protect its domestic AI chip industry by blocking H200 imports at the border, meaning these companies must look elsewhere if they want to access the latest, most powerful AI GPUs to train their models.

Moonshot also has a limited window if it wants to secure compute capacity outside of China — Nvidia has reportedly told its data center clients to limit their business and avoid serving more than 49% of their capacity to Chinese outfits. This could be a harbinger of things to come, especially as reports like these could spotlight the bipartisan bill and increase pressure to advance the legislation sooner rather than later.

#10276. India targets creating homegrown GPUs within 2 years and plans to commercialize indigenous SHAKTI and VEGA processors

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

India targets a homegrown GPU within 24 months as it expands its chip industry and AI ambitions

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

India wants to develop its own graphics processing unit (GPU) within the next two years as the country steps up efforts to build a domestic semiconductor industry and reduce its dependence on foreign chip suppliers, reports ETGovernment. The target was outlined by S.Krishnan, secretary of India's Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY), in a November 4 interview. "Under India Semiconductor Mission 2.0, we intend to target the development of an indigenous GPU chip. We should have a GPU chip of our own. This will not happen overnight. It is a project that will require approximately 24 months, and we intend to work towards that objective," Krishnan said.

The proposed GPU is part of a wider plan to establish an Indian hardware and software ecosystem, with Krishnan describing five layers needed to support AI development. These include: energy, accelerator chips, computing infrastructure and servers, AI models and applications. Indian companies such as VVDN, Kaynes and Avalon are already manufacturing servers, while the Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C-DAC) has developed its Rudra server range, whose systems are designed to support AI workloads, after which the government transfers the technology to local manufacturers. Krishnan also sees AI as a potential repeat of the opportunity India's IT industry experienced during the Y2K (the year 2000 bug) transition. "AI could create a similar opportunity, although the nature of the work will be different," he said.

The comparison is big for a country whose IT-enabled service sector employs about 6 million people. A NITI Aayog paper estimates that roughly two million existing jobs could become redundant as AI adoption changes the industry, but around 4 million more jobs could emerge. Krishnan argues that Indian companies could benefit from helping businesses worldwide integrate into their AI operations, just as they benefited from the demand for software services around the turn of the century.

The country has already attracted major interest with ASML, partnering with Tata Electronics to equip India's first commercial semiconductor fabrication facility in Dholera, Gujarat. The planned facility is expected to use 300mm wafers and eventually reach a capacity of 50,000 wafers per month. Intel also announced a strategic partnership with Tata Group covering semiconductor manufacturing and packaging for the Indian market. However, the proposed GPU remains an early-stage target. Krishnan did not identify a manufacturer, chip supplier, or builder for the project. India is also looking to bring its own homegrown SHAKTI and VEGA processors to commercial markets. Both are based on the open-source RISC-V architecture, allowing local developers to build processors without relying on proprietary CPU designs. Notably, these are CPU designs, separate from the GPUs now being targeted by MeitY.

That said, it is not the first time India has attempted to build its own GPU production unit. In 2019, India's IT Madras released a software development kit that allowed developers to begin building applications before the processors were commercialized. The project included several processor classes aimed at different markets, from embedded devices and micro-controllers to workstations and server workloads. The effort has continued since then, with the Aries 3.0 development board, which uses the C-DAC’s VEGA ET301 processor, being introduced in 2024. The 32-bit chip runs up to 100MHz and includes 26KB of SRAM and is intended for relatively simple applications such as sensors, wearables, and Internet of Things devices, rather than desktop PCs or graphics-intensive workloads.

However, there are signs that the SHAKTI and VEGA platforms are nodding beyond development boards and into practical applications. At the September 26 SEMICOM India event, the government highlighted projects using the processors, including an autonomous water-cleaning robot around SHAKTI and a digital telescope using VEGA. Another VEGA-based project was designed to monitor forest environments. The government's Design -linked Incentive (DLI) program is already supporting more of that work. MeiyT says 24 companies received assistance under the scheme, and subsequently raised venture capital or other private investment. Their projects include microcontrollers, drone flight controllers, smart meter chips and CCTV cameras.

India's semiconductor market is currently valued at just about $50billion and is expected to approach 100 billion by 2030, according to a report by the India Electronics and Semiconductor Association (IESA). The government wants local companies to capture more of that growth domestically rather than remain dependent on imported components.

#10273. How Motion Design Improves SaaS Onboarding and Feature Adoption

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most SaaS products don't lose users because the software is bad. They lose users in the first few minutes, before anyone has seen what the product can actually do. Someone signs up, lands on an empty dashboard, feels a flicker of confusion, and quietly leaves. The industry has a name for the gap between signing up and getting value: activation. Closing it is one of the hardest problems in product design.

Motion design is one of the most underused tools for closing that gap. Used well, animation isn't decoration sprinkled on at the end. It's a layer of communication that tells users where to look, confirms that their actions worked, and makes a complex interface feel learnable instead of overwhelming. This article looks at why motion influences behaviour during onboarding, the specific patterns that move the needle on activation and feature adoption, and how to build them responsibly as a developer — with an eye on performance and accessibility throughout.

Motion works in onboarding because it speaks to how human perception actually operates, not because it looks modern. A few principles do most of the heavy lifting.

It directs attention. The eye is drawn to movement before it reads a single word. A gently pulsing button or an element that slides into view tells a new user where to go next without a paragraph of instructions. On a crowded first-run screen, that guidance is the difference between a confident first action and a confused exit.

It preserves context. When a panel expands, a card flips, or a new view slides in from the right, a short transition shows users where things came from and where they went. This spatial continuity keeps people oriented. Interfaces that snap instantly between states force the user to re-scan the screen each time and silently rebuild their mental model.

It confirms actions. Every click, toggle, and form submission is a small question: did that work? A quick visual response — a checkmark drawing itself, a row settling into place — answers it immediately. Without that feedback, users repeat actions, doubt the system, or assume something broke.

It shapes perceived performance. A thoughtful loading animation or skeleton screen makes a wait feel shorter and the product feel responsive, even when the underlying request takes exactly as long. Perception of speed is part of the experience, and motion is how you manage it.

These are the patterns that reliably help new users reach their first win.

Onboarding doesn't end after the first session. Most SaaS products keep shipping features, and each one faces its own adoption problem: users settle into a few familiar workflows and never discover the rest. Motion is how you reintroduce the product to people who already think they know it.

Contextual nudges that appear in motion — a tooltip that eases in near a newly relevant button, a gentle highlight on a feature the moment a user hits the limit it solves — are far more effective than a generic email. They arrive at the point of need and use movement to pull attention without hijacking the screen.

"What's new" reveals can announce an update with a small, well-timed animation rather than a modal that gets dismissed on reflex. A badge that animates in, or a panel that slides open on the first visit to a page, respects the user's flow while still being noticed.

Animated demonstrations carry the most weight for genuinely complex features. Showing a feature in motion — rather than describing it in text — lets users grasp a multi-step workflow in seconds. For intricate capabilities, this is where a short, purpose-made explainer earns its place, a point we return to in the next section.

The right tool depends on how complex the animation is and where it lives. Roughly in order of weight:

CSS transitions and keyframe animations should be your default. They cover the large majority of onboarding motion — hovers, state changes, highlights, progress bars — and the browser can run them efficiently. Reach for JavaScript only when CSS genuinely can't express what you need.

The Web Animations API gives you CSS-level performance with programmatic control, which matters when animations depend on data or user input, or need to be sequenced and interrupted. SitePoint's guide to the Web Animations API is a solid introduction, and the broader web animation archive covers libraries like GSAP for orchestrating complex timelines.

Lottie is the bridge between designer-made animation and the web. Designers export vector animations as JSON, and a lightweight player renders them natively at small file sizes — ideal for polished illustrative moments like success states and animated empty states, without hand-coding every frame.

Pre-rendered video is the right call when a feature is genuinely involved and a looping CSS animation or a tooltip simply can't carry the explanation. A short, well-produced walkthrough can compress a multi-step workflow into something a user absorbs in under a minute, which is why many teams lean on a dedicated SaaS explainer video for their most complex features and onboarding moments. The trade-off is weight and production effort, so reserve video for the handful of places where it genuinely outperforms in-interface motion, lazy-load it, and never autoplay it with sound.

Motion that stutters does more damage than no motion at all, and motion that ignores accessibility can actively harm users. Two disciplines keep animation on the right side of that line.

For performance, animate the properties the browser can handle cheaply: transform and opacity. These can run on the compositor thread without triggering layout or paint, which is what lets animation hold a smooth 60fps. Animating properties like width, height, top, or left forces the browser to recalculate layout on every frame and is the usual cause of janky motion. Keep animations short — most interface transitions should land between 150 and 300 milliseconds — because motion that overstays its welcome makes a product feel slow.

For accessibility, honour the prefers-reduced-motion media query. Some users experience dizziness, nausea, or disorientation from movement, and the operating system lets them ask for less of it. Respecting that setting is straightforward:

Beyond that, never let an animation block interaction. If a user has to wait for a flourish to finish before they can click, the motion is working against them. Animation should support the task and then get out of the way.

Motion design is a product decision, so treat it like one and measure it. The metrics that matter map directly to the problems motion is meant to solve.

Treat animations as hypotheses and A/B test them. Ship the animated onboarding step to half of your new users and measure activation against the control. Motion should earn its place with data, not assumptions — and occasionally the data will tell you an animation is slowing people down, which is just as useful to know.

The best onboarding motion is the kind users never consciously notice. It simply makes the product feel clear, responsive, and easy to learn — guiding attention, confirming actions, and turning a complex interface into something approachable. That's the real payoff: not animation for its own sake, but fewer confused users, faster time to value, and features that actually get discovered.

Start small. Pick the single point in your onboarding where users most often stall, add one purposeful animation that removes the confusion, and measure what happens. Motion, used deliberately, is one of the most direct levers you have on activation and adoption — and like any good tool, it works best when it draws no attention to itself.

#10271. Combined OpenSSH and musl-libc flaws let anyone into Alpine Linux and other distros by typing anything over 256 characters as a password

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Meanwhile, the parties involved are playing a game of hot potato with who's fault.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The internet is still reeling from the PHP engine vulnerability and all other security vulnerabilities found over the past few weeks, and we're not done. Today's special is quite spicy: you can get into Alpine, OpenWRT, and other Linux distros without needing a password, thanks to a combined bug with OpenSSH and musl libc. Just randomly type over 256 characters on the password prompt and you'll be let in through the SSHhole.

It works if you set an empty password for system users, which is the default on multiple distros. The bug is present, though not necessarily enabled, across Buildroot, Yocto, and Void Linux. OpenWRT uses Dropbear for authentication by default, but users can install OpenSSH instead.

The largest concern is by far Alpine, though, as it's pretty much the default choice for what could amount to millions of installs across Docker/Kubernetes/Proxmox containers and endless dependencies thereof, servers, cloud VMs, and embedded devices, among many others. Alpine comes with the requisite conditions set and password authentication enabled by default, which is why it needs immediate patching.

Broadly speaking, the vulnerability combination is: OpenSSH, musl, password authentication not explicitly disabled, not using PAM, and the one arbitrary user with a password set, using a SHA-512/256 hash. Patch your systems immediately, as per the project's advisory note. Meanwhile, the easiest mitigation is explicitly setting "PasswordAuthentication" to "no" in your OpenSSH configuration file and restarting the service.

While in practice the vast majority of Alpine and similar installs aren't reachable from the internet, any attacker inside a network, including but not limited to corporate setups, home labs, cloud networks, and VPNs, can let themselves in. Likewise, all it takes is one valid user account, not too hard to get in a large enough corporate environment, and then an attacker is inside the network. Scanning and testing the vulnerability is also trivial.

OpenSSH has already published a fix and an advisory post, as did musl author Rich Felker. Both parties seem to believe the other is at fault, but looking from the outside, blame is shared.

If you've ever looked in the /etc/passwd or /etc/shadow files, you may have noticed a single asterisk as a placeholder for the password hash for accounts with no password — meaning "no password should work." The SSHhole website has all the details, but the snafu happens both when musl's crypt() function returns "*" as an error condition instead of a null value as per usual conventions, and then OpenSSH uses it to compare to a password hash, when it shouldn't. An overly long input then triggers the chain of mistakes.

#10269. Payday Aces High review: This year’s must-play multiplayer VR game

Join up with three other heisters and get revenge on the billionaire who sent you to jail in the first place in Payday Aces High, now available on Meta Quest, SteamVR, and Steam Frame.

Payday Aces High is the return to the series’ roots I had been hoping for. Payday 3 wasn’t received particularly well, and while the team behind it has turned things around in the past few years, it’s pretty clear that Payday 2 is still everyone’s favorite entry. But that stops for me now as we enter a new chapter in the series, with four new characters, a new “villain,” and made-for-VR mechanics that shine.

I’ve been playing Payday Aces High throughout the alpha and beta stages and got access to the final review build a week ago. Since then, my son and I have robbed banks, stolen from museums, hoisted a fragile quantum computer out of an icy compound, and generally made life a living hell for Warren Jupiter, the man who made billions framing the characters you play as in Payday Aces High.

If you’re familiar with Payday games, this one will mostly feel like a return to the original game with new mechanics in tow. Some of the fan-favorite mechanics from Payday 2 (and even 3) aren’t here — you can’t take hostages, for example — but other mechanics, like gun upgrades and perks, will keep you coming back over and over again.

While you can choose from the four main Aces characters — the Ace of Hearts, Clubs, Diamonds, and Spades — the character itself is just a visual change rather than a stats-based one. You customize your own stats throughout the adventure as you unlock points and earn money. You’ll also collect the infamous masks that each character dons, including DLC sets (and missions) scheduled for the next few months.

Payday Aces High begins with six unlockable missions, while a seventh free one (named Coup de Grace) is landing on December 14. Developer Fast Travel Games is a VR veteran and is hosting community challenges throughout the next few months, unlocking special loot when completed. The game has full crossplay support, so it should be very easy to find and invite friends, no matter which platform they prefer.

I played the standalone Quest 3 version, which looks quite nice visually and sports great performance. VR-specific mechanics are excellent, including on-body inventory, fun skill check-style minigames to keep equipment working, physical interaction with locks and other environmental details, and lots of shooting with physics-based gun mechanics. Hacking a computer while shooting in a completely different direction is something that only makes sense in VR, and I love leaning into those types of mechanics.

The biggest miss of Payday Aces High is not providing enough bespoke VR mechanics. You can’t grab characters, can’t break glass with a melee attack, and the stealth leaves a bit to be desired. I would love to be able to toss an object across the room to distract guards for a moment, for instance, as it would fit the medium very well.

But outside of a few immersion-breaking decisions like this, the action is incredibly fun, challenging, and, maybe most important of all, rewarding. My son and I have completed several missions many times over in hopes of perfecting the formula, and the increasing complexity and difficulty of each has proven to be an enjoyable challenge. Payday Aces High is my multiplayer VR game of the year, hands down, not just because I already loved the series, but because I find co-op multiplayer to be the most fun thing to do in a VR headset.

#10265. Why Your AI Agent Can't Read the Web (and How to Build a Fetch Layer That Can)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

This is the least glamorous part of building with LLMs and it's where a lot of agent projects quietly fall over. The model is rarely the problem. The fetch is. In this article we'll look at why live web retrieval has become so unreliable for AI tools, what the numbers say about the shift, and how to build a small, well-behaved fetch layer in Python that fails loudly instead of feeding your model garbage.

A few years ago, a plain requests.get() worked on most of the public web. That's no longer true, and the reason is simple: there are now more bots than people.

According to the 2025 Imperva Bad Bot Report, automated traffic made up 51% of all web traffic in 2024. That's the first time in a decade it has passed human activity. Bad bots alone (scrapers, credential stuffers, scalpers) went from 32% to 37% of traffic in a single year.

Source: Imperva Bad Bot Report 2024 and 2025

From a site owner's point of view, your polite research agent and a credential-stuffing botnet look much the same at the network edge. Both are non-browser clients making fast requests from data-center IP ranges. So the defaults have tightened. In July 2025 Cloudflare began blocking known AI crawlers by default for new customers, and bot management products now look at TLS fingerprints, header order, IP reputation and JavaScript execution, not just the User-Agent string.

It helps to understand the publisher's side of the deal, because it explains why your agent is being treated with suspicion.

Cloudflare splits AI crawling into three purposes: training (collecting data to train models), search (building an index for AI search answers) and user action (fetching a page because a person just asked a question). Training dominates.

Training crawls take content and send nothing back. Cloudflare measures this with a crawl-to-referral ratio: how many pages a platform crawls for every visitor it sends to the site. In its crawl-to-click gap analysis, the ratios for some AI platforms ran into the tens of thousands, compared with single digits for traditional search.

If you ran a publication and saw those numbers, you'd block too. The catch is that blocking is usually done by user agent or IP range, so it hits user-action fetches as well. Those are the ones that happen when a person asks your agent to read a specific article, and Cloudflare's 2025 Year in Review found they grew more than 15x during the year. Your agent is caught in a fight it didn't start.

The mix of crawlers also shifts quickly, which makes static allow and block lists fragile on both sides:

And restrictions keep piling up. The Consent in Crisis study audited 14,000 web domains and found that a large and fast-growing share of the sources behind the C4 training corpus now restrict AI crawlers, either through robots.txt or their terms of service.

Source: Longpre et al., Consent in Crisis (NeurIPS 2024)

So that's the environment your agent fetches in. Here's how to build for it.

Before writing any retry logic, put names on your failures. "The request failed" is useless to both you and the model. In practice almost every failed fetch falls into one of four buckets:

Bucket 2 is the dangerous one, because a block page often comes back with a 200 OK. If you don't check, the model will happily summarise "Checking your browser before accessing..." as if it were the article.

Here's a compact fetch layer in Python using httpx and the standard library's robots.txt parser. It's around 100 lines and covers the four buckets above.

Use a descriptive user agent with a contact URL. It costs you nothing and site operators are far more likely to allow a client they can identify and reach. Then check robots.txt (standardised as RFC 9309) before every new host.

The lru_cache means you fetch robots.txt once per origin rather than once per page. In a long-running service you'll want a cache with a TTL instead, since robots.txt files do change.

This is the core of the layer. Instead of returning raw HTML, return a result that says which bucket you landed in.

The marker list is deliberately simple. It won't catch every interstitial, but it catches the common ones, and a false "blocked" is much cheaper than a confident summary of a CAPTCHA page. Log every blocked result with its first few hundred characters so you can grow the list from real data.

Retries should depend on the bucket. Throttled and broken responses get exponential backoff with jitter. Blocked and not-allowed responses get no retries at all.

Note the cap on wait. A site sending Retry-After: 3600 is telling you to go away for an hour. An interactive agent should report that to the user rather than block a request thread for that long.

The cheapest fetch is the one you don't make. Agents often re-read the same documentation page several times in one session. Store the ETag and Last-Modified headers and send them back as If-None-Match and If-Modified-Since. A 304 Not Modified response costs the server almost nothing and lowers the odds of being rate limited.

Wire these into fetch() by passing headers=conditional_headers(url) to client.get(). On a 304, return the cached text. On a 200, call remember().

Finally, don't hide failures from the LLM. Give the tool output a structured shape so the model can reason about it:

(extract_main_text is whatever readability or boilerplate-removal step you already use. Libraries like trafilatura work well here.)

With this in place, the model's answer changes from a made-up summary to "I couldn't read that page because the site blocks automated access. Here's what I found from two other sources." That's a better product, and it builds trust with users.

Once you've done everything above, some legitimate user-action fetches will still fail. That's usually because the site blocks data-center IP ranges outright, not because of anything your agent did. Teams handle this in a few ways, roughly in order of preference:

The principle is the same in every case: the network path is a reliability decision, not a way around a site's wishes. Your robots.txt check and your honest user agent should stay in place whichever route the request takes.

Once your fetch layer returns structured results, observability is easy. Emit one event per fetch with the host, status bucket and latency, and watch these:

If you're prototyping agents with AI coding assistants, ask them to generate these metrics hooks from the start. They're easy to bolt on now and painful to add later. For a sense of how well current tools handle this kind of infrastructure code, Vibe Coder Journal publishes hands-on reviews and benchmarks of AI coding tools.

The web your agent reads in 2026 isn't the open, fetch-anything web many of us learned on. More than half the traffic is automated, publishers have good reasons to be wary of AI crawlers, and the defaults now lean toward blocking. You can't change that. You can make your agent a good citizen, though: one that identifies itself, respects the rules, backs off when asked, and tells the truth when it can't read something.

Most of the reliability gains come from the unglamorous parts: classifying failures, caching and passing honest errors to the model. Get those right and your agent will feel much more dependable, even on the days when half the web won't let it in.

It depends on the jurisdiction, the site's terms of service and what you do with the content. Respecting robots.txt and site terms is the baseline. For commercial products, get proper legal advice.

Many site owners expect it to, and RFC 9309 makes no exception for user-triggered fetches. The safest default is to obey it and tell the user when a page is disallowed.

Many bot-protection systems serve a challenge page with a 200 status. Check the body for challenge markers before passing it to the model, as in the looks_blocked() function above.

Only for pages that render their main content with JavaScript. Try a plain HTTP fetch first. Headless browsers are slower, cost more to run and are easier to fingerprint.
